But he did not resolve to attack Tarsos and Mopsuestia, either on account of the winter or because his love for Theophano drew him away toward her, for he departed with her to Cappadocia. And from there, when spring had arrived, he returned again to Cilicia. He himself besieged Mopsuestia, and he set his brother Leo over Tarsos. Since Mopsuestia is divided by the river Saros, with one part in the mid...
dle being besieged, the Hagarenes who were inside it shifted toward the remainder, setting fire to what had been left behind. Then he captured the other part as well, and no one escaped from there. When those in Tarsos learned of the desolation of Mopsuestia, they called upon the Emperor and surrendered the city. Three days passed, and a fleet from Egypt sailed down to bring aid to Tarsos, but it was not permitted to put in at the shore; wherefore it sailed away again, and having encountered violent gusts of wind and Roman triremes, the greater part of it perished. The Emperor returned to Constantinople, bringing with him the gates of Tarsos and Mopsuestia, which were made of bronze and exhibited the precision of high art. Having embellished these further, he fitted some to the wall by the Acropolis and others to the western wall. And, as a kind of first-fruits of his campaign, he dedicated to God the venerable crosses which the Barbarians had taken when the Stypeiotes, being Domestic of the Schools and besieging Tarsos, had met with disaster through imprudence, depositing them in the divine temple of the Sophia of the Word of God. He also restored to the Roman Empire Cyprus, which was held by the Hagarenes. Then he invaded Syria and, after having stormed cities and captured regions lying near Lebanon and the seacoast, he came to Antioch. But because the Antiochenes resisted more fiercely, and with a shortage of supplies occurring, and the ground being flooded by rains, he abandoned the siege and returned to the imperial city, having brought with him the sacred and divine image of the Savior Christ, which he had found in a jar at Hierapolis in Syria, and a lock of the hair of John the Baptist clotted with blood. C In military matters, therefore, such was this Emperor, and he had extended the borders of the Romans for a long distance and spread out the boundaries of their dominion. But for certain reasons, he became hateful to all. These reasons were: that when the soldiers who had been his confederates in his insurrection were plundering everything in their path, he did not concern himself with those who had caused the damages; and, when many homes of illustrious men and commoners had been looted, he acted as if no evil had occurred. Rather, he even delighted in the injuries with which the citizens were burdened, although it was by their aid that he had attained the empire. Furthermore, under the pretext of continual wars, he oppressed his subjects with new taxes, by increasing the old ones, and with exactions of every kind, for the soldiers acted no more mercifully toward those subjects wherever they went than they did toward the enemies. D In addition, on account of frequent expeditions, by alleging a lack of money, he in part curtailed the imperial largesses customarily given to the senate, and completely abolished the annual gifts long since attributed by ancient times to temples and other pious houses. He also issued an imperial decree that the lands of the churches should not be increased, because (as he himself asserted) those riches were not being conferred by the priests for the utility of the churches. Furthermore, a contention having arisen between the patriarch and the bishops concerning suffrages—with the latter claiming for themselves the authority to decide and the power to approve, and the former criticizing the suffrages as being rendered neither without partiality nor with right judgment, and endeavoring that he himself be admitted into the counsel of those who were to make the judgment—the Emperor, having seized this occasion and having claimed for himself all authority for the appointing of bishops, issued an edict that no one should be sent to any church without his order. Therefore, when a bishop died, he would send one of his own ministers into the vacant church, who, by managing the necessary expenses sparingly, would claim the remainder for himself. Nor, indeed, while he was reigning, was there in any of the provinces
did those appointed to record, inspect, and serve as army officers and protonotaries cease their activities; they reduced the subjects, oppressed by every kind of evil, to utter destitution, without even taking the most needy into account. For they transferred those who had previously been in the dromōn armies into naval service; those in the naval forces into the infantry; the infantry into the cavalry squadrons; the cavalry into the cataphracts: assigning to each a more burdensome military duty. For he reduced everything to a single military purpose, and he was known for managing all things in a military fashion. Whence, as far as it lay in his power, he decreed by edict that soldiers killed in wars should be honored equally with the martyrs and should be celebrated with similar hymns and veneration. And had not the patriarch and certain of the leading bishops, together with the princes of the senate, valiantly resisted and asked how those who in wars both kill and are killed could be judged to be martyrs, or equal to the martyrs, whom the censure of the sacred canons [(62)] stigmatizes, barring them from that awesome and holy communion for three years, perhaps that extraordinary decree would have been confirmed.
It is also apparent that he was contaminated by the filth of avarice. For whereas until his time every coin weighed a full weight, he invented the *tetarteron*, having diminished its weight, and performed the exactions with the heavier [coinage], while making distributions and all expenditures with the clipped coin. Now, since it had been accepted from of old that every old *stater* bearing the imperial image was worth the same as one newly struck by the reigning emperor, he ordered his own currency to be held of higher value. To what end? That only it being sought by merchants, he might derive much profit for himself from the premium of the exchange, which he demanded to be large. When the citizens were vexed by these things, the *aediles* of saleable goods took no account of matters, but each merchant set the price at his own discretion. Whence those who were buying necessary goods became poorer day by day.
XVI.
These and many other things (for it would be difficult to recount everything) inflamed everyone with bitter hatred against Nicephorus, so that all prayed to be delivered from his tyranny. This, however, most of all troubled men...
him, in that he enclosed the palace with a wall. For having received an oracle that he was destined to be killed within the palace, he thought to procure safety for himself through the wall, though he ought to have reasoned that if the oracle were true, it would be fated—or, to use such language, ordained by God—and thus inevitable and immutable; but if it could be prevented by some artifice, it was not strictly true, and the artifice was superfluous. But he, considering none of these things, surrounded the palace with the wall that is seen even now, at great expense, which the citizens judged to be an Acropolis and a tyrannical fortress built against them. When, therefore, he had completed the fortification, and thought he had secured his safety, then he learned that his invention had been of no avail to him, since he suffered his destruction within it. But let this be passed over for the present, and let the narrative recount his deeds.
As the emperor was returning from the Antioch on the Orontes, he built a fort on Mount Taurus—which the common people now call the Black Mountain—in which he left Michael Burzes, whom he had honored with the rank of patrician and appointed duke of the Black Mountain, commissioning him to harass the Antiochenes. He also left behind a certain Peter, one of his own attendants—a eunuch, indeed, but a vigorous and steady man, skilled in commanding soldiers—having designated him stratopedarch, that he might provide a place for the army to winter in Cilicia. When the emperor had returned to Byzantium, Burzes, by frequent incursions, harassed the Agarenes in Antioch, assiduously contemplating the possibility of capturing the city and gaining glory for himself. Therefore, at a certain time, having secretly measured the height of one of the towers and prepared ladders proportional to that height, he approached the walls of the city in silence on a winter night that was moonless and snowy. Having set the ladders against it, he ascended with three hundred men (for he had brought so many, and no one had perceived them), slew the guards of that tower and any others he encountered, and immediately sent word to the stratopedarch of what had happened, urging him to bring aid. But he hesitated; for it was said that the emperor had commanded him not to attack Antioch, because there was a common rumor that the capture of that city would be followed by the emperor's own death. When the Antiochenes realized that the towers had been occupied, they gathered there eagerly, intent on driving out the Romans. The stratopedarch, therefore, his judgment caught between two conflicting concerns—the imperial command and the death of Burzes and the three hundred men with him—decided that he must not allow so many men to perish. Bringing along all his forces, he attacked Antioch, and the enemy immediately lost both their strength and their resolve. Burzes, who with his men had already despaired of salvation, took heart; and having cut through the doors with an axe, he provided the stratopedarch with an open entry. With such ease did the illustrious Antioch come into the power of the Romans. This, when reported to the emperor, brought him more grief than joy; and, angered at the stratopedarch and Burzes, he received the glorious feat with insults and
threats, stripped him of his command, and ordered him to remain confined to his house.
XXVII. Another incident occurred which exasperated the people against the emperor, and the emperor against the people. According to an ancient custom, on one of the feasts of the Lord, the emperor went in procession to the divine church at the Spring [Pega]; and as it happened, a quarrel broke out between the Armenians and the sailors, which resulted in a great commotion, much noise, and the death of men on both sides. The news of what had happened reached those in the city before the emperor’s return, and the unruly members of the populace, dividing into bands in various places, went out to meet the Armenians as they were returning and beat them with clubs. As the emperor had reached the market of the Bread-sellers, they began to heap insults upon him; a tumult arose, with shouting and jostling, and some threw stones at the emperor, while others threw dust at him and reviled him as far as the forum which is paved with slabs, where the round porphyry column stands. And had not some of the more honorable citizens there received the emperor and restrained and driven off those who were pursuing him, hurling insults and rioting in disorder—escorting him instead with a circle of men and acclaiming him as they conducted him to the palace—a great disaster might have resulted from that sedition. From this, a rumor spread that the emperor, nursing his anger against the people, intended to take revenge upon them at the horse races. Some time later, when a horse race was held, the emperor brought armed soldiers into the middle of the theater, either to provide the people with a pleasant spectacle of a battle, or perhaps to strike fear into them. When the soldiers drew their swords, the people in the theater, thinking this was the very thing that had long been bruited about in men’s talk, rushed to flee from the theater; and as they were crowded together at the exits, many were trampled by one another and perished in no small numbers. And the danger might have spread much further, had they not seen the emperor remaining unmoved upon his seat, upon which they ceased their flight. But the relatives of the dead, mourning their own, publicly mocked the emperor with jeers; and because he knew that he was for this reason hated by the citizens, he surrounded the palace with a wall. When this was completed and the keys were brought to him, that which he feared befell him regardless, and the fortification he had devised was of no use at all. As for the Turks, the Hungarians
Various readings and notes
(64) That a procession was held according to an ancient custom, Leo the Deacon indicates, in his Hist. lib. iv: etc. Moreover, Luitprand mentions this imperial departure in his *Legatio*, whose apparatus and pomp he describes thus: "Therefore, for these three weeks Nicephorus had his *metastasis*—that is, his station—outside Constantinople, in a place called... that is, at the Springs, and he ordered me to come to the same place." But whether this is the one Zonaras mentions here is not entirely certain. See our *Constantinopolis*, lib. iv, where we treat of this temple.
knowing that he was hated by the citizens, he surrounded the palaces with a wall; but when this was completed, and the keys to the gates were carried off, then the fear he had dreaded came upon him, and he derived no benefit from the security he had devised for himself. While the Turks, that is to say the Hungarians, were ravaging Thrace, the Emperor Nicephorus wrote to the prince of Bulgaria not to allow them to cross the Ister and harass the Roman provinces. But he did not obey, saying: "When they were warring against us, you, when requested to aid us, were unwilling. And now, when we have been forced to make a truce with them, do you deem us worthy to break the treaty, to take up arms against them, and to stir up an unprovoked war?" Repulsed, therefore, by this, he sent an embassy to the prince of Russia, Sphendosthlavus, inciting him against the Bulgarians; for this reason he also sent the son of the one presiding in Cherson, Calocyrus, to Russia. A Russian force therefore took the field against the Bulgarians, and plundered a great part of their land, and carried off booty beyond count; and in the following year the Rus afflicted the Bulgarians no less than before.
XXVIII. During the reign of Phocas there was a most terrifying earthquake, which, although completely harmless in Constantinople, nevertheless did great damage to other cities. In the month of May, winds blew by which the crops were ruined, and a severe famine occurred, which Nicephorus turned to his own profit, hoarding wheat in granaries and selling it at a high price to those suffering from hunger; he did not reflect that he was cursed by the public, as Solomon says in the Proverbs, but rather boasted that he was a benefactor to his subjects, because he sold two *medimni* for a *nomisma*. And he did this while having as an example Basil the Macedonian, who, in such a shortage not long before, had behaved in a magnificent manner. For when that emperor was once going as was his custom in procession to the church of the Holy Apostles, and had caught sight of certain citizens of bearing and gravity who appeared sorrowful, he asked the cause of their sadness. They answered: "Lord, how can we not be sorrowful, seeing death hanging over us, when two *medimni* of grain must be bought for a gold *nomisma*?" Hearing this, he groaned, and with money he dispelled their sadness, and he insulted and cursed the civil magistrates because they had not informed him of the scarcity, and he immediately ordered the imperial granaries to be opened. And he indeed acted as it befits an emperor; but this man behaved like an innkeeper, who, to a people suffering from lack of provisions,
offered no assistance, but even insulted them in their calamities, inhumanely tearing open the wounds of those whom hunger had injured. His brother Leo, too, by trafficking in the same way, oppressed both the city populace and the provincials. Since they behaved in such a way toward the afflicted, they were in turn pelted with sarcastic remarks by the people. For when the emperor was once reviewing newly recruited soldiers and saw a certain old man among the others, he asked, "Why, man, would an old person enlist for military service?" He immediately and wittily replied, "I am much stronger now, Master, than when I was in my prime. For then I could not have shouldered even half an aureus worth of grain, but now I will carry the grain of two aurei more easily." The emperor understood the irony, but, bearing it calmly, moved on to another.
Although Nicephorus had previously been vehemently enamored of Theophano, he subsequently refrained from carnal relations with her, either from satiety or from continence; for even in his youth he had not been much inclined toward lust. But she, abhorring her husband either on account of this alienation or being anxious about her sons (for there were some whispering that Nicephorus wished to castrate the royal boys and transfer the empire to his brother Leo), spoke secretly with Tzimiskes, either because she was captivated by the man's love (for he was preeminent in beauty and exuded the graces of his form) or because she judged him to be an adversary of equal standing to Nicephorus. Some even recount that she had consorted with him and, having incited him against Nicephorus, opportunely pushed the cart, as the proverb has it. For John was otherwise angry with the emperor, because, being suspected due to the envy and calumnies of the emperor's brother, he had been transferred from military command to a civil one and designated logothete of the dromos, which he judged to be not a position of power, but a heavy punishment. Added to these evils was the fact that he had been ordered to remain at home while dwelling in the Theme. For these reasons, John was angry. And when he found that wanton woman egging him on—she who had already brought about his return by imperial order—he set himself against the tyranny, having her as a collaborator, and, taking the empress and the most powerful of the [nobles]
as his accomplices, he revealed the secret to his associates. Embarking upon the crime, he arrived at the palace in the dead of the night with them in a skiff, at the place where it is washed by the southern sea. That spot is called the Bucoleon, because a stone lion is carved there, which, as it sits upon a bull of the same material, grips it almost by the left horn and twists its neck. When they had arrived there, he saw a basket let down, and in it he and his men were drawn up by the servants of Theophano. He had with him Michael Bourtzes, who was hostile to the emperor on account of that ill-considered rage of his, and Leo Abalantes.
Having revealed the secret along with those whom he was burying, he set to work, and taking them with him, in the dead of the night, he boarded a skiff and arrived at the southern coast of the palace. The place is called Boukoleon 71, because a stone lion is carved there, standing upon a bull of similar size, and with its left paw it grips the bull’s horn, and twists the neck of the bull. Having reached this spot, he espied a basket hanging from above, and by means of it, he and those with him were hauled up by the servants of Theophano. These were Michael Bourtzes—who was also embittered against the emperor because of his, as has been said, unreasoning rage—and Leo Abalantes, and the swarthy Theodore, whom they called Atzypotheodoros ["Black-a-moor Theodore"] because of the darkness of his appearance, as well as two others.
Having crept into the imperial bedchamber in silence (for Theophano had managed even this for them), they found Nicephorus sleeping on the floor, and someone leaped upon him, insulted him, and roused him from sleep. When, awakened, he raised his head, he immediately received a mortal wound upon it, which shook both his body and his soul. Then Tzimiskes sat upon the imperial couch, and they brought Phocas to him, already failing and supported from both sides. He asked him the reasons for which he had been harmed by him; but as he answered nothing, but only kept saying, "Lord, have mercy" 73, he ordered those around him to shatter his jaws by striking them with the hilts of their swords, or to knock out his teeth. Then someone from behind pierced him in the back with a spear, and the spear emerged through his chest 73.
As a clamor had arisen from those stationed nearby (for they had already become aware of the conspiracy), and he had just breathed his last, his head was cut off and shown to the bodyguards through a window, and thus their frenzy was quelled. It is said that a few days earlier Nicephorus had found a document in his bedchamber, revealing the conspiracy. Leo, the own brother of the Emperor Nicephorus, having learned of what had happened, took refuge in the Great Church with his son Nicephorus. The murdered emperor was fifty-seven years old, of which he had held the Roman scepter for six years and as many months 74 75.
Variæ lectiones et notæ.
71 Boukoleon. I have collected many things regarding this in my Notes on Villehardouin and in *Constantinopoli Christiana*; I only add that it can be gathered from the continuator of Theophanes, lib. III, n. 34, that marble statues of lions and bulls were accustomed to be placed at the gates of cities of this kind.
72 Lord, have mercy. A formula of supplication, or also adjuration, very familiar to the Greeks. St. Athanasius in *Apologia ad Constantium Aug.*: [...] As the *Apophthegmata Patrum* in Macarius, n. 19: [...] The same Scylitzes, p. 781, testifies that it was shouted in battles. Sometimes they also invoked the Mother of God, with this formula, as in Scylitzes: Theophanes in the year of Copronymus, p. 325: [...] But regarding both formulas see what we note in *Dissert. de inferioris ævi numismatibus*, n. 28 et 30.
73 Emerge. Anonymus Salernitanus, part vii: But while these things were being done, Pandulfus, regarding whom we spoke before, a prince while he was held captive at Constantinople, and the aforesaid Emperor Nicephorus himself occurred. For since he was just and an observer of the law, his most cruel wife Theophana, because of the madness of her greed, together with John Simulchi (Tzimiskes)—he who at that time held the duchy—cruelly killed him, and John himself took the empire.
74 But since the six years and as many months. Liutprand writes in his *Legatio*, in those Sibylline books of the Greeks, in which it was written how many years each emperor was to live, or to rule, it was foretold that Nicephorus would live only for seven years.
75 Of the Romans. Liutprand in his *Legatio* depicts the stature and form of [Nicephorus] Phocas thus: "I was led before Nicephorus, a man quite monstrous, a dwarf, fat-headed, and like a mole in the smallness of his eyes, soiled by a short, broad, and half-gray beard, disfigured by a neck as thick as a finger, in the lushness and density of his hair like an Ethiopian, whom you would not want to meet in the middle of the night, extended in belly, dry in buttocks, very long in the hips to the very measure, short in the legs, and equal in his heels and feet," etc., which indeed I know not whether they agree with Leo the Deacon, lib. II, where he depicts Phocas thus:
BOOK SEVENTEENTH
A 1. And he thus paid the debt. But Tzimiskes, having gained mastery of the empire, made the children of Romanos partners in the government [(76)], though they were still children. He took up the chamberlain Basil—whom Phokas had honored with the title of president, though that title had not existed long before—and, as he was well-versed in the administration of public affairs, entrusted the whole management of them to him. And he removed from his path those who were related to Nikephoros, or who were suspected of still harboring goodwill toward him, banishing the curopalates Leo and his son Nikephoros to Lesbos, setting the city of Amaseia as a fixed boundary for the other son of Leo, Bardas, and dealing with others in various ways. He brought back those who had been exiled by Nikephoros, among whom were numbered bishops who had refused to subscribe to the decree by which it was ordained that no bishop should be appointed without his own consent. Later, John went to the Great Church to be adorned with the diadem. But Polyeuktos [(77)] did not allow him to enter the church, as he was polluted by murder. Tzimiskes bore the rebuke with a steady mind and, in his defense, declared that he had not committed the murder with his own hands, but that Abalantes and the Atzyptheodorus of Theophano had slain Nikephoros at her command. The patriarch then demanded that Theophano be driven from the palace and banished, that the slayers of Nikephoros be sent into exile, that the decree we mentioned be torn up, and that John himself submit to penance. He promised that he would do everything, and immediately sent guards to bring the empress out of the palace, tore up the decree that had been brought to him, and promised to distribute to the poor the wealth he had possessed while a private citizen. When these things had been accomplished, on the feast of the Nativity of our Savior and God, he was admitted into the Church and simultaneously adorned with the diadem, while Theophano was banished to Prokonnesos. C Having thus been proclaimed emperor, since the Great Antioch lacked a bishop, he appointed a certain monk, Theodorus, who had prophesied the empire to him and had bidden him not to hasten or seize it, but to await the appointment of the Divine. Having accepted the bishopric, he requested from him that the Manichaeans, who by their foul sect were contaminating many, should be transferred from the East to the West. He complied with his requests and the nation...
Various readings and notes
(76) ...those whom Nikephoros Phokas had previously placed far within himself, as we gather from Liudprand in Legation: The two boy emperors, once his masters, now subjects, sat to the left, not in the same line, but far below.
(77) By Polyeuktos. Balsamon thus recounts this on can. 12 of the Ancyran synod: The patriarch Polyeuktos first drove out the emperor Lord John Tzimiskes from the sacred precincts of the most holy Great Church of God, as one who had murdered the emperor Lord Nikephoros Phokas, but later he received him. For he said, along with the holy synod, in the synodical act performed at that time and stored in the Chartophylakeion, that the baptism of holy water wipes away sins committed before it, whatever and how many they may be; and by all means the anointing of the empire also wiped away the murder committed by Tzimiskes before it. It was decided that those who had sacrificed before baptism, and were baptized after these things, should be advanced in rank, as having been washed clean.
He designates a certain monk, Theodore, who had prophesied to him that he would attain the empire, and urged him not to be hasty or to seize it, but to await the appointment from God. When he had accepted the priesthood, he made a petition to the emperor John to relocate the Manichaeans from the East to the West, as they were corrupting many with their filthy heresy. Fulfilling this request, he moved the Manichaean race to Philippopolis. The patriarch Polyeuctus, having survived for thirty-five days after the accession of Tzimiskes, departed this life, and the monk Basil Scamandrenus was introduced into the episcopal throne of the Queen of Cities. But the Hagarites, thinking it intolerable to be deprived of the cities they had already lost, gathered together from everywhere and, having made a pact of alliance, and entrusting the leadership to the Carthaginians, campaigned against Antioch by Daphne, and began to besiege it. As the citizens within the city resisted them with great fortitude, the uprising of the nations was reported to the emperor. He commanded the commander of Mesopotamia to bring aid to the besieged, and upon a battle being joined against the Hagarenes, the barbarians, although far superior in number to the Romans, were routed and scattered. As for the Rus, having brought the Bulgarian nation and their country under their own control, and having set aside the leaders of that nation, Boris and Romanus, they no longer remembered their own homes, but desired to remain there, occupying the land; and Calocyrus incited them to this. For he said, "If I am proclaimed Emperor of the Romans by you, I will cede the land of the Bulgarians to you, and I will establish a perpetual peace with you, and I will provide much more than what was promised." They, being charmed by the excellence of the lands and puffed up by the words of Calocyrus, did not obey the emperor's letter, in which he affirmed that he would fulfill all that Nicephorus had promised them, and they treated the ambassadors with arrogance. For this reason, he was compelled to take up arms against them, and having appointed as army commander the magistros Bardas Sclerus — who was the brother of his recently deceased wife — he ordered him to set out against the Rus. The barbarian leader Sphendosthlavus, having learned of this, armed the Bulgarians and, having taken on as allies the Scythian Patzinaks and also the Turks who inhabit Pannonia, gathered an army of about three hundred thousand men and began plundering all of Thrace. Sclerus, not daring to engage in battle against such a multitude, since he was far inferior in forces, overcame the barbarians with military cunning. For first, having set up ambushes and attacking only the Patzinaks, he skillfully lured them into the ambushes; some he destroyed, and some he took alive. Then he fought on equal terms for a time against the rest. But as a certain Scythian, who seemed superior to all his countrymen in bulk of body and vigor of spirit, saw Sclerus riding on horseback among his own ranks and inciting them to valor, he spurred his horse and struck with his sword at the man’s head; however, due to the strength and smoothness of the helmet, it did not penetrate. Sclerus in turn struck the enemy on the head with such vehemence that the barbarian was severed into two parts, and his middle parts fell to either side of the horse. Terror-stricken by this deed, the barbarians turned to flight, and the Romans, pursuing them from the rear, killed many and took many captive. The falling of night brought an end to the pursuit of the remnants of the barbarians, who were indeed already suffering from wounds.
II. While these things were being conducted against the Russians, Bardas Phocas, the son of Leo, having fled from Amaseia where he was living in exile, occupied Caesarea in Cappadocia and, relying on the resources of factional men, laid claim to the tyranny, having assumed the imperial insignia. His father, Leo, together with his other son, Nicephorus, also planned to cross from Lesbos to Thrace, with the Archbishop of Abydos assisting them. But when this plan became known, he was condemned, together with his son Nicephorus, to have his eyes put out; however, it is said that the emperor secretly commanded the ministers to pretend to perform the blinding, but to preserve their sight unharmed. When these things had been accomplished and the Scythians—as the Russians were recently called—had been routed, letters were brought to Sclerus from the emperor, instructing him to take his army into the East and crush Bardas Phocas. When he had arrived at Caesarea, Phocas was deserted by his own faction (for everyone was flocking to Sclerus) and proceeded alone with his attendants to the castle of Tyropaeon. When he was not far from the aforementioned fortress, the horsemen sent by Sclerus caught up with him. One of them, Constantine Charon, being more audacious than the rest, outstripped his fellow soldiers and, charging with a more violent and persistent impetus at Phocas—who was bringing up the rear of his own company—harassed the man with insults and reproaches. To him he replied: "It does not befit you, I say, to insult a man struggling with such a calamity." But as Charon meanwhile came closer and was about to throw a weapon at him, he raised his mace and struck Constantine upon his helmet, shattering it together with the man's head, so that falling from that single blow, he spewed forth his life's blood. Those who were following behind, seeing him lying there, ceased their pursuit, and Phocas entered the fortress. Afterward, Sclerus also arrived and persuaded him to surrender himself to the emperor's good faith; and, upon his approach, he became a cleric and was relegated to Chios, while Sclerus was recalled to the West. The emperor, through the zeal of Basil the chamberlain, took as his wife Theodora, daughter of Constantine Porphyrogenitus, and in the second year of his reign, during the spring, he waged war against the Russians with many armies and a notable fleet. As he was departing, Scythian scouts, who were sent to explore the strength of the Romans, met him under the guise of an embassy. The emperor, not unaware of why they were there, ordered them to be led around through all the camps and, having inspected them, to be sent away. When they had departed, he himself, followed by five thousand light-armed troops and four thousand cavalry, arrived unexpectedly at Great Persthlava and fortified his camp; this event brought terror to the Tauroscythians. Calocyrus, however, who...
was the prime mover of these evils, being present there at the time and learning of the emperor’s arrival, fled to the camp of the Rus; having learned from him of the emperor’s presence, they were troubled, yet they nonetheless drew up their military camp against the Romans. Those with the emperor, having come to the city of Persthlava, as has been said, found about eight thousand men training outside, and they engaged with them. They held their ground for a short while, then turned their backs and fled. Those inside, arming themselves as best they could, went out to aid their countrymen and marched toward them in disorder. The Romans, meeting them, slaughtered many, while others retreated into the city. But when some Roman cavalry rode out and blocked the path leading into the city, the Barbarians were scattered on the ground outside the city, where some were taken captive and others were killed, and those in the city, having shut the gates, were besieged. On the following day, when Basil the chamberlain arrived with the entire army, the emperor pressed the siege more vigorously; some, having scaled the wall using ladders, slew the Barbarians they caught there, opened the gates, and afforded the entire Roman army an easy entry. In this way the city of Persthlava became subject to the Romans, and there the Bulgarian king, Boris, was also captured, whom and all the Bulgarians the emperor treated kindly, setting them free and saying that he had not taken up arms against the Bulgarians, but against the Rus. Since many Scythians had fled into a certain fortification, the emperor ordered that it too should be taken. The soldiers, however, were reluctant due to the difficulty of the location. John, perceiving this, went ahead of the others on foot toward the fortification, and in this way shamed the soldiers so that he had them following him; the fort was taken, and of those within, some were destroyed while others became captives to the Romans. Having established a sufficient garrison in this city, the emperor departed for Dorostolum, which is also called Dristra; having captured some cities along the way and razed their forts, he reached Dorostolum. The leader of the Rus, Sphendosthlavus, was camped before this city. When the armies saw one another, they immediately engaged in battle, and for the greater part of the day
they fought with equal fortune. Towards late evening, the Rus, being exhausted, inclined toward flight, and the Romans pursued them. Most of the Barbarians were destroyed, and no fewer were taken prisoner, but others returned to Dorostolon. The Emperor set up a camp in front of the city and waited for the triremes guarding the Ister, so that the Rus might not escape. Sphendosthlavus, fearing that the Bulgarians he held—who were twenty thousand in number, as is said—might revolt against him or go over to the Emperor, placed them all in chains. When the fleet also arrived, the Emperor besieged the city. Ambassadors came to the Emperor from Constantia and other forts, asking for pardon and surrendering themselves and the forts to him. The Romans took possession of these. The siege of Dorostolon lasted for many days, with the Barbarians struggling bravely. But as many had become wounded from the constant fighting, and their vital supplies had already failed them, their spirits began to break. However, Sphendosthlavus, waiting for a moonless and stormy night, embarked about two thousand men in monoxyla (dugout canoes). Having boarded them himself, he went away to gather provisions, and having collected what was needed, they returned to Dorostolon. Seeing some of the Roman servants scattered about the river on various errands, they landed from their boats, killed some of them, and secretly returned to Dorostolon. This greatly vexed the Emperor, who was angry with the commanders of the fleet and threatened them with death as their punishment if the Barbarians were ever to escape by sailing off again. Having besieged the city for sixty full days without yet having taken it, the Emperor decided to overcome those inside by famine, and he sat around the city, keeping a close watch so that no supplies could be brought to them from anywhere. The Barbarians often made sallies but were defeated. Since they were pressed by famine and had no hope of supplies or of allies, some advised fleeing by night, others suggested trusting themselves to the Romans and thus returning to their own lands, while others proposed something else.
III. Sphendosthlavus, however, encouraged his men to try the gamble of war once more, and either to prevail and be saved or to prefer an honorable death over a wretched life. This sentiment was approved by the crowd, and on the next day, not refusing to die, they engaged with the Romans. For a long time, sometimes the Romans and sometimes the Barbarians prevailed. But when the Emperor noticed that the narrowness of the place was to the advantage of the enemy, and that the Romans could not rush against them in a mass, he advised the generals to lead the battalions slowly back into the plain by retreating step by step. When this was done, the Barbarians, thinking the Romans were fleeing out of fear, followed them with shouts. When they had reached a wider field, the Emperor gave the signal to the Romans to turn about; they did so, and attacked the Barbarians, and the battle became terrible. The Emperor sent the Magistros Bardas Sclerus with the battalions under him to block the Barbarians' path back to the city. While the victory was for a long time uncertain, the Romans are said to have been aided by divine power. For a storm, it is said, beat against the faces of the Scythians, and a certain horseman was seen by many, who defended the Romans and threw the Barbarians' phalanxes into confusion. On that day, the memory of the holy master of soldiers, Theodore, the glorious martyr, was being celebrated. The Scythians were therefore put to flight and sought for the city, but since they could not enter it, being blocked by Sclerus, they were scattered into the suburbs, where they were slaughtered, and—trampled and crushed by one another—the number of the slain could not easily be reckoned. All the rest were afflicted with wounds. That the master of soldiers, Theodore, was an aid to the Romans against the Barbarians was known from the fact that a certain Byzantine woman, reli-
religious and modest, on the day before that battle, were seen in a dream to behold the holy Theotokos proceeding with a great retinue, and to hear the Mother of God saying to one of those who were walking ahead of her: "Lord Theodore, my John and yours is enduring heavy struggles; hasten, therefore, and bring him aid." The woman revealed the dream to some people. They noted the day, and it was the one before the final battle. The emperor, in return for the help, built a most beautiful church for the martyr at Euchania—or Euchaita—having torn down the old one, in which his body, which had finished many struggles, had been deposited, and he renamed the city, the old name having been abolished, Theodoropolis. But the barbarian Sphendosthlabos, being in despair from every side, sent envoys to the emperor, seeking pardon and requesting the opportunity to return home, and a station among the federates of the Romans, and free trade for the Tauroscythians in the Roman provinces. When the emperor had granted all things, Sphendosthlabos himself approached him, beseeching him to treat with the Patzinaks so that they might grant passage through their territory to the Russians returning home. The emperor, having asked for this, and for an alliance in war, and also that they might not cross the Ister to devastate Bulgaria, requested this of them; the Patzinaks agreed to the other things, but refused only the passage of the Russians.
IV. The war against the Russians having been finished in this manner, the emperor returned to Byzantium and was received brilliantly by the entire state. For the patriarch went out to meet him with all the clergy, along with the senate and the people, offering crowns and a four-horse chariot, and they requested that, having mounted it, he should triumph. But he accepted the crowns and triumphed on a single white horse, and he placed the royal robes of the Bulgarian kings on the chariot, and set an image of the Mother of God above them, and the chariot went before him. When he reached the so-called Placotian forum, he stripped Boris of the signs of the Bulgarian kingship and honored him with the dignity of master. Sphendosthlabos, however, while returning home with his Russians, was caught in an ambush by the Patzinaks and they were all destroyed at once. This emperor built a magnificent church of Christ the Savior at the Chalke. D Patriarch Basil Scamandrenus, having been removed from his position by a council four years after his designation due to certain crimes brought against him, Anthony Studites was designated patriarch. The emperor, meanwhile...
seeing that the cities which had been acquired for the Romans in the East were looking toward rebellion and had shaken off the yoke of servitude and shattered the bonds of Roman rule, campaigned against them. Some he brought back to the yoke of Roman rule without conflict, while others he forced with iron and great might. Returning from there, and arriving at Anabarza and Podandos, and seeing there and in many other regions villages that were populous and fertile in every respect, he inquired whose they were. And learning that they belonged to the *parakoimomenos* Basil—some having been acquired for the empire and gifted to him by the emperor Nicephorus Phocas, some by himself while he was *domestikos* of the schools, and others by others—he was aggrieved and said it was unjust that a single eunuch should enjoy public holdings acquired by the sweat and blood of many excellent men. B These words, reported to the *parakoimomenos*, incited him to plot against the emperor. Having prepared a poisonous drug—not one that was active or quick-acting, but mild and working over time upon the one who drank—and having approached with gifts the one accustomed to serving wine to the emperor, he poured it into the sovereign's cup. Once consumed, it gradually weakened the constitution of the drinker's body, until it brought the emperor to his death. Thus, John Tzimiskes having departed from here, he left the empire to its heirs, namely Basil and Constantine, the sons of Romanus, having reigned for six years and as many months.
Now, I must relate a matter that had almost escaped me. For how the *kuropalates* Leo Phocas and his son Nicephorus, though sentenced to blinding, were not deprived of their sight—the emperor having secretly ordered that they be spared—I have already recorded. But not content with this generosity, they aspired to the throne again, having reconciled many of the citizens and courtiers to themselves. And having been discovered in this scheme, betrayed by one of the conspirators, they met their end, both of their happiness and of their ambition, when their eyes were gouged out without any mercy.
Various readings and notes
(81) That the emperor Nicephorus found the sandals of our Savior Christ and the venerated hair of the Forerunner in Membij, and placed the former in the church of the Blachernae and the latter in the church of the Chalke, which he himself had erected from the foundations: Concerning this fortress, which is called *Membe* in the Syriac tongue, which he attacked with war and all manner of engines, having found the sandals of our Savior Christ there, he took them as a divine gift, and the hair of the venerable Forerunner and Herald. He deposited the former as a kind of precious treasure in the famous temple of the Mother of God, which was built at the imperial residence; and the latter in the temple of the Savior, which he himself raised from the foundations. The emperor himself, most glorious and lofty, presided in the so-called Chalke prison, together with it the divine temple, which the great among kings, John who followed Nicephorus Phocas, built.
(82) Basil, who is called the Younger: "King of the whole earth, sun of the purple, Basil, the offspring of the imperial robe, mightiest of both in trophies and words," and he is also called *Porphyrogenitus* by Michael Psellus at the end of his *Synopsis of Laws*.
V. Thus the Roman empire was restored to those to whom it belonged by hereditary right, Basil and Constantine, of whom the former was in his twentieth year of age, and the latter in his seventeenth. Although they bore the insignia of the empire, all authority lay with the president Basil; and Basil, not yet trusting in himself, endured this, hearkening to the master of the bedchamber as if to a tutor, and conforming himself to the latter's handling and administration of the commonwealth, so that he, too, when time required, might follow the same method in military enrollments and in maintaining civil equity. For he was of a lively and stimulated temperament, not dissolute or enslaved by sloth, as his brother was.
When these first obtained control of affairs, the president recalled their mother from exile and restored her to her sons; and because he held Bardas Sclerus, the *magister militum* to whom all the Eastern forces were obedient, in suspicion, he stripped him of that dignity and designated him Duke of Mesopotamia. Sclerus, bearing this with a most heavy heart—and being otherwise ablaze with a desire for the kingdom—was incited to defection. Through that office, and having secretly communicated with many, he held almost all the soldiers obedient to his word, because he was most favored among them for his brave deeds. He called for his son Romanus, who was living in the royal city, secretly through a certain individual; and having summoned him, he immediately affected the kingdom, having taken the diadem and purple shoes.
Having been saluted as Emperor, he zealously sought out funds and recruited as allies the Emir of Amida (which is also called Emet) and the Emir of Martyropolis (which is called Miephercim), and indeed also the Arabs. Joined by these, he hastened toward [the city of] Constantine, buoyed by good hope, which was further increased by the dream of a certain monk, a man devoted to virtue, who seemed to himself to see a certain woman sitting on a high watchtower and offering him an imperial lash. Although that lash, in any event, signified the wrath of God and the destruction of the Romans through intestine wars, Sclerus, estimating the vision according to his own desire as an omen of empire, interpreted it [favorably].
When the report of Sclerus’s tyranny reached the city, the emperors gathered whatever soldiers remained and prepared to resist, in the meantime sending the bishop of Nicomedia to him, who, although he proposed many conditions for peace that were not to be despised, obtained nothing. For Sclerus, pointing to his crimson shoe, said that it was not possible for one who had once put it on to cast it off rashly. That answer having been received, necessity was laid upon the emperors to contend by war, with the eunuch Peter, son of Phocas (of whom mention has also been made above), as leader of the forces.
The armies met at Lipara, which is also called Licandus; the imperial forces resisted vigorously for a time, but were finally routed, with many slain and their camp plundered. Tzamandus, a very populous city, also made submission to Sclerus, from which he collected a great sum of money. Thus, with the emperors' forces overcome, he drew many to himself, as the wealth and safety of those [others] were already despaired of; and not only obscure soldiers, but many also of the illustrious men
as well, flocked to him. When these things were reported to the praepositus of the bedchamber, Leo the protovestiarius was sent again with supreme power and authority, having received the license to conduct everything by his own judgment just as if he were the emperor—a power which the Latins call a dictatorship. Having joined the stratopedarches, and having tried in vain to sound out the spirits of the soldiers with promises and honors,1 he passed by Sclerus under cover of night and attacked him. For this reason it happened that the soldiers, fearing for their own loved ones, abandoned Sclerus and betook themselves to him. Sclerus, terrified, sent the magister Michael Burzes, who had followed his side, and the patrician Romanus Taronites with an army against the protovestiarius; being defeated in battle, the protovestiarius himself attacked them with all his forces, conquered them, and captured the man, while the stratopedarches Peter was slain. This victory greatly increased the resources of Sclerus, just as it brought the cause of the emperors to despair, especially since the tyrant also held dominion over the sea. Wherefore the praepositus of the bedchamber sent a fleet, and having joined in battle with the ships of Sclerus, he defeated and dispersed his fleet. Once the sea was pacified, having undertaken the care of the provinces, he sent Eroticus to Nicaea, the metropolis of Bithynia, to defend it. Sclerus tried in vain to take it by force, and thinking it could be forced to surrender by famine through treachery, he drew out the siege. Eroticus, however, decided to circumvent the enemy by a ruse; having brought sand into the granaries and having thrown grain on top of it, so that the whole heap appeared to be grain, he showed the captives the half-full granaries and ordered them to report to Sclerus that, although he was in no danger from famine, yet if it were permitted for him to depart safely with certain men, he would surrender the city and join his party. Rejoicing at this news, Sclerus pledged his faith; whereupon he departed for Constantinople with the greater part of the citizens and his own soldiers. But when Sclerus entered Nicaea, he understood that he had been circumvented by a ruse. As for the praepositus of the bedchamber, once Sclerus had departed from there and was approaching the city, although he hesitated in his mind, he changed his opinion, recalled Bardas Phocas from exile, and having bound him by a most holy oath that he would never aspire to the kingdom or enter into counsel against the emperors, he appointed him magister, placed him in charge of the remnant of the forces, and, having liberally supplied him with money, sent him against Sclerus. When he learned of this, reckoning that at last he would have an equal adversary, he moved out from there, and a battle having been joined near Amorium, he emerged victorious, having routed his adversaries. On the following day, the leaders of the armies decided to engage in single combat and to decide the sum of affairs there. Sclerus was the first, as some relate, to strike his club against the head of Phocas; but others say he brandished his sword at him, which, when Phocas had dodged the blow, sliced off the ear of his horse with the tip of the blade. But Phocas, on the contrary, struck his club against the head of Sclerus in such a way that, with dizziness ensuing, he slumped onto the neck of his horse and was bloodied, so that his guards snatched him away, and, stripped of his strength, he was taken down from his horse, his blood was wiped away, and they refreshed him at a fountain. Meanwhile, his horse, while they were occupied with this care, escaped from the hands of the man by whom it was held and, without a rider, [fled] into the c-
But in the East there was a great multitude of Manichaeans, who are more ignorantly called by the common folk Paulicians, from a name composed of Paul and John; although these men were famous among them, they were not the originators of the heresy nor the authors of the perverse opinion, but rather its followers, students beyond measure, and heralds. The empress, having in mind to draw them away from their heresy to the true religion, sent distinguished men, who, having administered the business with no dexterity and no skill, not only undertook a vain labor, but also compelled the entire population, which numbered many thousands, to revolt; consequently, they joined forces with the Ishmaelites, campaigned with them against the Romans, and brought many disasters upon them.
But the emperor Michael, having passed the years of boyhood and reached adolescence, bore it with an ill will that the administration of the kingdom was in the hands of others, and at the same time was incited by his uncle Bardas, one of his tutors, with a desire to conduct the principles of government according to his own will. Manuel, however, having at one time had a dispute with Theoctistus, the Logothete of the Course (both of them were fellow tutors of the emperor), abandoned the palace where he was then residing, retreated to his own house, and from there, frequently visiting the palace, took hold of the administration of affairs. This was a subject of singular pleasure for Bardas, and he also studied how to remove Theoctistus; for, burning with the desire for power, he feared that he might be hindered by him. C Furthermore, the emperor had a tutor, a man of base and wicked character, but so pleasing to the youth that he wished to decorate him with a more illustrious dignity, and sought this from his mother. But Theoctistus denied that an honor should be bestowed upon the tutor which would exceed his condition, lest the dignities themselves be affected by indignity. From this, Theoctistus was hateful to the tutor, and this came as a very opportune opening for Bardas to carry out his own desire. Therefore, through the tutor, he instilled various slanders against Theoctistus into the ears of the emperor, stirred up his ease-loving nature against him, and finally became the author of his removal. He maintained that this could not be done in any other way than by killing him, for he argued that if he were left living, he would be protected by the empress and would prevail by his authority. The murder of the man was decreed. His exit from the empress’s bedchamber was watched, for he used to report to her what had been done and what was to be done. As he was coming out, an assassin was sent in. When he noticed a drawn sword raised against him, he took refuge behind a stool; the assassin plunged the sword into his belly, while Bardas himself also wielded a drawn sword, threatening death to anyone who would bring aid to him as he lay dying.
pranced about the camp, its mane drenched in blood. When the army of Sclerus caught sight of it (for the horse was famous, being called "Egyptian") and thought that their commander had fallen, they rushed headlong into flight. Phocas, observing what was happening, attacked the fugitives while encouraging his own men; many of the enemy were slain, many were captured, and still more perished, being trampled by one another. From there, Sclerus, finding himself in straits, fled with the small number that remained to Chosroes the Babylonian. Upon learning this, the Emperor sent to Chosroes, requesting that he not receive the man who had played the tyrant and risen against his own master, lest he provide an evil precedent to his own detriment. The envoy also brought letters for Sclerus and those with him, confirmed by the imperial hand, offering them amnesty for their actions if they would cease from their undertaking and submit to the Emperor. When the Babylonian learned of these royal letters addressed to Sclerus and his companions, he imprisoned both the envoy himself, and Sclerus, and the soldiers with him. Thus, then, did the insurrection of Sclerus come to an end.
VI. Now when the patriarch Anthony had renounced the priesthood, which he had held for six years, and died a short time later, the Church remained widowed of its high priest for four and a half years, after which Nicolaus Chrysoberges was appointed. Meanwhile, the affairs of the Bulgarians were again in motion; for when the death of Tzimiskes was reported, they entrusted their authority to four brothers: David, Moses, Aaron, and Samuel, who were called the Comitopuli, because they were sons of one of the distinguished men among them, as they are called counts. For the royal line had failed amongst them, with only one of the sons of Peter surviving—namely Romanus, who was a eunuch. Of these four, David soon died, and Moses [fell] in the siege of Serra
by a stone blow, and died immediately. Samuel, meanwhile, removed his brother Aaron from life, along with his entire family, with the exception of one of his sons who was called by the double name of John-Sphandosthlavus; either because he wanted the supreme power for himself alone, or because he seemed to be favoring the Romans—for both are reported. And the principality of Bulgaria devolved upon Samuel alone, who, finding the Roman armies occupied with civil wars, went about all their Western provinces with impunity: and he did not merely ravage them, but even made the regions and cities subject to his own rule.
When, as has been recounted, the emperor had cast off the tyranny of Sclerus, he burned with a desire to avenge himself upon the barbarian, and resolved to wage war against him under his own auspices. He therefore set out on his own initiative, without communicating about the matter with the Magister Bardas Phocas, despite his being the Domestic of the Schools of the East, nor with any other of the commanders of the Eastern forces. Having already entered the borders of the Bulgarians, he ordered the Magister Leo Melissenus to follow behind to guard the mountain passes: he himself, meanwhile, prepared to lay siege to Sardica, which is also called Triaditza (83).
But Contostephanus, the Domestic of the Western regiments, thinking that if the emperor were successful in his military campaign he would never cease to conduct his wars personally under his own leadership—and that he himself and the rest of the commanders would no longer be considered of any great account—devised a scheme to undermine his undertaking and retard his advance. Coming to him, therefore, he falsely alleged that Melissenus was attempting a tyranny and was departing with haste for Byzantium to seize the throne, and he urged that one must not delay, but should turn the army back without hesitation. This perturbed the emperor to such an extent that he gave the signal for the host to retreat.
But Samuel, who was lurking on the mountain peaks (for he did not dare to fight in open battle), seeing the sudden withdrawal, attacked the Romans, whom he assumed were acting out of fear, and put them to flight with an unexpected assault; and he seized not only the camp, but also the imperial tent and imperial regalia. The emperor, with difficulty, escaped to Philippopolis, where he found Melissenus, and being enraged against Contostephanus, he cast insults at the man. The latter, finding it hard to bear, grew sharp in return, and the emperor, his bile surging with anger, sprang from his throne, and catching Contostephanus by the hair and beard, dashed the man to the ground. From this, the leaders of the Roman forces, angered at the emperor—Phocas, because he saw his hopes frustrated and his affairs proceeding, as the proverb says, like those of Mandrabolus; others, because they thought themselves despised, having not been consulted in the planning; others for other reasons—gathered in Charsianon, and invested Bardas Phocas with the diadem, proclaiming him emperor, having managed their defection, as they thought, quite properly. Sclerus, however, who was being held in Babylon, was released from custody beyond all expectation. For the empire of the Persians having been overthrown by the Saracens, a certain Persian named Inargus incited the populace with his words, persuading them to break the yoke of servitude and take up arms against the Saracens under his leadership. Chosroes, moreover, although he had often fought against them...
was defeated as many times as he engaged them, and in despair fled for refuge to the Romans who were held in confinement. He released them from their prison and consulted with Sclerus about the war, asking him not to hold the past ill-treatment against him. Though Sclerus at first declined the war, upon the insistence of the Babylonian he gave his consent, but on the condition that he lead only Romans into battle. The prisons were therefore searched, and as many as three thousand Romans were found; these were brought out and armed. Having conquered the Persians under the leadership and auspices of Sclerus, neither they nor he decided to return to Chosroes, fearing that they would again be cast into prison by the barbarian, especially after having given such a specimen of their valor. Chosroes, indeed, while they were intent on flight, sent a great crowd to pursue them; and these, although far superior in number, once battle was joined, escaped in smaller numbers than those they were pursuing, the others being killed there by Roman hands.
VII. Sclerus, therefore, with his men, had returned to the Roman borders. Having learned that Phocas was already aspiring to the kingship and had begun a tyranny, he inclined toward Phocas, desiring to be a partner in both the war and the empire itself. Phocas accepted the partnership, and if they should attain their desires, he reserved for himself the queen of cities and the lordship of the greater part of the nations and territories; to Sclerus he assigned Antioch, Celesyria, Phoenicia, Palestine, Mesopotamia, and Egypt, and he confirmed these agreements with oaths. Relying on this, Sclerus approached Phocas. But the latter, having caught him within his net, stripped him of his royal apparel, threw him into prison, and placed a guard over him. Having brought the army that was with him under his own command, he held his tyrannical position more firmly. Sclerus, however, providing for himself some salvation against future failure, before he was seized by Phocas, had arranged for his son Romanus to flee secretly, so that he might defect to the emperor; he was received with great joy by the ruler and was immediately appointed magister. Phocas, meanwhile, having placed a portion of the army under the patrician Delphinas, sent him to Chrysopolis, which lies opposite the queen of cities, while he himself set out with the rest of the army for Abydos. But as for Delphinas, who had encamped at Chrysopolis, the emperor suddenly attacked him with a Russian army (for he had formed an affinity with Vladimirus, their leader, by taking his sister Anna to wife, and had summoned auxiliary forces from there), and having easily overcome the enemies,
he captured Delphinas himself and impaled him on a stake.
Then he proceeded to Abydus with his brother Constantine. When the opposing armies stood in battle array, and Phocas was positioned at the head of his own troops, while Emperor Basil rode around on horseback encouraging his own legions, Phocas, seeing him, drove his horse toward him, having given the horse its head, judging that if he could strike him down, he would have won the battle. But before he had gone far from his own troops, some say he was thrown from his horse, while others say he turned aside to a hill, dismounted, and fell to the ground, where he immediately yielded up his spirit. Some claimed the man had been struck by a blow in a vital spot. Emperor Constantine, for his part, attributed the man's death to himself—but this was mere talk, for nowhere was any wound found on his body. Others conjectured that he had been done away with by a poisonous drug, which indeed seemed more plausible. The drug, however, had been prepared by Basil, and was offered to Phocas by his cupbearer, who had been suborned by the Emperor's bribes.
When Bardas had fallen, his army was scattered; some were killed as they were caught, while others were taken alive and, having been seized, were punished according to the Emperor's will. He became more savage than before; for he had grown haughty, base in character, suspicious of everyone, and inexorable in his anger. Nor did he show this arrogance only to others, but also to his own *parakoimomenos* (chamberlain). He grew to hate the man and hastened to transfer the administration of affairs to himself; and indeed, he even removed him from the management of public business, ordering him to remain at home and not to go out. A little later, he sent him into exile; and having scrutinized each of the things he had done, he judged most of his actions to be idle. He stripped him of most of the monastery (85) he had built, and he seized not only his fields, lands, and houses, but also his votive offerings, marble slabs, and costly columns. Whereupon that man, overcome by such a cloud of despondency, became paralyzed in his limbs, and appearing as a living corpse, after a short while, he miserably departed this life.
It was then—and only then—that Basil truly became autocrat, and having learned how great were the cares required by the administration of the empire, he spurned royal delights, abstained from splendid clothes, and repudiated every luxury—though he had not lived in such a manner before, but had previously indulged in love affairs, banquets, and pleasures, and delighted in royal luxury. Now, having claimed for himself all authority, he afforded his brother nothing but the name and the distinguished insignia of power; he was kept under guard, albeit a light and far from magnificent one. Since he was a man of a more relaxed and softer nature, a voluptuary who delighted in hunting and the company of his peers, he bore this state of things and did not oppose his brother. Such, then, was their situation. Sclerus, however, upon the death of Bardas, was released from his chains, by...
and again took up his former undertaking, nor did he delay in rebelling once more. The emperor, by letters inscribed to him, exhorted the man to cease at last from his folly and not to wish eternally to be the cause of wars among his own kin, nor to stain the earth with the blood of Christians. But being already worn out by old age and feeling dizzy at the thought of the future, he made terms and approached the sovereign. The latter was seated in the imperial tent, and Sclerus was walking toward the tent, supported on either side—partly from old age, and partly from the bulk of his body (for he was large)—at which time it is said that the emperor Basil uttered that celebrated phrase: "He whom I feared is being led to me by others guiding him." Others say that Sclerus had lost his sight upon approaching the emperor, and that this was why he had to be guided. When the man had approached the tent, the emperor noticed that he was wearing crimson shoes on his feet (for Sclerus had cast off the other insignia of royalty, but had not removed the shoes; whether he had forgotten or did so from thoughtlessness, he did not remove them). He immediately turned away his eyes and would not endure to speak with him unless he were in the attire of a private citizen. When, therefore, he had cast off those invidious shoes before the tent, the emperor arose upon his entering, reached out his right hand, conversed with him, and both used the same table and drank from the same chalice. In this way the tyranny of Sclerus was put to rest, and he was appointed curopalates; and each of those who had assisted him was permitted to retain whatever degree of dignity and power, or whatever possessions, he had received from him during his rule. The emperor, relieved of the cares of the defections, went off to Thrace and visited Thessalonica to give thanks to the celebrated martyr. Having left behind there a man of distinction as commander, with a large force, to restrain the incursions of Samuel, he returned to Byzantium. From there he set out for Iberia, having been left as heir by the curopalates David, who had died, to the realm which he had held. Having reached that place and taken possession of the country left to him—and having induced George, the brother of David and ruler of inner Iberia, to remain quiet and be content with his own domains, and having taken his son as a hostage—he marched upon Phoenicia. He restrained the rulers of Tripoli, Damascus, Tyre, and Berytus—who had formed a conspiracy and were ravaging the territory of Antioch near Daphne—and compelled them to serve the Romans; and having received hostages from them also, he returned home. And these matters did indeed turn out in this way.
Various readings and notes
(86) ‘ἢ γὰρ λάθετ’ ἢ οὐκ ἐνόησεν.’ Homers’s hemistich in Iliad 1, verse 533.
VIII. When Nicolaus Chrysoberges had adorned the pontificate for twelve years and eight months and had departed this life, Sisinnius the magister, a man reared in letters from his early youth, was appointed patriarch. When he, too, had governed the Church for three years and died, Sergius was chosen patriarch; he had been in command of the monastery of Manuel and happened to be a relative of the patriarch Photius by lineage. Now, since Samuel, prince of the Bulgarians, was plundering not only Thrace and Macedonia, but also Greece and even the Peloponnesus, the emperor sent forth the magister Nicephorus Uranus, the praetor of the West, who, when Samuel had pitched his tents by the Spercheius river, set up camp on the opposite bank. Because the river could not be crossed on account of frequent rains, Samuel feared the aggression of the Romans not at all. But when Uranus had discovered a ford at a certain place by night, he led his army across in silence and suddenly attacked those around Samuel. The barbarians, stunned by the unexpected event, fell without even raising their arms to strike back. Samuel himself was wounded along with his son, and both would have been captured had they not hidden among the corpses and thus escaped unnoticed. Thereafter, the emperor himself waged war against the Bulgarians and, having destroyed some of the fortresses in Sardica, returned to Mosynopolis. He also captured the Great and Little Persth-lava and Pliscova through his generals. Berroa was also handed over to him by Dobromerus, who had gone over to the Romans. Servia, however, was taken by siege, and Nicolaus—whom they called Nicolitzas as a diminutive because of his short stature—to whom the guardianship of these places had been entrusted, was captured; although he had been honored with the dignity of patrician, he nevertheless fled and returned to Samuel. Then the emperor again waged war against Bidina and took the city. While the emperor was besieging it, Samuel suddenly attacked Adrianople; as the celebration of a fair was being held outside the walls, he plundered the merchandise and, having taken a multitude of captives, withdrew. When the emperor had brought Bidina under his control and was returning, he discovered Samuel encamped by the Axius river (which was formerly known as the Bardarius). As the river was in flood, the barbarians were guarding their camp carelessly, for they did not at all think that the Roman army would be able to cross the river. But once a ford was discovered by the emperor, he led the army across, and with many of the Bulgarians slain, Samuel’s tent and the entirety of his camp were plundered; Samuel himself fled. The emperor also received the city of Scopia, which had been handed over by Romanus, the son of Peter, formerly prince of the Bulgarians, to whom its guardianship had been entrusted by Samuel. Afterward, having returned to Constantinople, he decreed that the tax of the poor, who had perished, should be exacted from the rich, which was called *allelengyon*. And although many, including the patriarch Sergius himself, pleaded that this unjust and unusual exaction should be abolished, nevertheless...
the emperor was not to be persuaded. For by the length of time and his victories over all, he had become puffed up, and showed himself disdainful towards everyone; he wished not for the good will of his subjects, but for their fear—both the military and the civil classes alike. He did not wish to carry on affairs according to the established custom (which lawgivers pronounce to have the force of law), but according to his own judgment and will. Thus, he paid no heed to learned men, and even considered rhetoric to be something superfluous and useless. Consequently, he chose men for himself who were distinguished neither by their birth nor by their erudition, and to them he entrusted the imperial correspondence and shared his councils; to them also he would dictate his compositions in a simple manner, just as it occurred to him. Furthermore, having blocked the exits of the treasuries, he filled them with money. It is said, indeed, that two hundred thousand talents of gold were stored up in his treasuries. For no one could calculate the rest of his wealth; since, when his treasuries were full, he stored the remainder under the ground, having dug labyrinthine passages. There were also caskets filled with precious stones—in addition to others, those white ones which are called by the specific name of pearls. But he did not use them, except for a few to adorn the ensigns of the purple, which he wore when he went out in procession, or when he gave audiences to ambassadors, or when he was celebrating some other public festival; the rest lay in the rooms as a useless burden. In times of war, his temperament was varied and multifaceted, but in times of tranquility and peace, he was more severe. He would dissimulate his anger, keeping it suppressed within himself, only to bring it forth when the opportunity arose to exact retribution upon those who had offended; and since he was of a stubborn nature, he did not easily change his decrees. Wherefore, if he was angry with anyone, he was not easily soothed. And let these things be said to characterize the morals of the emperor. Now let our narrative return to recount in order the events which took place under him.
IX. The ruler of Egypt, having broken his treaty with the Romans, destroyed the temple in Jerusalem, in which is the tomb of Christ, as well as the monasteries of that place.
But the emperor, constantly attacking Bulgaria, was laying it waste and burning it. And when Samuel, since he could not resist the Roman army in open battle, was endeavoring to block the entrances to his kingdom, and was occupying the mountain passes with forts and garrisons. The emperor, however, having returned, and having attempted to penetrate with a great force, although he was repulsed by the valor of the garrison troops, nevertheless did not desist from his undertaking, but persisted. He kept attempting the fort, sending one of the generals with his legion to see if he could open an entrance somewhere else by taking a roundabout way. He, having deceived the Bulgarians through the mountains and many difficulties, forced his way in and fell upon the garrison troops from the rear; they, being terrified by the sudden event, did not...
striving not for the defense of the fortification, but for the avoidance of total destruction. A There the Roman army, with no one to stop it, passed through the broken fortification, and pursuing the enemy, killed many and took more captive. Samuel having scarcely escaped, the Emperor gouged out the eyes of all the captives, who numbered about fifteen thousand, leaving to every hundred men one with a single eye to act as a guide for them, and in this way he ordered them to depart to their own leader. Seeing them, and unable to bear the catastrophe, Samuel became dizzy and collapsed to the ground, and having recovered only a little, he suffered a heart seizure and died (90). The leadership of the Bulgarians passed to his son Gabriel, who was also called Romanus. Not yet had he reigned a year when his cousin, Uladisthlavus Joannes, son of Aaron (for he too was known by two names), killed him.
The Emperor, having passed the fortification in the narrow pass, as has been said, overcame other strongholds and the Bulgarians within them, and arrived at Mosynopolis, where news of Samuel’s death was reported to him. Straightaway, therefore, he attacked Bulgaria, and captured both its cities and fortifications. Now the son of Samuel, Romanus—or Gabriel (for he had not yet been killed)—having sent to the Emperor, promised obedience. But after some time had passed, a minister of Joannes Uladisthlavus arrived, reporting the murder of Gabriel by his master, and many others among the illustrious Bulgarians flocked to the Emperor. Realizing, however, that Uladisthlavus was not planning according to his promises, the Emperor made an incursion into Bulgaria again, having carried out pillaging far and wide, and having captured the royal city of the Bulgarians, Achrida, and taken other castles through his generals, he returned to Constantinople. But finding war again brought against the Barbarians, he killed many of the Bulgarians and captured others, while taking some alive. The Bulgarian ruler, Joannes Uladisthlavus, meanwhile, launched an attack against Dyrrhachium, and while laying siege to it, he fell, having ruled the nation for two years and five months. Learning of this, the Emperor immediately set out, and as he reached Adrianople, certain illustrious Bulgarians approached him, surrendering to him Pernikos and thirty-five other fortifications; many other of these Barbarians also flocked to him. Maria, the wife of Joannes Uladisthlavus, also arrived shortly after, having been sent with letters to him by David, the Archbishop of Bulgaria, promising to surrender Bulgaria to him if she could obtain what she wished; she came with three sons and six daughters. There are also three other sons of hers, Prusianus with two brothers, who had fled into the mountains; finding the fortifications occupied, they were compelled to entrust themselves to the Emperor’s loyalty. Of these, he made Prusianus a *magistros* and the others patricians.
