With the whole of Bulgaria subdued, and some castles razed and others fortified and put under just garrisons, he set out for Athens to fulfill vows to the Mother of God; and after dedicating many precious gifts in her temple, he returned to the imperial city and led a triumph, adorned with a straight tiara, which the common people call a *tupha*.
a name I suppose derived from the pride it breeds, as it causes those who are bound by it to become arrogant. In this manner, he arrived at the Church of the Word of God, and therein offered his thanks to the Lord. The patriarch, who was present there, entreated him at great length to abolish the *allelengyon*, but he could not obtain his request, even though the emperor had promised he would do so if he were to overcome the Bulgarians. This Patriarch Sergius, having shepherded the Church for twenty years, departed this life, and Eustathius, who had presided over the priests of the palace church, was appointed prelate of the Church of Constantinople. C Upon the subjugation of Bulgaria, the nations of the Croatians also submitted to the Roman sovereignty, as did Sirmium. Then the emperor set out on an expedition against Abasgia, because its prince had violated their pacts. When he reached that place, Xiphias and Nicephorus, the son of Bardas Phocas, both patricians, revolted behind his back; but their rebellion was quickly extinguished, for Xiphias killed Phocas, and then was himself seized and led away in chains to the Queen of Cities. Having just rid himself of the anxieties surrounding this tyranny, the emperor clashed with the Abasgians, and a fierce battle ensued in which many fell on both sides and the struggle was evenly matched. Then, when the war broke out again, the Romans were victorious, and the prince of the Abasgians, George, fled into the deeper parts of Iberia. Shortly after, having sued for terms, he yielded to the emperor as much territory as the sovereign desired, providing his son Pancratius as a hostage. Having accomplished these things, the emperor returned. He had also been contemplating an expedition to Sicily, but his plan did not come to pass, for his life was already nearing its end.
Shortly before he passed away, the patriarch Eustathios died, having presided over the Church of the Orthodox for eight years 97. As the emperor was lying ill, Alexios, who at that time held the administration of the monastery of Stoudios, came to the emperor, bringing with him the sacred head of the Honorable Forerunner. The emperor immediately appointed him patriarch, and that very evening he departed this life, having lived and reigned for seventy-two years, and having ruled alone for fifty of those years; for the rest of the time he had reigned jointly with his father, then with Phokas, and after him with Tzimiskes 98.
X. A Since Basil has now died in his old age, his kinsman immediately seized the power—Constantine, I mean—who was but a little younger than him in age. Being of an indolent and relaxed disposition, or perhaps having arrived at a lifestyle given to ease and idleness (for his kinsman had never shared any imperial business with him), even after taking control of affairs he maintained the same standard of life. He handed over the administration of public affairs to others, and not to those who had already demonstrated their worth, but to men who were not only low-born, but not even of free condition, foreigners who were unable to speak correctly, but spoke exactly like barbarians. He gave himself over to luxuries, for he was conquered by his belly and by sensual pleasures, and he was mad about dice and the theaters, and indeed also about hunting. He was tall in the bulk of his body and very noble in strength, but cowardly in his soul’s disposition; hence, he did not decide to appease the assaults of the barbarians through warfare, but rather through gifts and dignities. He punished his subjects even on the basis of mere suspicion, and he easily lent an ear to calumnies; he was quick to anger, yet not so tenacious in his wrath as his brother, and was more placable, feeling remorse if he had treated someone badly. Many of those he punished he deprived of light, cutting out their eyes; he deemed this punishment milder than others, and used it more often, as it rendered those condemned to it incapable of action. As a young man, he took as his wife Helena 99, the daughter of Alypios, a man who at that time was among the most prominent, and from her he had three daughters.
Others among the ancient emperors 
Prepared for themselves foreign tombs. 
But I, Basil, born of the purple, 
Enter a tomb in the midst of the land of the Hebdomon, 
And I grant a Sabbath rest to the immeasurable toils 
Which I endured in battles, which I bore with constancy. 
For from the time God called me to be emperor, 
No one has seen my spear at rest, 
And of this the Scythians and Persians bear witness, 
Along with the Ishmaelite, the Abasgian, the Iberian, and the Arab.
Theoctistus, as has been said, was put to death. When the empress learned of what had happened, she rushed weeping from her chamber, railing at her son and her brother, and calling down a similar fate upon them both. Bardas, however, piling evil upon evil and preparing the way to carry out his own plans, persuaded them that the empress Theodora herself must be removed from the palace. When Theodora understood what was being plotted, she decided not to resist, so as to avoid the slaughter of their own kin. Having summoned the council, she said, "I myself am withdrawing from the palace. But so that the emperor cannot say that he found the imperial treasury empty of wealth, for that reason I shall make known to you the stores that have been laid up." She immediately commanded the treasurers to report the number of gold and silver coins. They affirmed that they held one thousand and ninety centenaria of gold and about three thousand centenaria of silver. "Besides these," the empress said, "much other wealth of every kind has been stored up." Having said this, she departed the palace.
III. Michael, having gained sole power, quickly squandered such vast wealth, wasting it on mimes, flatterers, and charioteers, and, as the saying goes, pouring it out by the cartload. When the treasuries were exhausted and the time approached to distribute money to those who held dignities, and he had no other source from which to provide it, he melted down the golden plane tree, the two lions, the two griffins, and the organs, all of which were fashioned of gold and adorned the palace, and were a cause of wonder to foreign nations; and having melted them down, he ordered coins to be struck from them. He did the same with the imperial robes, some of which were still intact...
He had children from her, and though he lost the mother who bore them to death, the daughters were raised in the palace. The eldest, Eudokia, having had her beauty marred by a pestilential disease, was consecrated to God by His will; the rest, however, remained with their father, who had become sole ruler, and he considered nothing regarding them that befitted a father. He was generous with his hand to those around him, yet he did not rightly apply this virtue, whence he fell into opposing vices. For to those around him, the barbarians were slaves whom he himself had acquired and castrated, and he possessed chamberlains and eunuchs, whom he honored and deemed worthy of the highest offices, bestowing upon them whole wagons of money, while to others he extended his hand very sparingly. Such, then, were the sentiments of this emperor. Let the discourse now relate the external events that occurred.
The Patzinaks (these are Scythians, as has been said before), having crossed the Ister, caused no small damage to the region of Bulgaria. But Constantine Diogenes, who governed Sirmium and was also called the Duke of Bulgaria, went out against them, routed them, and compelled them to remain quiet, having forced them back across the Ister. When Emperor Basil died (for he had granted the tribute-paying subjects a respite, as taxes were owed for two years), this man exacted the taxes of the preceding years and those for the three years of his own reign without mercy, and this at a time when a great drought occurred during the entire period of his reign, whence the poor were utterly destroyed.
The strategy commander of Samos defeated a fleet of Hagarenes that had sailed against the so-called Cyclades islands, capturing twelve ships together with their crews and scattering the rest. Now, the emperor, having already reached an advanced old age, and being afflicted by another disease in addition to the wasting of senility, was deliberating about who should succeed to the empire. He decided, therefore, to make the patrician Constantine Dalassenos his son-in-law by one of his daughters and his successor as emperor, and he sent men to summon him from the Armeniac province, where he was living. But before his arrival, Constantine changed his mind and chose as successor to the empire and as husband to his daughter a certain leading man, sprung from a noble lineage and elevated to the rank of prefect. But since the matter was difficult, because that illustrious man, Romanus Argyrus, had a wife, what did he contrive? He feigned a most grievous and sudden anger against the man and sent satellites to lead him to punishment, and to have his wife’s hair shorn and to transfer her to another mode of life. She, ignorant of the pretense, accepted the change with a ready mind, and having had her hair cut, she assumed black garments. But he, having been brought to the palace, married the other daughter of the emperor; for it was said that the third daughter refused to marry him because his previous wife had been unwillingly separated from him. Thus, having been declared Caesar, he married Zoe. Constantine, however, three days after the wedding, paid the debt of nature, being over seventy years old, having held the empire for three years, less one month.
XI. Romanus, subsequently, who was called Argyropolus, seized the scepter, and at once utterly extirpated the *allelengyon* tax; and for the chanters of God assigned to the Great Church, he increased the imperial distribution of gold coins usually granted from the treasury, knowing that the allowance provided to them was insufficient, since he had once been a great administrator (*oikonomos*) of the Church of the Word of God, before he had been permitted by the emperor to appoint the administrators of that same church. Those who were bound by public or private debt he liberated—the public ones by abolition, the private ones by paying them off. On behalf of his father-in-law, the Emperor Constantine, he distributed a great sum of money; he ransomed very many captives; he refreshed the bishops who had been driven to penury by the *allelengyon* tax; and he comforted those who had been afflicted by the late emperor, whether by bodily mutilation, confiscation of property, or other means.
But Prusianos the *magistros*, a Bulgarian, having been suspected of secretly plotting with Theodora, the sister of Augusta Zoe, and coveting the kingdom, was seized and held in custody, and afterwards blinded; Theodora was relegated to Petrion; and Constantine Diogenes, the husband of the emperor’s niece (the daughter of his sister), having been accused of meditating tyranny, was bound and cast into a tower.
However, George, the prince of Abasgia, having died, his widow sought a renewal of the treaty and a wife for her son. The emperor renewed the treaty and sent Helen, the child of his brother Basil, to Abasgia as a bride, having honored her bridegroom, Pancratius, with the title of Curopalates. The emperor also desired and prepared to launch an attack against Chalep, which is also called Berroia. For although the emperors Nicephorus and John had subjected many cities of Phoenicia and Syria to the Roman empire, Basil, who succeeded them, being occupied with the Bulgarian wars, had no leisure to secure these cities. Yet, as long as he lived, they did not dare to openly cast off the yoke of servitude. But when Constantine had obtained the scepter and was governing the empire in a relaxed manner, the descendants of Hagar, thinking this was a convenient time to carry out what they had been planning, attacked the garrisons of the cities and dealt with them. Indeed, the prince of Chalep, by frequent raids and pillaging, was harassing Antioch itself and other neighboring peoples of Syria under Roman rule; from whom the Duke of Antioch, while Constantine was still alive, had been shamefully defeated, and having lost most of his men, had barely escaped; to whom Romanus, having appointed a successor, went himself to war. But when, on the march, the envoys from Chalep met him with many gifts, seeking peace and the renewal of servitude, he gave them his daughter Zoe as a wife.
A. But the leaders of the cities themselves became masters.
The chief of Chalep, making continuous raids, pillaged Antioch itself and all the nations neighboring the land of the Syrians and subject to the Romans. The Duke of Antioch, having drawn up a line of battle against him while the Emperor Constantine was still living, was shamefully defeated; and having lost most of those with him, he was barely able to flee. Romanos, stripping him of his command, appointed another. But he, too, set out, and as he was leaving, envoys sent by the Chalepian approached with expensive gifts, asking to obtain pardon, to renew their servitude, and to pay the tributes that had happened to be late, and to pay these annually with gratitude in the future.
The Emperor, although many men distinguished in military strategy advised him against moving forward as unprofitable and counseled him to make a treaty, was not deterred from his impulse, but imagined it would be easy to overcome the Agarenes, thinking that by succeeding in this he would gain for himself a great and overweening reputation. He went into Syria and pitched his camp. But the Arabs, men of little equipment, daring riders on swift horses, and unarmored, lay in ambush on both sides of the camp; and if any went out to forage or fetch water, they would make an attack and slay or capture them, so that the soldiers and their horses were exhausted by thirst. Then, having become more accustomed to these incursions and condemning the Romans for cowardice, the Arabs suddenly rushed down from the heights, raised a barbarian shout, and, by scattering, created the impression of a great multitude, for they did not run in a dense line but loosely, and they invaded the camp without any order, terrified the entire army, and put it to flight. The Emperor himself would have been captured, abandoned without guards by his panicked bodyguard, had not someone lifted him onto a horse and urged him to flee, as he was almost dead with fear.
The Romans, therefore, fled in disorder, running as each chanced to go. But the Barbarians, stunned by the unexpected flight, did not pursue the fugitives, but ran against the camp, captured a few of the more prominent men, plundered the royal tent—which was full of wealth of every kind and royal luxuries—and, loading the rest of the baggage onto their horses, they returned.
XII. The Emperor was taken safely to Antioch by the efforts of his men, and from there he ran back to Constantinople, no longer showing the same disposition of soul, but changed for the worse. For in order to balance what had been lost, he became an exactor instead of an emperor, and a bitter accountant, demanding from the children the old debts of their fathers, and lighting fires against his subjects that the long passage of time had already extinguished or buried under ashes. From this, many were driven from their ancestral homes and lands, and, despoiled of their former wealth, were driven to utter poverty. Yet when these things were done, nothing was brought into the public treasury, but he consumed that money—and even that taken from the fisc—on a magnificent monastery and a temple built in honor of the Mother of God, while, as he says, he relentlessly demanded what was owed and expended what was due. From this point, conversing with base opinions and living with evil-minded thoughts, he changed in soul and turned his judgment toward actions that were not good.
He tore down, he built up, he changed squares into rounds. He supplied expenses for monks—not those suited for that way of life, but ones suited for delicate and voluptuous men—sanctifying to them, from public funds, entire provinces and cities, and those the richest and most fertile. But enough of these matters.
Amer, the son of the Chalepite, having entered the royal city, renewed the peace obtained from the emperor by means of many gifts. The empress Zoe, having set out for the Petrium, tonsured her sister Theodora as a nun. After the emperor had departed from the city, Constantine Diogenes, aspiring to the throne and intending to flee to Illyricum, was captured; and being interrogated, he threw himself down and chose to die rather than be forced by violence to betray his accomplices. At these times the Arabs invaded Mesopotamia, the Patzinaks invaded Bulgaria, and the Agarenes invaded the seacoast of Illyricum. The Roman fleet, engaging these alone, set fire to and burned most of their ships; those who had escaped from the battle perished in the Sicilian sea due to a storm.
Again, about a thousand ships set sail from Africa, and they ravaged many islands and certain maritime places. But Roman triremes, encountering a portion of them, destroyed many and captured five hundred alive, who were sent to the emperor in chains. Georgius Maniaces, the protospatharius, being commander of the cities along the Euphrates, captured Edessa, where he found the letter of our God and Savior Jesus Christ written by His own hand, which he sent to the emperor.
Furthermore, as famine pressed upon Cappadocia, Armenia, and Paphlagonia, and as locusts were infesting almost the whole East, many were compelled to leave their native land and seek new homes; the emperor, having relieved them with distributions of gold, persuaded them to return again to their own lands. When earthquakes occurred, the hospitals and buildings in Byzantium, which had long been assigned as a place of refuge for those whose bodies were afflicted by the sacred disease or infected with leprosy, were ruined; the emperor restored these, and also repaired the aqueduct of the city that had been fractured.
Variæ lectiones et notæ
(4) To the Mother of God, [named] the Peribleptos. Michael Psellus, lib. iii, Hist. ms., mentioning that he wished to adorn the Mother of God more beautifully than others, and [naming it] incorrectly, named it in a more human fashion; though the name is "Peribleptos" (well-respected), as "Peribleptos" truly is. To these, another addition had occurred, and the temple became a monastery.
(5) The Petrium. We have explained what the Petrium is in the Notes to the Alexiad, p. 249. It was, of course, a place outside the city towards the Ceratine gulf, in which various buildings and monasteries were constructed, in which he [the emperor] would immerse himself in the church of the holy protomartyr Stephen, called the Petrian. 
(6) Georgius Maniaces. I have both seen and admired this man; nature had bestowed upon him as much as is appropriate for one who is to lead an army. For he had risen to ten feet in height; and those looking at him gazed upward as if at a hill or the peak of a mountain.
XIII. The manner of his death
His life was now drawing to a close, and it must be related how he died. Having recently attained the empire, he promised himself long periods of both life and rule, although he was already sixty years old, and he dreamed that he would leave a successor to the empire sprung from his own loins; nor did he consider that the empress whom he had in marriage, being fifty years of age, was, with a worn-out womb, unsuited for receiving seed for conception. At the beginning he engaged with great intensity in sexual intercourse, using certain ointments and hanging small stones upon his wife that were thought to aid in conception; she bore all this, not even abhorring spells and incantations. But when he perceived that all was in vain and that he could not become master of his desire, he was not affected towards his wife as before; he visited her more rarely, being by nature quite sluggish and soft regarding intercourse, and then, too, his ability was dulled by age. Thus, when several years of his reign had passed, he turned away from the empress; and she, stung by both contempt for herself and by lust, was moved to hatred of her husband. There was, moreover, a eunuch who had been a servant to Romanos while he was still a private citizen, whose brother, Michael, had been endowed by nature with exquisite beauty; he, once commended to the emperor by his brother, had been admitted among the ministers of the bedchamber. The empress, captivated by love for him, burned in her soul, and her daily sight of his beauty fanned that flame. Although she had formerly hated that eunuch, she now began to call for him, and judged him worthy of more familiar conversation, and then began to inquire of him about his brother Michael. When this happened frequently, the sharp-witted man grasped the love at work and advised his brother that he should meet the empress, and if he were tempted by her, he should not be bashful, but should touch her, and kiss her, and embrace her. C Why say more? Their love advanced even to the point of coitus, and the matter at length reached such a state that the woman’s affection for Michael broke out openly, and it was whispered about not only in the palace but also in the public squares, unknown only to the emperor, who, while lying in the same bed with the empress, ordered Michael to rub his feet. Indeed, who would not believe that he also touched the feet of the woman? Thus the emperor became practically a pimp and roommate for both. But when his sister Pulcheria and others made the story known to him and warned him to be on his guard, he did nothing else than summon the one suspected by him to ask if he was loved by the empress? And when he bound him with an oath as he denied and disclaimed it, he thought the words of the others were calumny because the man had perjured himself. D As for Michael, it is said that an atrocious illness took hold of him on account of that perjury; for, as some relate, at certain intervals he was both alienated in mind, and he distorted his eyes, and was seized by tremors, and fell to the ground; then he came to himself.
This he underwent many times even in the presence of the emperor; but he, pitying Michael for his affliction, thought the reports even more false, and believed that he was neither in love with her nor was she with him, since he was in such a state. There are some, however, who say that the emperor was not ignorant of the love, but knowing that the empress was lustful and deranged in matters of desire, he endured her inclination toward Michael, feigning ignorance, so that she might not have affairs with many. While things were in such a state, the autocrat fell ill, and his face was swollen, and his breathing was continuous, and his appearance resembled a dead man, and the greater part of his head was bald, for the hairs had fallen off. It is said, indeed, that the man was worked upon by poisonous drugs. Being in such a condition, he went away to a bath in the palace to wash, and he went away neither supported by hands, nor expected to die immediately. When he had cleansed his body, he entered into the swimming pool there, where they say the whole drama was consummated: for they say that certain persons pressed his head down into the water for a long time, and that he was pulled out from there almost lifeless, and laid upon a bed. A rumor having arisen concerning this, the empress also approached, lamenting, as it were, for that misfortune, and having been assured of the death of the emperor, she departed immediately. He, having survived for a short time speechless, then bringing up something rather black through his mouth, gave up the ghost, having reigned for five years and a half; educated in literature, and having partaken of Greek learning, and not ignorant of the laws of the state, but he believed and boasted that he knew more than he truly did.
XIV.
Having met with this end of life, the empress was entirely intent on seating Michael on the throne of the empire, and although those around her, that is to say her paternal attendants, advised her to give delay to the matter and to deliberate, she did not tolerate it, being hurried toward the fulfillment of the task, and also by the eunuch John, the brother of Michael, who had whispered to her secretly that we shall all perish immediately if any delay is interposed in the business. Therefore, having immediately taken Michael, and having clothed him in royal attire, and having herself bound his head with the diadem, and having seated him on the royal chair, and sitting beside him herself, she exhorted all who were then present both to acclaim and to worship him. Some, however, have recorded that the sacred marriage ceremony also took place for them that very night, with the patriarch Alexius having been summoned to the palace, who found Romanus dead and Michael already conducting the auspices of the empire. Indeed, this deed having been bruited abroad outside as well, the whole city acknowledged the new emperor.
Partying, some did so to gratify him, while others rejoiced because they were freed from the hatred of the deceased. The empress, however, thinking that she had taken the best measures for herself and that, with all authority transferred to her, she would have Michael merely in the role of a minister, was deceived in her expectation. For at first, indeed, he feigned his former love, mollifying the woman with obsequiousness and doing those things which were grateful and pleasant to her. But when he could not prolong this simulation for very long, having changed his character, he began to hold her in suspicion; and fearing for himself, he removed from the palace her eunuchs—those familiar attendants of hers—and separating her usual handmaidens from her, he substituted others to attend to her. Furthermore, he sealed off the women’s apartments from her, placed a guard around them, and allowed no one to approach her unless they had explained to the prefect of the guards from where and for what purpose they had come. For he was afraid, having within himself the provocations for fear. Michael is said to have been driven to these things all the more by his brother, John the eunuch. For that man was sharp and keen, of an obscure disposition, mercurial toward those with whom he dealt, quick, tireless in the mass of his cares, and neither given to banquets nor to wine. Consequently, he never slackened in his zeal for managing public affairs. Such was the state of the empress. As for Michael, having taken up the care of common affairs, while his disease grew heavier—which some interpreted as the vengeance of the Divinity, having fallen upon him for his perjury, and which throttled the man just as Saul was once troubled, while others called it madness and derangement—the entire governance of the empire fell to the eunuch John, although he had long since put on the monastic habit, yet professing that manner of life only in his attire. But Constantine Dalassenus, the patrician, residing at home, and having heard about the new emperor, was indignant that the supreme power (*summa rerum*) had devolved upon such a man. Therefore, Michael, together with his own, was fiercely perturbed, fearing lest Dalassenus attempt to seize the kingdom. Accordingly, John dispatched a certain man to confirm to him with an oath that he would be in fear of nothing harmful, and to persuade the man to come and meet the emperor. Having come, he was kindly received by the emperor, increased in gifts and dignity, and ordered to reside in the city. But Nicetas, the emperor’s brother, who had been appointed as Duke of Antioch at Daphne, was not admitted into the city by the Antiochenes. For having killed a certain collector of public taxes, because they thought they had been treated too harshly by him, and fearing lest they pay the penalties for this crime, they had closed the gates against him; nor did they admit him until he had confirmed with an oath that that murder would not be to their detriment. However, in no way deterred by that oath, he killed many, and having bound certain distinguished citizens and sent them to the great city, he wrote to his brother John that he had been excluded by the Antiochenes because of their benevolence toward Dalassenus. Hence, Dalassenus was relegated to the island of Plate, and Constantine Ducas, his son-in-law, complaining of the injustice and not concealing the perjury, was shut up in a certain tower. Afterward, Dalassenus also was transferred from Plate and confined in a tower. At that time, the Agarenes captured Myra; the Patzinacae carried out raids throughout the Roman provinces. The Antiochene duke, Nicetas,
having ended his life, Constantine, who was also a brother of the emperor, succeeded him in Antioch, and his other brother was appointed protovestiarius. However, a fleet of Agarenes sailing out from Africa and Sicily was harassing the islands and the coastal regions. But a Roman fleet sailing against them sank many of their ships with their crews, and took many alive; some of these were sent to the emperor, while others were impaled along the shore. Pancratius, the prince of Abasgia—to whom the emperor Romanus had betrothed his own niece—dissolved the peace treaty with the Romans and reclaimed the strongholds and cities he had previously yielded to the Romans, perhaps to avenge the insult offered by the Romans to the emperor, the uncle of his wife. The Patzinacae often crossed the Ister and treated the provinces cruelly, putting all captives to death. The Arabs besieged Edessa and would have taken the city, had not Constantine, the duke of Antioch and brother of the emperor, brought them aid; the emperor rewarded his diligence by appointing him Domestic of the Eastern legions.
XV. When the prince who ruled Egypt, Amere, died, his widow, who was a Christian, sought to make peace with the Romans along with her son; and the emperor ratified a thirty-year truce with them. Two Saracen brothers, who were ruling over Sicily, had a falling out between themselves, and one of them fled to the Roman emperor, asking for an alliance. Accordingly, the patrician George Maniaces was sent to his aid, having been appointed strategos of Longobardia. But before Maniaces could reach Sicily, the rulers of that place were reconciled to one another, and, attempting to bar Maniaces from entering the island, they summoned reinforcements from Carthage. Yet, when battle was joined, the Roman army prevailed, and after a great slaughter of the Carthaginians was carried out, Maniaces first sacked thirteen cities; then, by advancing little by little, he restored the whole of Sicily to the Roman Empire. Edessa, however, was nearly taken by the Arabs through a ruse. For twelve of their princes brought five hundred camels to Edessa, each bearing two chests, and each of the chests contained an armed man. They claimed they were going to the emperor to present gifts, but if they had been admitted, they intended to draw their weapons by night and seize the city. The praetor honorably received the princes who entered to see him, while their servants remained outside the city with the camels. But when a certain Armenian beggar, who was skilled in the Arabic language, [was] near...
Variæ lectiones et notæ.
(10) Καὶ τοῖς Πατζινάκαις λεία γέγονε τῶν Μυσῶν. Notum proverbium, de quo in notis ad Alexiadem quædam attigimus. In Alexii Comneni Novella apud Balsamomen ad Nomocan. p. 140, 1 edit. Τὰ Φρυγῶν καὶ Μυσῶν, ἡ παροιμία.
. They wished, if they were permitted to bring the chests inside, to lead out the armed men within them by night and capture the city. When the leaders entered to the general of the city, he received them hospitably. Those serving them, however, were still outside the city with the camels. A certain Armenian beggar, who also knew the Arabs’ dialect, approached the Arabs bivouacking outside, intending to beg; he heard one asking from a chest where they were. He went into the city and reported this drama to the general. The latter left the Arab leaders feasting in the city, but went out himself with his attendants and, breaking open the chests, killed all the armed men inside them; then, returning again into the city, he struck down the remaining leaders with the sword. One, however, he mutilated by cutting off his hands, ears, and nose, so that he might report the disaster to those back home.
When the Carthaginian learned of the destruction of the troops he had sent into Sicily, he himself campaigned to the island with a larger force. Maniakes encamped against the Agarenes, and instructed the commander of the fleet, the patrician Stephen—the emperor’s brother-in-law (for he too had been sent along with the fleet)—to guard the coastline diligently, so that the Carthaginian, if defeated, might not be able to escape. When battle was joined, a multitude of Africans perished, almost greater than could be expressed by number, but their leader escaped from the battle, boarded a small boat, and, deceiving the sailors, escaped back home. Maniakes took this very hard and insulted the commander of the fleet. Being confident in his relationship to the emperor, however, Stephen took these insults with an ill temper and retorted something in turn; Maniakes, inflamed with anger, struck him. Stephen immediately wrote to his brother-in-law, the eunuch John, the so-called Orphanotrophos, slandering Maniakes as attempting a rebellion. For that reason, Maniakes was put in chains, led to the city, and committed to custody, with all authority transferred to Stephen.
Not long after, the island returned under the power of the Agarenes due to the commander’s inexperience, negligence, and especially his avarice; only Messana (which is also a city of Sicily) was preserved for the Romans through the valor of the prefect Catacalon Kekaumenos, who also slaughtered many thousands of the enemy. The emperor’s brother, the eunuch John, harboring a burning desire to manage the affairs of the Church, won over some of the high priests, who promised him they would remove Patriarch Alexius, on the grounds that he had not been appointed by common suffrage but by imperial authority. The latter, however, retorted: "If you assert that I have become patriarch against the canons, then let those also who were ordained by me be removed." This reply caused the other priests to desert the patriarch's accusers, and the Orphanotrophos was left without his desire.
Since he was most clever at devising schemes of gain and harassed his subjects with many unusual exactions, it happened that his sister Maria, the mother of Michael Cataphates who later became emperor, returned from Ephesus after fulfilling her vows and told him how miserably the subjects were being treated by those new exactions, and begged that the burden be lightened. The Orphanotrophos replied to her with a laugh, saying that she felt this way because she was a woman and did not know how much money the empire required. Zoe, the empress, is said to have laid a trap for him: she persuaded the physician, who was about to give him a purgative potion, with gifts and promises to offer poison instead of a salutary medicine; but he, having observed the trap, perished.
the danger. Thus were the public affairs administered by John the Orphanotrophos. His brothers, however, were no less harassing to everyone, being most unjust and greedy men; although the emperor hated them for this reason, the elder brother, the eunuch John, would not allow them to be restrained, sometimes offering excuses for them, at other times covering their crimes so that they might not come to the emperor's attention.
XVI. But as the disease grew, and fits of mental derangement began to afflict him frequently, the emperor neither appeared in public often, nor did he use royal processions, nor did he answer petitions while seated openly upon the imperial throne; instead, curtains were hung around him, and certain men appointed to watch him would stand around, and whenever they saw some sign of the mental derangement (for many things gave him advance warning of this), they would immediately draw the curtains around him, and, shielding the sufferer with these, would command the bystanders to withdraw. As the affliction seized him suddenly, so it also departed suddenly. In this state, Michael gradually refrained from the sight and approach of the Empress, whether out of shame that he repaid such great benefits with such a return; or because he was ashamed to be seen by her in the state of suffering into which he was so often and so suddenly snatched; or because his body’s condition had wasted away and he was now unfit for conjugal relations. D Some, indeed, say that having confessed the sins of adultery and the murder of the emperor to certain spiritual men, he received from them other commands, as well as the mandate to abstain from sensual pleasures; for this reason he also distributed much money, built sacred houses, and travelled to Thessalonica, beseeching the glorious martyr Demetrius that he might find some remedy for his condition. Furthermore, a dropsy was added to his illness. When his brother John saw him thus, fearing that if the emperor were suddenly to perish, the empire would revert to Zoe by hereditary right, and he himself along with his whole family would be wiped out, he persuaded the emperor with plausible arguments to name his nephew Michael, the son of his sister, Caesar and successor to the empire; and because Zoe was the heir to this, as has been said, he also dealt with her by guile. Thus they pretended that the noble matron was the mother of the ignoble boy. For both brothers spoke with her concerning this matter, [urging that] Michael [be named] her son
after the death of the former monarch; Basil, who reigned after him, recovered them, having found only three centenaria from the total amount previously numbered. Furthermore, the Emperor did not allow his mother, Theodora, to rest after she had departed from the palace; but at the instigation of Bardas, he sent and had both her and her sisters tonsured, and confined them in the house of Carianos, 46 and took away whatever property they possessed. Theodora, being wounded in soul by these things, withdrew to the monasteries there, and survived the calamity only a short time. Her daughters, the Emperor’s sisters, endured the evils into which they had been cast until Basil the Macedonian, having gained power over affairs, removed them to the monastery of their maternal grandmother, which is called Gastrion. After the Emperor's mother had departed from the palace, as has been said, the entire administration of the empire fell to Bardas, to whom his nephew conferred the dignity of Curopalates.
After these things, Michael, when his down was first beginning to grow, waged war against the Hagarenes, and undertook to besiege their city of Samosata, which is situated by the Euphrates. The Ishmaelites, having locked the gates of the city with bars, remained inside, giving the enemy the impression that they were afraid, because they were shielding themselves with their walls. This increased the Romans' boldness, so that they pitched their tents incautiously and wandered about fearlessly. But the Hagarenes, having feigned fear for three days, suddenly threw open the gates and, with a violent onset and great shouting, attacked the Roman camp. Not one of them withstood their attack; but all took to flight, and being hemmed in by the enemy, some were slain and others were captured. The Manichaeans, who were fighting on the side of the Ishmaelites, also sold many of the illustrious commanders who had been taken prisoner for a great sum of money. At that time, the Emperor himself narrowly escaped being taken, and would have been, had he not escaped the enemy by the speed of his horse. The camp was captured, and the imperial tent, and all the equipment for the Emperor’s service, and everything else was plundered. Michael then withdrew. But when he learned that the Hagarenes had again invaded the Roman provinces, he marched against them with a larger force. They, having traversed difficult and short paths, suddenly fell upon the Roman army and put it to flight, and the Emperor himself would have been captured had not Manuel, the Domestic of the Schools, saved him.
When the enemies did not cease from devastating the Roman territories, Petronas, the Emperor’s uncle, was sent against them with Roman forces. He, not daring to join battle and being averse to war, consulted a certain monk living on a mountain, who was renowned for his virtue and his foresight of future events, as to whether he ought to tempt the fortune of war. When the monk agreed and promised him victory, he engaged the enemy, and the army of the Hagarenes was defeated, and...
Various readings and notes
Publicly decreed to the well-deserving in the senate or elsewhere, were not in reality of solid gold, but of bronze overlaid with gold, as is in the old inscription 353, 4, "illustrious with gold," as in another 370, 3: which Isidore of Pelusium sufficiently intimates elsewhere, lib. I, epist. 395; lib. II, epist. 280, and lib. III, epist. 411. In the Anthology lib. IV, De Statua aurea Aureliani: "A golden Aurelian stands." Which was set up in bronze because of the law concerning adolescents, in the so-called places of Carianos. As Virgil, Eclog. VII: "Now we have made you a marble one for the time; but you, if fortune supplies the flock, be golden." So among Harpocration, regarding a certain Epicrates, that is, he had been gifted with a bronze statue. See the Alexandrian Chronicle, an. Theodosius the Great and an. Theodosius the Younger. See our Constantinople. Christ. 46 In the house of Carianos. Nicholas the Paphlagonian, in what are called the houses of Carianos. See our Constantinople, Christ.
She, neither daring to gainsay their wishes—being naturally compliant and easily led in her judgment—agreed. The senate and those who filled the roles of the imperial retinue (14) were summoned by proclamation to the divine temple in Blachernae; the empress was then brought forward, stepped inside the chancel rails of the altar, and there formally adopted Michael, embracing him as her son. The emperor immediately appointed him Caesar, and the assembly acclaimed him. John believed he had now accomplished everything and had secured the empire for his own family, but in that very act he brought about the utter ruin of himself and all his kin, as the narrative will show in due course.
XVII. As for the Bulgarians, who, as has been recounted, had been subjugated by the Emperor Basil and their own kingdom dismantled, they bore the yoke of slavery for a time. B Eventually, however, while they were pondering how to shake it off and refusing to obey orders, they lacked a leader and mastermind. A man named Dolianus, obscure of birth but full of guile and craftiness, having fled, it is said, from Byzantium, claimed he was the natural, though not legitimate, son of Aaron (who was the brother of Samuel, the prince of the Bulgarians), and was chosen king by the Barbarians. From that time on, the nation openly defected and turned to brigandage. A general was therefore sent with an army to check the incursions of the raiders. But because he treated his own soldiers badly, he provoked them against himself and would have perished had he not fled by night. The army under him, fearing punishment, defected and chose from among their own number a man named Tichomerus, of Bulgarian birth, as their leader, and named him King of Bulgaria. Thus the affairs of the Bulgarians were divided, with some flocking to Dolianus and others to Tichomerus. But Dolianus attacked his rival with treachery, summoning him to a partnership in the leadership and in the war against the Romans, and he agreed. When the two leaders had thus united and the multitude of the Bulgarians had gathered, Dolianus spoke to those assembled, saying that the Bulgarian nation would never be safe while governed by two leaders, and that if they wished for their own safety, they must remove one of them from the midst. "If you know me to be of the lineage of Samuel," he said, "remove Tichomerus. But if not, obey him and remove me." At this, a clamor was raised, and he was acclaimed king by all. Tichomerus was overwhelmed with stones. Having thus gained power, Dolianus captured Dyrrachium, attacked Greece, and took Nicopolis with the surrounding countryside, the citizens having voluntarily surrendered because they could no longer endure the insatiable rapacity of the Orphanotrophos; and, as the proverb has it, he made the Roman territories a prey for the Mysians. The emperor, upon being notified of the revolt, was incited to capture its author, declaring it unjust that a man who had contributed nothing to the empire should suffer any part of it to be seized by anyone. But because his [the emperor's] own safety was now despaired of.
A (for the dropsy had already mastered him, and his body was swollen), his kinsmen restrained him from the campaign with their entreaties, and the leading men of the senate did not approve of his plan. But he, strengthened by zeal and opposing the weakness of his body with the force of his spirit, set out against the rebels, and the event was a thing of wonder. For while he was distressed by his disease at night, and was not expected to rise on the morrow, at the first light of day he was seen on horseback, and he preceded the army. As he had arrived in the borders of the Bulgarians, he prepared for a pitched battle. Thus, as the battalions were engaged with one another, a certain event quickly granted the victory to the emperor without any effort. For the son of that Aaron, the patrician Alusianus, who was among the Romans and had offended the sovereign in some way, had been condemned to remain at home and neither to frequent the royal palace nor to enter Byzantium at all, unless he were granted permission to enter. He was, therefore, despondent on account of these things; but when he learned of the defection of the nation and the election of Dolianus to rule over it, he escaped from his house and changed his attire, putting on the garb of an Armenian and pretending to be a servant of Theodorocanus. Making his way to the camp, he reached the Bulgarians. But he did not immediately reveal himself; while conversing with some, he reminded them of Aaron, and said: "If any of his children were present, would the legitimate son seem to you preferable for the leadership of the nation to the bastard?" When all would have prayed for it to be ruled by a legitimate son of Aaron, and for their kingdom to be restored again to a man of undisputed standing who belonged clearly to the ancient royal lineage, he disclosed the secret to one of those to whom the family of Aaron was better known. He, having searched his features with his eyes more curiously, and then also knowing of a certain sign present on him that was indubitable, sought to see this as well. That sign was on his right elbow, a certain black spot, surrounded by thick hair. When he had seen this, with every doubt now removed, he prostrated himself, embraced the man's feet, and taught the others also that a man of royal blood was present. Many, therefore, torn away from that ambiguous leader, betook themselves to the legitimate one. But as they judged that the kingdom could not possibly stand while the empire was divided and the princes were in disagreement with one another, they made a pact between themselves to administer the state by common counsel, although each was nonetheless suspicious of and plotting against the other. In truth, Alusianus, being readier for treachery, forestalled the trickery of Dolianus. For having prepared a banquet and invited his colleague, he seized the wretched man during their drinking and blinded him. Having now gained sole possession of the rule of the Scythian nation, he ordered it to be secretly announced to the emperor that he would surrender to him, if he were received mercifully, and—oh, the judgments of God!—the emperor, even while ignoring all that is wont to enervate the body, neither counting the double exhaustion of his frame, nor the length of the road, nor the hopelessness of the war, nor anything else, sets out from the city and campaigns against the nation that has revolted; he is arrayed for battle, and rides on his horse a corpse.
Having seen this also, and no longer being able to hesitate, he throws himself down before the man, and embraces his feet; he also instructs the others who had come to them in the imperial line. And many, breaking away from the doubters, attached themselves to the true heir, and the authority was divided. But as the kingdom would not entirely be secured for them if the leaders were at odds with one another, they act in common and make a treaty; yet they also suspected and laid traps for one another. But Alousianos was found to be sharper regarding the plot, and he forestalled the treachery of Dolianos. Having prepared a drinking party, and having taken his co-ruler as a guest, he attacks him as they are drinking, and gouges out the wretch’s eyes; and from then on, the Scythian matter subsides into a single authority. Then, through secret messages, he signifies to the emperor that he wishes to go over to his side, if he might receive his favor, and will not lack commensurate rewards. He accepted the petition and promised to reward him worthily. And he immediately came, and was honored with the rank of magister. The emperor at once goes against the nation; and it, being leaderless and scattered, was not with difficulty overcome, and again submitted to the Roman government, and the emperor returned to Byzantium, and led a triumph, having brought many captives into it, and Dolianos himself, deprived of his sight.
Now the emperor was in poor health, and already he approached the dissolution of his physical frame. He therefore disdains the authority, and departs from the palaces, and goes to the monastery which he himself had established before the walls of the city, which he named after the call of the wonder-working Anargyroi. Having come there, he puts off the purple and all other signs of the imperial office, and is shorn of his hair, and undergoes the sacred rite, and puts on the cloak of humility in Christ, and girds himself with the divine skin, the symbol of voluntary dying, as one already dead against his will. When, therefore, the fact was reported to the empress, she goes out on foot from the palaces, and arrived at that place. But he bars the gates to her, and denies her entrance to him, I do not know if out of shame for what he had shown toward her, or because he was entirely given over to repentance, and did not wish to come into the memory of his old disposition toward her. She, then, not having met with Michael, returned. He, having survived his change of life for a short time, transferred his life to the eternal dwellings, until his final breath lamenting the crime committed by him against the emperor Romanos. He had been a kind and gentle emperor, as far as it depended on him, but his brothers sprinkled a stain of disgrace upon his goodness. He is said by some to have held the Roman scepter for seven years, and by others for something more.
XVIII. The Roman Empire comes to the Caesar, through the efforts of his maternal uncle.
For Michael...
annaling. The emperor had indeed named him Caesar, as we have written above, but as if repenting of the deed, he did not maintain his goodwill toward him. Whence his lodging was fixed outside the city, and he was not permitted to approach the emperor unless he were summoned. But when the emperor had departed from the palace, as has been said, his brothers forged letters in the emperor's name, by which access to the palace was permitted to him.
When he had come, the Orphanotrophus advised that he should undertake no business without the consent of the empress. Therefore they go to her, and with the Caesar prostrated at her feet, they remind her of the adoption, and, pledging their faith, they swear most sacredly that Michael would be emperor in name only, but that all authority should be hers, and that she might use them just as if they were slaves bought with copper and scales [i.e., absolute property]. With these things they deluded and charmed her—a woman who was otherwise easily led—so that they brought her to their own will. And the scepter is placed in Michael’s hand, and he is crowned with the imperial diadem, being unworthy of it, not so much on account of his base and obscure lineage (for his father was accustomed to caulk ships), as for the depravity of his character. He was, in truth, of a deceitful disposition, of an iniquitous and ungrateful spirit, respecting neither the laws of kinship nor friendship; his tongue, moreover, was in disagreement and discord with the sentiments of his mind, accustomed to hide one thing and say another. Added to these things was a singular envy. In difficult times he was servile and ignoble, irascible and fickle: yet in such a way that he inclined more easily from benevolence to malevolence for any reason, than from anger to clemency. Although he was such, he ascended to the helm of the empire, to the ruin and destruction of his uncle the Orphanotrophus, and of his whole family. Nor for long did he maintain his feigned and simulated manners, but after a very few days he called his uncle Despot, deigning to have him sit with him, and he referred all things to him. Then gradually he withdrew some of the honor: afterwards in most affairs he rendered his authority as nothing, and did now one thing and now another, which would vex him. Although he condemned the zeal he had shown for his nephew, nevertheless he did not know what to do at the present: but secretly, pondering I know not what in his mind, he approached the emperor more rarely. For a controversy arose between him and his brother Constantine, to whom alone of all his kin the emperor adhered and had granted the title of Nobbelissimos; since the emperor had not punished him, on account of John having been upbraided more harshly in his presence: John, exacerbated by the pain of the insult, withdrew further from the city. Having learned this, he drew a great part of the senate to himself, not because he pursued him with such goodwill— D
A —or to gather his benevolence. The emperor, however, fearing the gathering of many to him, wrote him a letter reproaching him for his pride and demanding that he come to him. But when he went, not having awaited the arrival of his uncle, he had betaken himself to the theater for the Circensian games. Finding him not there, and still burning with rage at the insult, the Orphanotrophus departed. Then indeed the emperor broke out into open enmity; he sent a ship and ordered him to appear, that he might give an account of his pride; and to the ship sailing toward the palace, from a higher location, giving the signal known to those by whom he was carried, he forbade entry and immediately, having dispatched a trireme, banished the man: in this way he rewarded him whose beneficiary he had been first in being designated Caesar, and later even emperor. Having disposed of him, he went on to his kinsmen, and men—not only by the natural meaning of the term, but also by that which signifies those who have married and reached the age of manhood, and have even become fathers of children—he deprived of their procreative organs, and practiced emasculation.
XIX. When he had thus triumphed over his own blood, thinking one more Herculean labor remained for him, namely the expulsion of the empress, he prepared for this also, by the persuasion and impulse of his uncle Constantine. The most ignoble son, therefore, spread rumors against his most noble mother, and concocted crimes, and called her innocent as she was a poisoner, and complained that she plotted against his life. And why should I go through the details? He thrust her out of the palace, and confined her to the island called Principis; and finally he sheared his benefactress, and made her change her attire, and instead of the purple that became her by ancestry, he put a coarse tunic upon her. When he had done this, as if all matters were settled, he was joyous and insolent, and surrendered himself to games and delights. But justice, not slumbering, quickly overtook the wicked and ungrateful man. For when that deed was made known in the city, all were to be seen sad and grief-stricken, and affected no differently than if it had been their own calamity. At length they even began to cast insults at the tyrant. The crowds lamented for the woman, and called her empress. Finally, the whole city erupted into open sedition. And in order to avenge his audacity, disregarding their own lives, arming their hands with whatever was at hand, they ran to the palace. Him, indeed, the beginning of the people's agitation did not much move, but when he saw the populace raging, and—
forming into companies, and the people streaming in, while he perceived that those around him were of doubtful mind (for they too had come to loathe the crime committed against the empress), he became fearful and anxious. Yet the Nobilissimus somewhat relieved his anxiety. For having learned of the sedition, he armed his many retainers (for he kept a great number) and, passing through the midst of the forum, went into the palace; and immediately they sent to bring back the empress, and opposed the rioters with certain men who, from hidden places, shot missiles at them and [hurled] stones from slings, whereby not a few were killed. And having already brought back the empress, they placed her on a high platform in the theater, to show her to the rioters, so that, as they supposed, their fury might be calmed by the recall of their own mistress. But this only inflamed their anger all the more and kindled their wrath. For when they saw her in a changed habit, they were goaded further into war, and in their hatred of the one who was exercising tyrannical malignity, they abandoned the empress Zoe and turned toward her sister, Theodora, who, as the narrative has already explained, had also been compelled by her sister to adopt the monastic habit. Having taken with them one of her father’s servants, they arrived at her residence, brought her out, and, surrounding her as if with a shield-wall, led her to the great temple of God the Word, the Sophia; and there, by the consent of all—the senate, the people, and the clergy themselves—Theodora was proclaimed Augusta and empress. This extinguished every hope of Michael and those around him, and fearing for his life, he departed from the palace, boarded a ship with the Nobilissimus, and went to the famous monastery of Stoudios, having laid aside the imperial insignia and assumed the monastic habit. When this became known to the people, they could not contain their joy and ran toward him. As the day was now drawing to a close, those around Theodora, fearing lest the empress Zoe might again recall the one who had cast her out, so that the sister would not be forced to share the empire with her, sent men to gouge out the eyes of both. And so they were dragged out from the very sanctuary of the monastery of Stoudios and led away. The multitude that had flocked around them mocked and insulted them, and composing songs, chanted them in a tragic manner, until, in the place called Sigma (for that is the name of this place), their eyes were gouged out, Constantine bearing the calamity bravely, while Michael wailed and lamented. When this was done, the ministers of the punishment returned to Theodora, who was still staying in the church. But it did not please Zoe, who had returned to the palace—as she was most ambitious—to admit her sister to a share of power. However, the senate, revering both, persuaded the elder by birth to admit her sister as a colleague, regarding the one as their empress of long standing, and the other as one lately designated and the cause of the overthrow of the tyranny. Theodora, therefore, entered the palace and shared power equally with Zoe; the blinded ones were punished with exile, their rule having been terminated in four months and five days; would that they had lost it sooner. D
XX. The Roman Empire devolved upon the gynaeceum.
Since the Roman authority had descended to the gynaeceum, no one initiated any revolution against these women; rather, all submitted to them, both the leaders of the senate and the most prominent men of the military establishment. Both women sat upon the tribunal, all royal ceremonies were performed before them, while the customary guard was deployed, and the primary senators and magistrates stood around them in a state of veneration. Furthermore, judicial inquiries, the resolution of public disputes, the reception of embassies, elections, and all other business pertaining to the empire took place before them. Yet whether it was out of fear that the sister [Theodora] alone might control the state (for it was whispered among some that the authority fit her better, since she had been the cause of the tyrant's removal), or whether Zoe was driven by a desire for a husband and the pleasures of conjugal union, she deliberated on summoning an emperor and husband to the palace. Constantine Dalassenus was therefore brought, as if summoned for some other reason. But because he spoke quite harshly, and his character appeared stubborn and intractable, he was rejected.
Then she inclined toward another man, who was not distinguished by nobility, by high dignities, or by having held illustrious magistracies, but who was worthy of the empire on account of his appearance—a man whose soul was not given over to luxury and who could entice [her] into love. The empress set full sail toward him, but her hopes were interrupted by a disease that suddenly snatched the man from life. His wife was not free from suspicion, as one who had destroyed him with a poisonous concoction out of jealousy, in order that she might not be deprived of her husband while he was still alive and see another lying beside him. Thus, he was cheated of his hopes, and the empress of her wish.
However, her calculations inclined toward Constantine Monomachos, a man of noble birth and illustrious standing, endowed with excellent form, who, after his first wife had died—she was not, however, the sister of the emperor Romanus—had obtained nothing magnificent from him. Nevertheless, since he was moving about the court on account of his affinity, he had become dear and familiar to the empress, and she—being of a liberal hand and royal spirit—had bestowed upon him gifts of many talents. Whence, the suspicious and the malevolent seized an opportunity to speak ill of them; and Michael, the successor of Romanus, because he also had heard these things, seized power and relegated the man to Lesbos on certain trumped-up charges, when the only true cause was jealousy. In that exile he remained during the entire time of Michael’s reign, nor was he...
treated with more kindness by the second Michael. But when the power returned to Zoe, the man was released from exile and was brought to the queen of cities; and when he had drawn near, he was told to stop, and a messenger sent from the palace, meeting him by the temple of the archstrategos at Damocrania, invested him with an imperial robe, and having set out from there with an imperial escort, he entered the imperial city. The marriage ceremony for the sovereigns is performed by one of the priests; for Patriarch Alexios, because of the third marriage 27, was cautious about officiating the ceremony of the union for them himself. But on the following day he performed the ceremony of the coronation, and Monomachos was crowned and the imperial scepter was delivered to him.
XXI. From that point, the power of the sisters, which had lasted for a third month, came to an end, and the administration of public affairs passed to Monomachos. And he immediately, without reason or any prudent management, bestowed neither honors for positions of rank, nor disbursements of money; but he dispensed honors indiscriminately, and granted them to those to whom they did not belong, and it occurred that the imperial treasuries were emptied in a single day, and he thought nothing of it. To those, however, whom he had offended while he was a private citizen, he forgave all grievances, and held a grudge against no one. He gave himself over to pleasures and luxuries, partly in an attempt to cast from his mind the wretchedness brought on by the storm of calamities, having just arrived at what he deemed a calm port of imperial power, and partly to gratify the empresses 28 and to make himself agreeable, for they were of a fickle temperament. He devoted attention to learning, or rather to men renowned for learning, although he himself had barely, as they say, tasted of doctrine with the very tips of his lips. While this was the state of affairs at the palace, the situation outside did not fare well for the Romans. For a certain Scythian man from Byzantium, a fugitive named Boisthlavos, having gathered a band, settled in the Illyrian mountains, inhabiting them like a wild beast, and harassed with plundering the peoples adjacent to those mountains who were under Roman rule, such as the Triballi, the Serbs, and other peoples of that kind. Upon this news, the emperor wrote to the prefect of Dyrrachium that he should join battle with Stephanos—for Boisthlavos also had that name—who having engaged in battle rashly (for he lacked the emperor’s skill in the art of war)
became the cause of a great calamity for those serving with him; for he himself barely escaped with a few, while the rest of the entire army was destroyed there, being unable to use their hands or avenge themselves upon their killers. Such were the events in Illyricum.
But let our account now return to the private affairs of Monomachos. After his second wife had died, he did not consider contracting a third marriage, but he began [to have] intimacy with the cousin of his former wife, a widow, at first secretly, but later without concealment. She was in the bloom of youth, exceptionally beautiful, and of noble lineage, for she was a scion of the Skleros family. They were so bound together and so inflamed with such ardor of love that, unless they were together, they deemed life more bitter than death. Therefore, when he was sentenced to exile—as mentioned above—the woman herself voluntarily left everything behind and followed him, so that she might see the one who loved her and whom she loved in return, and not be exiled away from him. Thus, she accompanied the man, assuaged his misfortune with every service as best she could, and relieved his calamity to the extent of her ability, providing for his expenses from her own means so that he would not be pressed by poverty in addition to his other distresses. For she too relied upon the vain hope of principate at that time.
But when he was raised to the pinnacle of the empire, the emperor did not forget that woman in his prosperity; rather, he opened discussions with the empress regarding her, requesting that the woman be recalled and that she might receive some degree of comfort. She, however, did not refuse; for time had carried away her jealousy, as she was now advanced in years, and the temptations that had befallen her had made her more moderate. Sclerena was therefore brought back from Mitylene, and at first, no glorious dwelling or delicate ministry was prepared for her, but as time went on, an imperial bodyguard was assigned to her, and her house was transformed into a palace. For Monomachos pretended that he was building a private house in the place where she lived, so that, under the guise of inspecting the construction, he might visit her frequently. For a time, he hid his love—as it were—behind a veil, and seemed to blush at what was happening. Then, however, he cast aside both shame and dissimulation, and treated the woman not as a concubine or a half-wife, but plainly as a spouse. Thus, imperial treasures flowed toward her like a river, and veins of gold were opened to her, the emperor associating with her openly; and, thinking it intolerable for himself if he did not enjoy her constant company, he communicated the matter first with the empress and, having obtained her consent, brought Sclerena into the palace. Indeed, it is said that a pact of friendship was struck between the women at the emperor's suggestion, and the woman attained the honor of an Augusta, in both dignity and title, being elevated to the first rank and called "Lady." The empress took all this neither in anger nor with vexation. But since her husband lived between herself and the Augusta, the empress was not accustomed to meet with him until she had carefully inquired whether his beloved was with him—a matter that he…
had, however, made secure. But deeming it a terrible thing if he did not live with her and dwell with her continuously, he brought the woman from there into the palace, having first deliberated about this with the empress and persuaded her not to be displeased at what was happening. It is even said that oaths of friendship were performed between the two women at the emperor’s request. The woman was honored as *sebaste*, that dignity and title having been first conferred upon her, and she was called *despoina*. The empress did not grow angry at these things, nor was she vexed, but when the emperor resided in the middle, the empress and the *sebaste* dwelt on either side alternately. And the Augusta never approached the emperor without having verified that he was separated from his beloved; but, having remained thus, and having been raised to such great prosperity, and cherishing greater hopes within herself, she was suddenly seized by sickness and died, leaving great grief to the emperor.
XXII. And she having departed, civil war came upon the emperor. For George Maniakes C (31), a man most brave in soul and most noble in strength of hand, and most skilled in the command of armies, having been sent to Italy when those women were in charge of the affairs of the empire, was warring against those who were encroaching upon it, and those who had made some parts of it their own, setting out to recover these for the Roman empire, and he stood against the adversaries of the Romans. But when the axis of the empire had rolled over to Monomachus, Romanos Skleros (for this man was a brother of the emperor’s beloved)—who had great power through his kinswoman, and had been honored with the rank of *magistros* and *protostrator*—since he lived in the house of Maniakes in the theme of the Anatolikon, he managed the man’s affairs poorly, harboring a grudge because he had been defeated by him in a previous dispute. It is also said that he had violated the bed of Maniakes. These things, being reported to the man, stirred him to anger and caused him great distress; then he was even deprived of his command, and that was brought about by Romanos. Considering, therefore, that if he should return to Constantinople, the one who ruled would not be well-disposed toward him because of Skleros, he attempted a tyranny. And many of the military, as if to the most eminent of generals and a martial man, attached themselves to him. The latter, having embarked his army on ships from Italy to the continent opposite, arrived. This threw the emperor into turmoil, and he inscribed a letter to Maniakes, absolving him of all fear if he would lay down his arms, and promising better things; but as he had once cast the die, he no longer let go of his arms, and the emperor also campaigned against him. Yet the emperor did not set over the army any of the noble men, or those experienced in generalship, fearing that such a one might himself dare...
And the emperor would have perished as well, if the Magister Manuel, who was Domestic of the Schools, had not saved him. The enemy did not cease from harming the Roman lands. Petronas, the emperor's uncle, is therefore sent against them with Roman forces to oppose them. He, however, did not have the courage for the engagement, but dreaded the war. Having learned, therefore, that a certain monk who practiced asceticism on a mountain was famed for his virtue and could foresee future events, he goes to him and asks if he should undertake the war. The monk permits it and promises him victory. Petronas went off and attacked the enemy; the army of the Agarenes was defeated, and their commander, Amer, fell. His son, who was away from the battle (for he happened to have gone off on a plundering expedition), upon learning of the defeat and the destruction of his father, took to flight, but he too was captured in his flight and delivered to the general. It is said that before this battle, Amer asked one of the captives what the place where they were encamped was called, and what the stream flowing by it was called, and by what name that entire region was known. When the man replied that the region was called Lalacaon, the place of the rampart Ptosantes, and the river Gyres, Amer, having taken an omen from these names, said that these words portended an inauspicious outcome for the war. For he interpreted Lalacaon as the "affliction of the people," Ptosantes as "the fall," and from the name of the river he divined that his men were to be surrounded by the enemy. Petronas, having won the victory, returned in triumph to Byzantium, was received with honor, and was appointed Domestic of the Schools. For the Magister Manuel had already died; and not long afterward, he himself paid the debt of nature.
IV. Bardas, having cast all these men aside, administered all affairs according to his own will. For Michael cared for nothing, but occupied himself only with theaters and chariot races; and it was not others who held the reins, but the Emperor Augustus himself who drove the chariot, competed, and instead of the imperial robe, wore the garb of a charioteer. Nor did the evil stop there, but he even forced men outstanding in rank and honors to stand, some with him and some against him, in the horse races and to drive chariots. Once, therefore, when he himself was wearing the blue color, and the Logothete of the Course the green, and other distinguished men were clothed in other colors, and they were already about to mount the chariots and race, letters were handed to the Logothete of the Course by someone who had just arrived, reporting that the Saracens had pitched camp at the Melangeia—these are what are now less elegantly called Malagina. The logothete, therefore, with a gloomy look upon receiving the message, approached the emperor, explained the matter, and showed the letters. But he, looking at him with an angry and savage expression, said, "While I am in such a contest, and as I am hastening to drive the one in the middle to the left, you, fool, speak to me of a Saracen incursion?" Such was the illustrious emperor; and thus, regarding public affairs and the empire
to seize the throne. A Nevertheless, while he was riding along the battle lines, dispersing the phalanxes, and those whom he addressed immediately broke ranks and fled, he received a lethal wound. A great flow of blood caused a darkness to descend upon his eyes; the reins slipped from his hand, he slid from his saddle, and died where he fell, not far from the field where the battle was engaged. The imperial troops, however, did not dare to attack him until his soldiers, learning of their leader’s death, were terrified and scattered. Only then did the wretched men bring the severed head to their own commander, who immediately dispatched it to the Emperor. He hung it high in the theater, and when the eunuch, who had seemed to have brought the war to a successful conclusion, returned with the army, a triumph was celebrated. The Emperor presided with great majesty in the porch of the Savior’s temple in the Chalkē, which faces the forum, sitting between the empresses. Such was the end of Maniakes.
But another tyranny arose, fostered by Theophilos Erotikos. Having learned of the end of Michael II — who was the grandson of the first [Michael] — and aware that the empire was entrusted to women, he won over the Cypriots (for he was at that time their governor) and attempted a revolt. However, Monomachos did not need much time to put him down. He sent a fleet, by which he captured Erotikos and easily brought the island back into servitude.
Upon the death of Patriarch Alexios, who had held that see in peace for eighteen years, Michael, surnamed Keroularios, was appointed in his place. Monomachos, in truth, took twenty-five centenaries of gold from the monastery of the Patriarch Alexios, which had been stored there by that patriarch. Furthermore, the eunuch John, who had been Orphanotrophos and was the own brother of the late Emperor Michael the Paphlagonian, was transferred to Mytilene during the reign of Monomachos. There he was blinded, whether by the order of Theodora without the emperor’s knowledge, or by the mandate of the emperor himself, who was angry with him on account of his undeserved and lengthy exile. He survived the blinding for only a few days before he ended his life.
XXIII. But another tyranny, more terrible than the former, arose against Monomachos, with Leo Tornikios as its author, who was his kinsman through his mother. He lived in Orestiades (for thus was the city of the emperor of old called, after Orestes, the son of Agamemnon), and he held sway over the Macedonians who obeyed his word...
as if he were someone superior. For the man was not inferior in appearance nor in spirit. He had, moreover, something else by which he attracted the majority to himself. For rumor prophesied about him—as it is vainly said about others—that he would one day be master of the Roman scepters. The emperor was not only not disposed toward him as a kinsman, but he even persecuted the man with hatred. Euprepia, the sister of the ruler, however, was friendly to him and welcomed him; she was a noble matron and of most steadfast character, having been raised to the brilliance of fortune and to a surplus of wealth. She was not devoted to her brother, and he was cautious of her because of the surplus of her prudence. She, receiving nothing of consequence from him, neither approached him frequently, and when she did come, she behaved toward him haughtily, reproving and rebuking him, and regarding his consequent anger with contemptuous silence. The emperor, therefore, bearing heavily and with annoyance this sincere affection of his sister toward Tornikios, in order to separate them by a great distance, placed Tornikios over the Iberians of the Roman domain, veiling this exile with a specious title. When, therefore, he departed, and rumor did not cease to follow him with the hope of great fortune, certain men seized upon this as a handle to calumniate him to the emperor.
