Moved by their words, the emperor shears the hair of Tornikios, thinking that he would shave away his hope along with it, and he clothes him in a black cloak, thinking thereby to cast shadows upon the splendor of the fortune he expected. And when he returned, he not only did not pity him, but he even sent him away with a mocking laugh. But the Macedonians, being, as was said, already devoted to him and cherishing good hopes regarding him, took him by night from the queen of cities and led him to their own metropolis, Adrianopolis; and either by the zeal of those attached to him, most of the armies quickly gathered there, or else by their hatred toward the emperor. For, for certain reasons, he had not treated the soldiers well, but rather nurtured suspicions against them, and they were, on account of these things, stirred up to hatred against him. When, therefore, they had gathered, they immediately deemed him worthy of royal acclamation and proclamation. Together with them, he immediately busied himself with the task, and suddenly swarms upon the imperial city. Many, both soldiers and citizens, flocked to him as he approached, and he was buoyed by hopes, as if no one would stand against him, but rather that the citizens would easily open to him the gates of the city, both because the Eastern armies were preoccupied with wars there, and because...
the people of the city were angry at the Emperor, as if he were not acting according to their wishes. Buoyed by such hopes, he encamped before the city, intending to lay siege to it if it did not receive him most readily. At dawn, therefore, having drawn up his lines and arrived near the city fortifications about the Blachernae, he demanded that those inside open the gates to him, encouraging them toward this action with brilliant promises. When they did not even show him regard, but rather mocked him and jeered at him, he attempted an assault. But the Emperor also drew up his line, with a few soldiers and the town rabble and some other men, servants of certain of the senators, not even amounting in all to a full thousand; and having stationed these before the city fortifications face-to-face with the tyrant’s men, he thought that by this means he would strike terror into his adversary. The Emperor himself sat in royal fashion in one of the imperial chambers that projected beyond the others and looked out over the plain before the city, so that he might see his enemies and be seen by them. Now the Macedonians, accustomed to ribaldry, on seeing the Emperor, some openly abused him, while others, forming choruses and improvising comic songs, danced and sang these things, beating the earth with their feet. Then, when a portion of the enemy advanced upon those stationed outside the wall, some rushed toward the gates, while others fell into the moat and perished; and such fear overcame everyone that even those standing upon the walls threw themselves down and fled, and those guarding the city entrances became useless at once, not even bolting the gates. If, therefore, that war had possessed a man who knew how to use victory,1 nothing would have prevented Tornicius from gaining entry to the city and bringing his undertaking to an end. But this, since it had not been ordained by the Providence above, escaped the tyrant. Yet the Emperor was near danger; for one of the enemies aimed at him and let fly an arrow; but it missed the Emperor and struck a youth among the chamber servants. However, the wound from the arrow was not fatal to him either. But those around the Emperor, being afraid, immediately shifted positions, and he himself was moved elsewhere with his seat. C In the manner that has been told, the good fortune escaped Tornicius, and affairs turned against him. For the city had again been secured by guards, and when the tyrant attacked again at dawn and was repulsed, he almost perished. For when stones weighing a talent were being hurled from an engine at the enemy, one was directed at the tyrant himself; it missed him, but it struck fear into him and those with him, and having broken off the engagement, they returned to their own camp, and no longer attempted the siege. The tyrant waited there for a few days, but when he saw that some of his own men were deserting to the Emperor, and others were being scattered and fleeing, he departed from his blockade of the city and went away, thinking he would easily take the Thracian fortresses, but he was repulsed. Meanwhile, the Eastern forces were recalled and arrived, and were sent against the tyrant. When his allies learned of this defection, fearing for themselves, and since the commander of the Eastern forces, having encamped near them, had persuaded some by conversation and others by letters to defect to the Emperor, almost everyone deserted and joined the imperial forces; and these were not only rank-and-file soldiers, but also illustrious men, commanders, and magistrates. Only one of the more notable men, Batatzes, remained with him. Tornicius, therefore, abandoned by all, with no hope of escape left, fled with Batatzes into a certain temple. From there, having been put in shackles, they were led to Monomachus, and, their eyes having been gouged out, they were blinded, and the sedition was completely crushed.
XXIV. So far we have narrated civil wars; now let our discourse turn to external wars, and let the expedition of the Rus be recounted first.
This nation had frequently attacked the Romans in the past, and had been defeated. Finally, a treaty was struck and a marriage alliance formed, as they sought to bind both peoples together, and they began to trade with one another. When, therefore, many Tauroscythians were selling their wares at Constantinople and our own people were trading with them, it happened that a dispute arose between some of them and some Romans; this progressed from fighting to wounds, and from wounds to killings, and among those who fell was one of the notables among them. The prince of that land seized this as a pretext for an uprising against us, and immediately assembled a great number of boats—which they call "monoxyla"—and, having embarked a multitude that exceeded almost any number, he launched an undeclared war against the Romans and entered the Propontis.
When the sovereign learned of their approach, he sent envoys to them to lay down their arms, professing his readiness to provide satisfaction if any grievance had occurred to them. But the Barbarian received the envoys arrogantly, and, having spewed forth insolent and offensive words, dismissed them with ignominy. The sovereign, therefore, despairing of peace, prepared himself for a naval battle; and because the fleet was very far away, occupied in guarding the provinces, he prepared other triremes and cargo ships, loaded them with an abundance of liquid fire, and arrayed them against the Barbarian fleet, while he himself remained present, sitting up above the harbor.
When the better part of the day had passed and the Barbarians remained still, yet did not break their formation, the emperor ordered Theodorocanus to take three triremes and harass the Barbarians, so as to draw them into battle. He did not, however, begin with skirmishing but with a full naval engagement, and he burned some of the Barbarian vessels with the liquid fire, sank others along with their crews, and captured one, onto which he leaped himself, having cut down some and terrified others, who were as if paralyzed by fear. With these deeds having been successfully accomplished by the three triremes, at a signal from the emperor, more cargo ships set sail. When the Barbarians caught sight of them, they became unmindful of the battle, broke their line, and turned to flight, gradually retreating; the Romans were also assisted in that battle by divine providence, for suddenly an East wind arose, and, as huge waves were rolled toward the west into the Barbarian ships, it submerged many and smashed many more against rocks and hidden reefs; those on board the waves partly engulfed.
And as the assault against the enemy had thus been successful with the three triremes, and the emperor had provided the signal to the other merchant ships, more set sail. When the Barbarians saw them approaching, they no longer recalled the battle but, breaking their formation, they reversed their sterns and rushed into flight. At that time, a certain impetus from above also fought alongside the Romans; for a strong wind suddenly blew from the East toward the West, and, stirring up a tempest, it was brought against the vessels of the Barbarians; it submerged many and delivered them to the depths, and many it dashed against underwater rocks and reefs, smashing them to pieces. As for their crews, the water covered some, while others were destroyed by the military formation lying in wait on the shores as they were cast up by the swell. Thus, the multitude of the Barbarians having been vanquished, the emperor, bearing trophies, returned to the palace.
XXV. One barbarian war has thus been described; let another movement of a nation be mentioned, which at that time took hold of the East and even to this day preys upon it: I speak of the Turks. These people are a Hunnish race, very numerous and free, inhabiting the northern side of the Caucasian mountains. For when the Persians, while the Macedonians were contending among themselves, had recovered their kingdom, as I have related before, they were again defeated and oppressed by the Saracens, and lost it once more. The Saracens, however, were not obedient to one ruler, but, discord having arisen among them, they were divided into factions and attacked one another. Muchumetus, therefore, the son of Imbrael, prince of Persia, Chorasmians, Media, and certain other peoples, in the times of Basil Porphyrogenitus, having succumbed in a war undertaken against the Indians and Babylonians, summoned the Turks as allies. And there was a commander of those who came for the alliance with Muchumet, namely Tangrolipix Mucalet, with whom Muchumet, having attacked his adversaries, routed them. And having marched back to his own lands, he did not allow the Turks to return to their own [territory], but wished to hold them back so that he might use them as allies against others as well. They, being unable to return to their own land unless the bridge of the Araxes were yielded to them (this is a river which cannot be crossed except by the bridge, and the bridge was towered on both sides, and guards stood at the towers), revolted; and, having fled to the mountains (for they were confident that they were three thousand, and did not dare to fight against many myriads in a pitched battle), they would sally forth from there and plunder the property of the Saracens. Muchumet, therefore, sends against them an army of about twenty thousand, entrusting the leadership of them to ten commanders of his own race. The Turks, having attacked them at night, easily slaughtered most of them. The survivors, together with their commanders, returned to Muchumet. He blinded those ten commanders of the army, and threatened the surviving soldiers with the most terrible things. Fearing this, they joined the Turks. Already, others also—many slaves and criminals who delighted in plunder—flocking to them, a numerous army was formed, and in a battle joined with Muchumet near Apascham, they slew many Saracens, and among others, Muchumet himself also fell. Tangrolipix, therefore, having received the empire of all the nations that had obeyed Muchumet by the consensus of the armies, tore down the towers of the bridge of the Araxes. The crossing of the river thus being opened, a great multitude of Turks
the barbarians flowed down to Tangrolipix, and the leader of the Turks, having gathered a heavy force from these, began everywhere to strip the Saracens of their power, and distributed it to himself and those of his own race, and made those from Hagar his subjects. Then he himself gained the Babylonians in battle, and sent his nephew Cutlumus with an army against the Arabs. But he, being defeated, returned, and sent an embassy to the prince of Media (for so Vaspurakan was called of old), requesting to be allowed to pass through his country. He, however, having been appointed by the Emperor to rule that land, not only did not grant him passage, but actually drew up in battle array against him; and having engaged, he was defeated, and while he lost many of his own men, he himself was captured. Cutlumus, therefore, having returned to Tangrolipix, narrated the events of the war in Media, and praised the great fertility of the land, adding that it was easily captured, being enjoyed by women—for so he called those against whom he had fought. B Tangrolipix, however, was angry with him for his defeat, while he [Cutlumus] went off against the Arabs. And Cutlumus, fearing, fled with those of his company, and having seized Pasare (which is a city of the Chorasmians), he defected from the sultan (for thus was Tangrolipix named) and for a long time resisted his uncle, who was besieging him; yet the latter laboured in vain on account of the strength of the city. While the sultan was occupied with the siege of Pasare, he sent Asan with a competent force against Media. But he, having attacked the country and engaged with the Romans, fell himself, and his army with him was defeated, and with the exception of a very few, the whole was destroyed. The sultan, having learned this, was dejected, but hastened to retrieve the failure, and having entrusted an army numbering about one hundred thousand to his half-brother Alimus Abramius, he sent him against the Romans. The general of Vaspurakan informed the Emperor of these matters by letter, asking for an alliance. And he sent to Liparites, who held the rank of dynast of part of Iberia, that he should join the Romans and assist against the Turks. But the Turks having already arrived, the Romans did not decide to fight them unless they were united according to the imperial command with the Iberians as well. While the soldiers were keeping quiet, Alimus, being unable to engage them as they were stationed in a fortification, moved against the town of Arzen (for this was a small city); a multitude dwelt in it, if I may so speak, of merchants almost innumerable, and men of great wealth. Thinking he would capture it at the first onset, he was frustrated in his hope when he invaded it. For the inhabitants obstructed the approaches with logs, and destroyed many by hurling missiles from the houses. This having been done for six days, when the leader of the Turks saw that the Arzenoi were defending themselves most courageously, and that it could not be easily taken by siege, he set out to overcome them with fire; having cast this into the houses, as the conflagration raged on all sides and the citizens turned to flight, the barbarians occupied the town and found much gold and other wealth which the flame had not consumed. Then they hastened again against the Roman army. When Liparites with the Iberians was already present, a battle having been joined towards evening at Bulytum, Alimus and Chrosanes, another leader of the barbarians, turned and fled with their own men, the Romans pursuing them until late at night. But Liparites, while fighting on the other wing, had been captured by the enemies, and while the Romans, having returned to the camp, were waiting for him for a long time in ignorance of his fate, someone announced that he had been captured by
his captors were departing in haste. The Romans, upon hearing this, were in low spirits, while the barbarians carried Liparites to the sultan. The emperor, however, learning of the capture of Liparites, sent to the sultan seeking that Liparites be deemed worthy of freedom, and he sent many ransoms for him, and also gifts to the sultan, and proposed that a treaty be made between the Romans and the Turks. The sultan returned Liparites to him as a gift, and gave back all the money that had been sent for his redemption, and exhorted him never again to take up arms against the Turks. He also sent an ambassador to the emperor, whom they called a *serif*. This man among them holds the same position that in former times the person called a *syncellus* held among us; for just as the *syncellus* took the place of a dead patriarch, so too the *serif*, upon the death of the caliph, succeeded the deceased. Therefore, the *serif* entered the great city and, having come to speak with the emperor, behaved arrogantly and demanded that tribute be paid to the sultan; and thus he was dismissed. For this reason the sultan, enraged, himself attacked the Romans; and at that time the Turkish race, advancing further, subdued the whole Orient and arrived at the continent facing Byzantium. And thus matters concerning the Turks transpired.
XXVI. On the Patzinaks
The nation of the Patzinaks was moving about in the European region. It is a Scythian and populous nation, inhabiting the area beyond the Ister. At that time, Tyrach governed the nation; he was a man noble in lineage, but sluggish in character. Another man, however, named Cegenes—although obscure as far as genealogical reference goes—was a man of action and energetic, and because of many excellent deeds performed in wars, he was beloved by the nation. For this reason, he was stalked by Tyrach, who sought to kill him. Having noticed this, he gained the friendship of two of the tribes—there being thirteen in all—and with them he crossed the Ister and came to the Roman borders, claiming that he had come as a defector to the emperor and that he would not be useless to him. These things were reported to Monomachus, and the governor of the region was commanded to receive the Scythians. Cegenes, having traveled to the queen of cities, came into the sight of the emperor, and having received the saving baptism, he was honored as a patrician. He also persuaded the Scythians with him to be cleansed by that divine bath. But when he held a permanent encampment on the banks of the Ister, and having suddenly crossed the river, he harassed the territory of Tyrach with slaughters and plunderings; Tyrach, through legates
complained to the emperor that he had received an apostate from his nation, and threatened that, unless he ceased to harm the nation by harboring the fugitive, he would have the entire nation as his enemy. To this, the emperor replied that he would neither betray the one who had fled to him, nor forbid him from defending himself against those who had plotted against him. When these messages were delivered to Tyrach, the winter season arrived, and the Ister, frozen by the cold from one bank to the other, had become solid like crystal, so that he crossed the river on foot with his whole nation; and having seized the Roman territory, he laid waste to everything before him. The emperor, upon learning this, immediately sent an army to assist the governor of the province and Cegenes. However, the Patzinaks, finding an abundance of food, and wine, and other beverages prepared from honey, gorged themselves greedily on these and fell into a bowel disease. When Cegenes the deserter learned of this, he persuaded the Roman forces to attack the enemy. When the Barbarians saw the Roman advance against them, being exhausted by their illness, they became dizzy, and losing heart, they cast aside their weapons and became suppliants, surrendering themselves to the enemy. Those who fell into the hands of Cegenes were partly slaughtered by the sword and partly sold into slavery for gold. The rest of the multitude were deemed useful to the Romans if, after being disarmed, they were settled in the Bulgarian territory, which—because that nation had been destroyed not long before—was for the most part desolate: a plan which was carried out by the emperor’s order. Tyrach himself, along with the nobles of his nation, was brought to the emperor, and having been deemed worthy of divine baptism, was honored with brilliant dignities.
When, at that time, there were wars for the Romans against the Turks in the East, the emperor selected fifteen thousand Patzinaks, armed them, provided them with horses, and appointed commanders for them from their own people; he ferried them across to Chrysopolis and ordered them to go into Iberia, adding a guide for the journey. When they arrived near Damatris, they stopped and would not go any further, but turning back and reaching the strait, they crossed the sea with their horses near the monastery of Saint Tarasius, and marched continuously until they joined their fellow countrymen. Having done this, they fixed their tents in the camps on the banks of the Ister and made incursions into Thrace, being repeatedly superior to the imperial forces sent against them, so that they wandered with impunity throughout all of Thrace and Macedonia, plundering everything, not only in remote areas but also those adjacent to the city. Finally, they concluded a thirty-year truce with the Romans, and thus the war of the Patzinaks ended.
XXVII.
The Sultan, however, did not cease to harass the Roman cities and provinces, plundering some, devastating others, and annexing others to his own kingdom. At that time, the emperor discovered a plot made against him [by Romanus the Boilas...
This was an obscure man, having a half-mute tongue, and destitute of the power of articulate speech, partly through a defect of nature, and partly through simulation and affectation, by which he aggravated this inherent defect. The emperor took more pleasure in this man than in any other, and preferred his trifles—spoken incorrectly and confusedly—to all other pleasures; whence he became so familiar to him that he dared to approach and address him at any hour. The women's quarters were no less open to him than the emperor’s private chamber, whenever he wished, and wealth flowed to him from both sides, so that although he had been raised from the crossroads to the senatorial summit, he was not content with that prosperity, but coveted the kingdom, and strove to claim it for himself by killing the emperor.
He therefore revealed the secret to those whom he knew to be ill-disposed toward the emperor; and if they approved of his speech, he confirmed them in the crime by holding out great hopes. But if any rejected the attempt, he would say that he had spoken for the purpose of testing them, in order to explore their loyalty toward the emperor, and, having praised their integrity, would solemnly affirm that he would testify to their goodwill before the emperor. Managing many in this way, the conspirators thought that his design could most easily be accomplished. For not only was the path to the emperor’s bedchamber open to him, even when the emperor was sleeping or lying with the empress, but the keys to the secret entrances had been entrusted to him. Therefore, he would have fully executed his design, had he not been denounced by one of the accomplices and caught in the manifest crime, still holding the very sword with which he was about to strike the emperor. However, he himself paid no penalty due to the man’s stupidity, only the accomplices in the plot being subjected to punishment.
This emperor, wishing to be accounted benign and liberal, but possessing neither the skill nor the measure to do so, slid from the virtue of magnificence into the vice of prodigality. For he distributed public money in such a way that he observed neither to whom, nor how much, nor when it should be bestowed. For when he was building the monastery of the Mangana (it is said that he began the structure because of his beloved, I mean Scleraina, so that by this pretext he might more frequently visit her, as she was then living in the Cynegesium), the fisc and public treasuries were exhausted, and being reduced to a shortage of money, he did not abstain from illicit gains. But since certain provinces were exempt, which, because of the narrowness of their regions, defended themselves against all taxes and barred the barbarians from access to Roman territories, he abolished the guards and imposed tributes on those provinces; whence they lay open to the barbarians without any struggle. That, indeed, if one truly considers it, can be laid to his account, that the East was subjected by Barbarian warfare. He also built a new prytaneum, calling it a poorhouse, and from it released a great stream of gold to those who chose to practice asceticism.
Various readings and notes
(40) That Monomachus exhausted the imperial treasuries for the monastery of St. George at the Mangana, and what the nature and magnificence of its structure was, Michael Psellus recounts above others in lib. V of his History (manuscript), and not only in that, but also in building other houses dedicated to God or the saints: [index: entries preserved]
continuously, who at the time occupied the house of Cynegius, using as a pretext [the need for] the construction of the sanctuary, he exhausted the imperial or public treasuries; and being in need of funds, he did not abstain from forbidden sources of revenue. Furthermore, even from the regions that contributed no taxes to the public treasury for the emperors, but instead of all taxation guarded the difficult terrain and walled off for the barbarians the passage into lands subject to the Romans, he imposed taxes upon those regions and abandoned the garrisons. And from this, the passage into the Roman lands became easy for the barbarians. That man, therefore, will be judged by those who calculate dispassionately to be responsible for the eastern part falling prey to the barbarian spear.
[XXVIII.] When Empress Zoe had died in advanced old age (for her life had been prolonged beyond seventy years), he did not mourn her passing in a manly fashion, but rather in a boyish way. It is said that he even compared her to the angels, because a fungus had sprouted from a certain dampness on her tomb, and he called this a sign orchestrated from above, so that it might not be unknown that the empress had been received into the choir of the intelligent powers. But he, still burning with the fire of passion, introduced into the palace a certain young girl, the daughter of an Alan prince, who had been given to the Romans as a hostage, and, naming her Augusta, assigned her a royal retinue and most lavish provision of wealth. And if he had not stood in awe of the empress, I mean Theodora, or rather, if he had not been ashamed of the disgrace and impiety of a fourth marriage, he would have proclaimed this mistress of his an empress and adorned her with a diadem. But that happiness of the Augusta did not last long; for after the death of the emperor, she returned to her former condition of being a hostage.
Now I must speak of the end of this emperor. Not much time had passed since he had become master of the Roman government when an articular disease attacked him, with humors flowing down at first to his feet, so that he was unable either to stand or to walk. When, however, it was necessary for him to move, he would either walk with difficulty, supported by others, or he would be carried. But as time went on, the fluxions settled upon his hands as well, then vexed his shoulders, and finally infested and relaxed his whole body, insinuating themselves into the cavities of his joints. To these was added another evil, a stitch in the side from his breathing, which made it so that neither he himself had hope of a longer life, nor his grandees, the chief of whom was the logothete John—a castrated little man, born of an obscure and most ignoble family, and most inept at handling affairs, yet so versed in the art of grammar that he could neither speak properly in his affectation of Atticism, nor write without error. To him the emperor committed the summit of affairs; him the senate designated as prince. For before this, the administration of the republic had been in the hands of a man entirely unlike this one in birth, eloquence, and knowledge of the governance of the state; who, when the emperor ordered something otherwise than was fitting, did not immediately lean toward his will, but resisted and deterred him. When this happened frequently, he, with grave and inequitable spirit, liberated—
he was anxious. While he was in this state, Bardas was twisting affairs, leading everything just as he pleased, and he had ascended to the dignity of Caesar, longing for the empire for himself, and seeking an opportunity to attain it. He had achieved no good, save that he took much thought for the promotion of learning. For philosophy lay neglected and was almost entirely extinguished, so that not even a spark of it remained; the ignorance of the emperors had been the cause of this. But this man established schools for every discipline, appointed teachers, and decreed public stipends for each of them; and above all, he installed as a great and common teacher the philosopher Leo, whose fame for wisdom was great, and who had been held in honor by the emperor Theophilus on the following occasion. Since he had many students, it happened that one of them, traveling in the East, was captured by the Saracens, and, being well versed in geometry, was given over into the servitude of a certain illustrious man among the barbarians. Now the prince of the Agarenes was diligent in listening to learned and philosophical theorems, but was devoted above all to geometric methods. When the master of the captive attended the lecture-hall, the captive sometimes accompanied him, and, having heard the teachers and seen them tracing out geometric figures, and teaching that the triangle is the principle of every figure—so that every figure is composed from it and resolved back into it—and that the circle is the most capacious of all figures which are contained by an equal perimeter, and other such things, the captive asked his master if he might be permitted to question the teachers about what they were teaching. When he granted this, he asked them to explain to him the reasons for what they had taught. But as they were unable to answer, the prince of the Agarenes sent for the captive and asked him if he could do it himself; and when he affirmed that he knew, he ordered him to bring the explanation forward. The captive then began from the beginning to explain everything, citing the reasons and setting forth the causes. The listeners, having understood his words, perceived the theorems more keenly, as though their minds had been expanded, and in their admiration for the man they asked if Constantinople had other men like him. He said: "It possesses many who are superior to me, but one who is the teacher of all, a man in philosophical studies altogether incomparable." Having heard these things, the prince of the Agarenes was overcome by love for that teacher and longed to see and hear him; and entrusting the captive, whom he had treated liberally and kindly, with letters in which he urged him to come to him and share his wisdom with him, he sent them to him. But Leo, fearing that he might fall into a charge of treason on account of the letters sent by an enemy, handed the letter over to the logothete of the dromos, bringing the captive also to explain its subject matter. From this it became known to Theophilus, and, being favored with benefits, he was ordered to teach publicly; later he was also appointed bishop of Thessalonica. But after the death of Theophilus, among the other iconoclast bishops, he too was, for the sake of honor—
grew resentful of that man’s freedom, and in order to unburden himself of this opposition, he removed him from the administration of public affairs (this was Lichoudes), and introduced John in his place, entrusting the governance of political matters to him, so that he might not be deterred by anyone in those things he intended to pursue. This John, therefore, and others, who also had great influence with the Emperor—having despaired of his surviving—persuaded the ailing man to be concerned about a successor, and suggested to him that the Proteuōn was suitable for the throne. He, however, was girded with the government of Bulgaria and was away from the Queen of Cities. One was therefore sent to summon the man; but the plan was kept hidden from the Empress Theodora. But the design did not remain hidden from the Empress to the end. Having realized that the dying man was not settling the power upon her, she left him struggling with death in the palace of the Manganes, and she herself, with the haste of those attending her, having boarded a ship, sailed to the great palace. There the imperial bodyguard and the elite of the senate immediately flocked to her, and she was hailed as Empress by all, as the empire belonged to her by hereditary right. When this became known to Monomachos, it added to his pain, and after breathing only for a short time, just enough to lament the events that had occurred and to fall into despair, he expired, having reigned twelve years and eight months, and was laid to rest in the monastery of the Manganes built by him.
XXIX. The Empress Theodora
Theodora, having obtained the sole rule, though already advanced in age, did not wish to place an Emperor over affairs, but, delighting in the empire as long as she lived, kept everything in her own power, having been encouraged in this also by her attendants, whom she placed in the highest magistracies. She entrusted the administration of civil affairs to the syncellus B Leo, who was surnamed Paraspondylos, and who had formerly served the Emperor Michael. She herself sat in judgment upon the tribunal, responded to legates, created magistrates, delivered justice, cast votes, and interposed her own judgment in private and public matters. And her government was fortunate. For no one raised a hand against her, nor did her subjects neglect her decrees, nor did any nation then wage war against the Romans; indeed, the seasons had their proper temperate character, and the earth brought forth abundant fruits. Although she had attained the scepter at an advanced age, as has been said, she still anticipated a long time of life for herself, as she was of a sturdier body, one not easily broken by labors. But when fate also pressed upon her (even though certain monks promised her I know not how many ages of life) and her intimate friends, due to a most grave illness which physicians call iliac—
and this, too, was urged onwards by fate (even if some monks had promised her many more years of life) and she had fallen into a severe illness, which the Asclepiads call ileus, and when those around her despaired of her recovery, they gathered together and deliberated on whom they should introduce to the principate to preserve for them their prosperity and good fortune. It seemed to them that Michael Stratioticus was suitable for this—a man by birth a Byzantine, but one who, because of natural sluggishness and simplicity, and also because of the great length of age which had extended over his life, was entirely unfitted for the management of affairs, and that, too, of such a principacy. For he was not just an old man, as he was called by the common crowd, but truly decrepit. They therefore persuade Theodora to place him in the empire; and she immediately encircles Michael with the imperial diadem, having first bound him with terrible oaths that he would decree nothing in the affairs of the administration of the empire without their consent. After these things were accomplished, the empress breathed her last, having reigned for one year and nine months.
BOOK EIGHTEENTH
I. In the manner described, the old man Michael having been proclaimed emperor, he treated the senators and the plebeian multitude with the greatest kindness, advancing each of them to grades of rank and bestowing honors upon most of them that exceeded their capacity. But with the military rolls, and especially with those who excelled the others and had long been leaders of armies and wings, he acted quite differently. For upon those having recently entered the city, he bestowed no honor, nor did he soothe them with gentle words. The leaders among these were two men, both of them magistroi, both of illustrious birth, and having achieved that splendor even more by imperial skill, strength of hands, and boldness of soul: one was Isaac Comnenus, the other was Catacalo the Ambustus, whose fatherland was Colonea. As soon as Michael had been designated emperor, he deprived the latter of the duchy of Antioch and immediately declared his own cousin, Michael, magister and duke of Antioch, adding the surname of Ouranos to him so that he might appear to trace his lineage back to that ancient Ouranos. This Catacalo, therefore, as if he had performed the duties of his office poorly, had diminished the military forces, and was burdensome to everyone he met, he scolded sharply and did not refrain even from reproaches and insults; and when other most illustrious men, his colleagues, were about to defend the man, he did not moderate his tongue even against them, nor did he revere the Comnenus himself.
This cast the soldiers into despondency, for while they saw Michael being most generous to others, he would not show them any kindness even in his words; nevertheless, they refrained from any revolutionary action and resolved to make a second attempt. But the narrative must now relate another insurrection against the emperor, and thus it will return to these men. The first cousin of the emperor Constantine Monomachus, the A *proedros* Theodosius, having been deeply vexed by the proclamation of Michael, took his own attendants—and certain others who suffered from the same levity of mind as himself—and, surrounded by these satellites at twilight, he processed through the middle of the forum, claiming that the empire belonged to him by reason of his kinship with Monomachus, and crying out that he was being wronged. When he reached the public prison, which is called the Praetorium, he broke down the bars and provided an exit to those imprisoned there. He did the same in that place of confinement which is at Chalce, hoping by their agency to achieve his desire. But when he learned that military forces were being collected around the palace against him and were about to arrive, he turned toward the Great Church, and being abandoned there alone with his son—his followers having slipped away—he was led out and banished. This being the outcome of his attempt, the plebeian multitude sang this ditty about him in mockery:
*The foolish Monomachus, what he conceived, he did.*
And these things turned out in such a way.
II. But the Eastern commanders, having been affected by insults and being about to risk their fortune again, approached Leo, the *protosyncellus* who was managing public affairs, and through him they attempted to entreat the emperor not to be the only ones neglected, when all others had perceived some fruit of his liberality, and especially when others were living pleasantly, while they themselves were standing in the battle line and fighting for the Roman empire. But he, being no more skilled than the emperor in handling aggrieved men and in repressing the swelling arising from their pain, not only did not soften the asperity of the men's words, but even exacerbated it, disparaging each one and sending them all away without honor. C Angered by this second rebuff, they decided they should delay no longer, but each exhorted his neighbor, and they hastened to reveal the deed they were meditating. It seemed good to them, therefore, to hold Bryennius also as a confidant and an accomplice in the undertaking, being a man of distinction and leader of the Macedonian forces, and holding the command of Cappadocia, who had himself causes of resentment against the emperor.
When they discovered that he too was ready to join their conspiracy, they began to seek who among them should be emperor, and by the votes of all, Comnenus was preferred over the rest. These matters being settled, they all returned home, having made their departure known to the emperor. Bryennius also departed with his legions; and when he had arrived in the Eastern province along with the patrician John Opsaras, who carried the gold, he administered the distribution of the military stipends. When this had already begun, he was apportioning grain to the individual soldiers and increasing the sum of money. But the aforementioned quaestor of the gold denied that he had any such instruction from the emperor. Bryennius ordered him to be silent and to do what he was commanded more promptly. As Opsaras still did not obey, but even openly defied him with greater insolence, Bryennius, seized with anger, sprang up from his seat, and grabbing the man by the hair of his head and his beard, dashed him to the ground and threw him into chains. When the commander of the Pisidians and Lycaonians, who had his camp nearby, learned of this, judging the deed to be the beginning of a revolt, he suddenly attacked the tent of Bryennius with his own legions, and having released Opsaras, he handed Bryennius over to him; he immediately put out the eyes of the man, and thus sent him to the emperor, sending letters with him of all that he had dared to do.
Things having fallen out thus, Comnenus and those who had conspired with him met and set to work, as the military rosters flowed to them from here and there. For in all the military, there was a desire that a soldier should be girded with the imperial authority. For they were chafing under the rule of the civil faction, but did not dare to bring their longing into the open, hiding their love and laboring in the intention concerning this. But when they learned that Comnenus had attempted a revolt, and that he himself was the leader of the drama, and that the best of the families were co-operating with him in the enterprise—believing this to be what they themselves had been planning—they seized the opportunity, and each hastened to outstrip his neighbor. Hence, a heavy force gathered for Comnenus; he then sent out across all of the East, and collected taxes, having appointed receivers of these, and clerks for what was brought in. Then he set out for Nicaea, already proclaimed emperor by all the soldiers with him, wishing to become master of it, so that he might have the city as a base of operations or a shelter, in whatever direction fortune might turn for him; and he took it, and having established a sufficient garrison in it, he encamped twelve stadia from it.
But the emperor Michael, having raised his Western forces, and whatever ones of the Eastern ones had not departed from their allegiance, appointed as commander of the empire in the East the domestic of the East, Theodore the eunuch, the minister of the empress Theodora, having added as his colleague the master Aaron, the brother of Comnenus’ wife. They, having pitched their camp against the enemy, engaged in battle in a place which the inhabitants call Orcus. There, the right wing of the Comnenians was routed by the left wing of the imperial forces, and the one who was in command of it, Romanus Sclerus, was taken alive. But the enemy’s left wing, which Ambustus commanded, having put the right wing of the imperial forces to flight, penetrated even into the camp and tore down and overturned the tents. This occurrence greatly emboldened the spirits of the Comnenians, and so dejected the imperial forces that they gave themselves over to flight, in which many were slain, and not merely common soldiers.
Furthermore, a large number were taken prisoner, and victory now openly smiled upon Comnenus.
III. Having learned this, the Emperor Michael despaired of the war and wished to lay down his authority, but his intimates did not approve of this counsel, and therefore he was not permitted to carry his wish into effect. Men were then sent to Comnenus as envoys, chosen from the upper ranks of the Senate: these were the Proedros Constantine Lichudes, the Proedros Leo Alopus, and Michael Psellus, the wisest of philosophers, who held the rank of Prince of the Philosophers. The sum of what they were to announce from him was that Comnenus should be adopted by the Emperor and designated Caesar, with pardon and oblivion granted for the crimes of those who had assisted in his usurpation. When these things were announced to Comnenus in the hearing of the army, the soldiers grew tumultuous, declaring that they would not suffer their commander to be seen in a different habit, nor could they endure that he should be stripped of the imperial vestment with which he was already clothed. These words were uttered by many more out of obsequiousness than from the heart.
When the assembly was dissolved, Comnenus took the envoys aside and said: "If you will promise to convey certain secrets to the Emperor, I will explain to you what I keep hidden in my mind." When they had pledged their faith to secrecy, he said that he was now content with the rank of Caesar and did not seek the empire; but he demanded that the Emperor confirm that he would never hand over the empire to any other, that he would not snatch away the rewards he had granted to his own men, and that he would impart to him some portion of the imperial power, so that he might be permitted to adorn some with lesser dignities and raise others to military commands. If he obtained these, he would make peace and come to the city; and because these things would be displeasing to his own men, he would write two separate letters, one of which would satisfy the soldiers, while to the other he would entrust his secrets. Furthermore, he demanded that the person who currently mismanaged public affairs—and who was ill-disposed toward him and his own men—should be removed from office. D Having said these things and delivered the letters, he urged the envoys to depart.
The aged Michael, having received the letters and heard the secret mandates, was delighted and agreed to all of his demands, and he added: just as Comnenus had signified certain private matters to him through them, so he would announce even more secret things to him through the same men, namely, that they should confirm with an oath that not much time would pass before he would accept him as a colleague in the empire; but that for the present, for certain reasons, he was delaying this. Comnenus eagerly accepted what was thus announced by the envoys, and the others did not unwillingly accept it either, especially because the power of the administrator of public affairs was to be removed, and it had already been decreed by all that they should lay down their arms and refrain from usurpation. But the aged Emperor, before he would deal with Comnenus, in order to confirm—as he supposed—the goodwill of the senators toward himself,
had drawn up a certain written document, binding every man with oaths and curses so that they should neither call Comnenus emperor nor offer him imperial honor. By distributing money to some of the senators, bestowing dignities upon others, and using various allurements with others still, he persuaded them to affix their signatures to the document and to guarantee its contents. But when they heard that Comnenus was coming and would enter the city the following day, some factious men of the senatorial rank went early in the morning to the shrine of the divine Word, the Sophia, where they were joined by the leaders of the guilds, and with loud cries they demanded the presence of the Patriarch. He, having locked the doors on all sides, sent out his nephews to inquire what they wanted. But they, having grown in number by the arrival of a crowd, seized them and threatened to treat them ill unless the Patriarch immediately came out. It is said, however, that these things were feigned and simulated, so that the Patriarch might appear to be going out to the seditious against his will. When, therefore, he had descended from the upper stories, dressed in his priestly robes, he began to denounce those present—as if, forsooth, violence were being done to him. The bolder among the seditious, or rather those by whom the sedition was organized, surrounded him and demanded that he act as their envoy to the Emperor so that the document which they had been forced to sign might be returned to them, since he had now made peace with Comnenus. He agreed to fulfill their request; but these things were all a pretense. For a short time later they openly proclaimed Comnenus emperor and autocrat, with the Patriarch himself consenting and cooperating—he who had sent word to Comnenus not to delay, had demanded a reward for his suffrage, and had insolently ordered the previous emperor to vacate the palace. Upon receiving this message from the leading priests, the elderly Michael asked, "What reward will you give me in return for the empire?" And when they replied, "The kingdom of heaven," he immediately cast off the imperial insignia and departed from the palace. Comnenus, for his part, having honored Kekaumenos with the title of Curopalates, sent the man with a force to take possession of the palace, the people of the city having poured out to meet the new emperor, making his entry into the imperial city most glorious.
IV. The elderly Michael, therefore, having ruled for one year, fell from the throne, and after living for not long in a private state, paid the debt [of nature]. But Comnenus, having secured the empire,
attributed his success to himself and not to God; and this is evident from the fact that he inscribed himself on the coin holding a drawn sword, all but crying out that this, and nothing else, had procured the kingdom for him. He rewarded those who had assisted him most generously, and he hastened to give everyone their rewards so that they might all return home, lest they cause some disturbance or rise up against the people by strolling about the city. He held the patriarch in great reverence, and for his sake he honored the patriarch’s nephews with great honors and set them over haughty magistracies. B Furthermore, to please the patriarch, he assigned to the Church the administration of ecclesiastical affairs; for whereas up to that time both the great oeconomus and the sacristan were usually appointed by the emperor, he subjected both of these offices to the authority of the patriarch, removing public control over them. Then, he proclaimed his consort Augusta, and he honored his brother John with the title of curopalates and named him grand domestic. Since all the emperors before him who had held the empire after that Porphyrogenitus, Basil, had not used the imperial and public property well, but had either squandered it upon their own pleasures and the construction of monasteries, or had gifted it away to whomever they happened to choose as they saw fit, the imperial treasuries were exhausted and the public treasury was empty of money. Desiring to correct this vice, Comnenus did not undertake the reform slowly or by degrees, but just as he had had himself depicted on the stater with a hand raised holding a naked sword, so he applied himself to affairs, prepared to cut away the tumors at once, without employing medicines to soften and soothe the ulcerated parts. Therefore, from the moment he exchanged his tyranny for legitimate rule, he abrogated the acts of his predecessor without distinction; if that man had gifted anything to anyone, he snatched it away. But he also overturned and abrogated many of the acts of earlier emperors; and in this he spared neither the senate nor even the vulgus. Finally, as time went on, he turned against sacred property; he cut away much that had been contributed to the monasteries that had been constructed by them, and rather subjected the monks to the public tax rolls, leaving them only necessary expenses while attributing the rest to the fisc. As he thus introduced innovations day by day, he incurred the hatred of all—of the senators, of the common people, and even of the military, for he did not spare them either, but diminished their resources, and this more than toward all others, toward those who spent their lives in monasteries. But nothing—neither entreaty, nor fear, nor hatred, nor reproach—led him away from his purpose; to such a degree had he lifted up his spirits and puffed up his soul. 
V. But neither did the patriarch yield to his pride, for
he wished to issue commands to him, and if at any time he failed to obtain what he had requested, he was annoyed, rebuked him, and threatened him, and then, as though he himself had bestowed the empire upon him, he threatened that he would take it away from him again. Such treatment that emperor could not endure with equanimity, and therefore he wisely took measures to act before he suffered anything.
Thus, when the patriarch had gone away to his own monastery, the festival of the heavenly Hosts who were honored there being already at hand, in order that he might celebrate that feast more brilliantly, the sovereign sent a body of soldiers there (for he did not wish to drag the man away from the Church of the Wisdom of the Word of God, lest a disturbance arise among either the clergy or the populace) and banished both him and his nephews who were with him. Thereafter he set his mind on subjecting him to deposition, and he used certain of the high priests as collaborators for this, and also Psellus, the collector of many and weird accusations, which he had gathered and written down in a discourse against him; and he indicated to the man through certain persons that he should renounce the pastoral office, so that he might seem to resign it willingly, as if, should he not do this, he would be deprived of it against his will, with the accusations brought against him being exposed to all. But since the man would not yield to the emperor's will, the sovereign was concerned about his deposition.
But death hastened to resolve the patriarch's anxiety. For after a short time the patriarch departed from this life, and someone brought the news of his death to Comnenus, as though it were freeing him from his worries. But he at once changed his mind and lamented, and having brought his corpse back from exile with an honorable funeral procession, he laid it to rest in the monastery he had founded, and restored his nephews to their former honors. In the Church he replaced him with the president and protovestiarius, Constantine Lichoudes, a man who had long shone in the administration of public affairs and who had remained blameless, to whom Monomachos had also entrusted the oversight of the Mangana, and the documents concerning their freedom.
Wishing to make these under his own control, Comnenus devises a scheme full of wickedness and the offspring of a malevolent mind. For since Lichoudes had been elected and designated, and had already been ordained a priest, he informed him secretly that certain things were being said against him, which barred him from his priestly functions, and that if these were not spoken of and resolved synodically, it would be impossible for him to receive the anointing of the high priesthood. "But," he said, "if you give me the rights of the Mangana, I will overlook what is being said and allow you to be anointed." Lichoudes, therefore, perceiving that it was no longer possible for him to return to his former state, and that to remain in such a condition was shameful, promptly brought what was requested, and with no one hindering him, he was initiated with the pontifical rite.
VI. And Comnenus, having set out on an expedition against the Hungarians and the Scythians, who are called Patzinacae,
he granted peace to the Hungarians when they requested it. But against the Scythians, as he advanced, the others submitted and made peace (for they were not all ordered under one leader); but one of them, Seltes, being bolder, encountered a division of the Roman forces, was defeated, and having lost many of his own men, fled with the survivors. The emperor then moved his camp and, toward the end of the month of September, withdrew to Lobitzum, where, due to a violent downpour and untimely snow, most of the horses perished, and many of the soldiers were endangered by the cold; and when the overflowing and stagnant rivers suddenly cut off supplies, both the men and his pack animals suffered from a shortage of necessities.
However, once the rain and snow had abated slightly, the emperor stepped out of his tent and withdrew to a certain large oak tree nearby, with some of the nobles standing around him. But a loud noise having arisen in that place, he and his attendants withdrew from it, and the tree was immediately torn up by the roots and fell. Therefore, having returned to the great city, in order to render thanks to God for the safety he had received, he built a church within the palace grounds in honor of the protomartyress Saint Thecla, because on the very day on which her memory is celebrated, he had escaped that danger from the oak—but it was such a structure as to argue for his great pusillanimity; this, his nephew (the son of his brother), the emperor John, son of Alexius, restored most magnificently in the name of our Savior Jesus Christ.
VII. Now it must be told how this Isaac resigned the empire, although writers do not agree on the matter. For the most learned and eloquent Psellus relates that he was addicted to hunting, and as he often struck bears and boars with his spear, his side was struck by a cold wind, and from this a fever arose from within and flared up. The next day, when he was pressed by pains in the side and was breathing with difficulty, with uncertain hope of life—or rather, with life completely despaired of—he handed over the empire to Constantine Ducas and embraced a better way of life. The Thracesian, however, reports that while he was hunting near Neapolis, a boar of horrible appearance was seen, and while he was pursuing it with loose reins, while he was repeatedly aiming his spear at bears and boars, he was struck in the side by a cold wind, and then, not long after, the wound became evident; on the following day shivering set in, followed by a fever.
It was judged not a boar, but some sort of diabolical apparition; and meanwhile the emperor was struck by a flash like lightning, thrown from his horse in terror, and collapsed on the ground to the astonishment of all, frothing at the mouth and senseless. Put into a small boat from there, he was conveyed to the palace, and having been ill for a short time and then given up for dead, he had his hair shorn, put on a rough cloak in place of the purple, nominated Constantine Doukas as emperor, and thus sailed to the Studion monastery, where, having recovered his health, he spent the rest of his life. He reigned for two years and three months. He was a vigorous man, arrogant in disposition, very quick in transacting business, and highly skilled in warfare. He was not himself learned in letters, but he was a lover of learning and a patron of scholars; he was so chaste in his marriage that, when he once fell ill while abroad and was advised by his doctors to relieve his illness through the practice of intercourse with a woman, he refused to have relations with anyone other than his own wife. When he was accused of aspiring to the throne, he replied that he could not endure the rule of a fellow slave by whom just rewards were denied to him. And so, having escaped [the danger], he vanished, having supposedly been submerged in the sea, as most judged.
VIII. Constantine Doukas, having obtained the empire without labor, as has been said, raised many of the senatorial order and of the plebeian station to higher degrees of dignity; and those whom Comnenus had despoiled of their own honors—who were many—he restored to their original status. But he was very nearly drowned through a conspiracy. For when he was at the Mangana palace, the conspirators had devised a plan that, if a tumult were reported to him in the marketplace of the city, he would immediately cross by sea to the great palace; they had ensured that the imperial trireme and its pilots, who are commonly called *protocarabi* and had been brought into the conspiracy, would be absent. The emperor, hearing of the tumult, decided he must go straight to the palace. But as the imperial trireme was not to be found there, he boarded the nearest small boat. While he was sailing, however, the imperial ship appeared, which, cutting through the waves with frequent rowing and vehement impetuosity, was directed by the pilots straight toward the small boat, with the intention of sinking it and sending the emperor to the bottom. But those who were sailing with him shouted and cried out more boldly for them to check their oars and turn the rudders; the pilots, as if thunderstruck, by the will of God complied. Thus, with the danger avoided, he learned how and by whom the conspiracy was prepared, and he punished the guilty by merely confiscating their property. Even though he boasted of being descended from the family of the ancient Doukai—Andronikos, Pantherios, and Constantine—that family, when Constantine (as mentioned above) had aspired to the throne while Constantine Porphyrogenitus, the son of the Emperor Leo the Philosopher, was reigning, and because of his immature age was still under the...
actually, for having failed to award proper reverence to the holy icons—also deposed. Bardas, therefore, finding him at leisure, promoted him above the other teachers, and in a short time caused learning to flourish and make progress. He also made the civil laws to flourish again, by frequenting the courts himself, when the knowledge of them had almost completely vanished. Now, while Bardas’s zeal for the sciences and for learning is indeed praise-worthy, his other actions are highly blameworthy and detestable. For after the holy Methodius had guided the Church for four years and had departed to the eternal mansions, the monk Ignatius stood at the head of the Church. He was a grandson of the Emperor Nicephorus—the one who was of the genikoi—and a son of the Emperor Michael Rangabe, who, when that kingdom fell, was castrated by Leo, shaved his head, and for a long time tamed his flesh with the sweats of asceticism; he was raised to the pontifical throne of Constantinople by the Empress Theodora, who was then in power.
This holy Ignatius forbade the Caesar Bardas—who had dismissed his wife without cause and was said to be cohabiting with his daughter-in-law—from entering the sacred precincts. For this reason, Bardas drove the high priest from the Church, and after subjecting him to many evils, finally enclosed him in a tomb. Later, he took him out of there and banished him to the island of Mitylene. Nor did he treat only this man in such a way, but also many other high priests who were of the same mind as Ignatius—who had been interdicted by him—and he substituted in his place Photius, an illustrious man, a chief secretary, and most renowned for his learning. But when the legates of the Pope of Rome, who had been sent against the iconoclasts, were present, Bardas persuaded them too to come over to his own opinion. Therefore, having gathered in the church of the Holy Apostles, they brought back the holy Ignatius from exile, only to deprive him of his dignity. And these things happened in approximately this manner.
V. Now the Scythian nation of the Russ, which dwells by the Taurus mountains, having attacked the Euxine Sea with a fleet, themselves…
supervision of guardians. But that family had long since been utterly exterminated, root and branch, at the time when Constantine had attempted to seize power (as has been stated earlier). Since no male of that line remained, it is said that his ancestors descended from a certain woman; hence he was not considered a pure Ducas, but a mixed one, and one who possessed a corrupted kinship with the Ducas family. For this reason, most people did not call him Ducas, but used the diminutive form, Ducitzes 56. The man was, however, pious toward God, gentle in character, and a lover of justice, but in his reasoning he was slow and dull. While he took pleasure in those whose integrity had been proven, he loved money beyond measure and devised means by which he might amass many treasures. Therefore, he devoted great effort to two things. One was to increase the public revenues; for this reason, he sold the offices designated for fiscal administration to anyone who wished to purchase them, and he entrusted the collection of taxes to publicans with an agreement for added profits. This, then, was his first concern. The second was the hearing of lawsuits and the issuing of decrees. But in administering the law, he did not always observe the prescriptions of the statutes; rather, in some places his will stood in place of reason, and he would change the calculations, taking account of the persons involved in his judgments. Being parsimonious and stingy, he resolved within himself not to engage in battles with foreign nations, but to bind them to him with gifts and other tokens of favor, and to reconcile them to the Roman Empire. By this course of action, he sought two advantages: first, that he would not waste money on expeditions, and second, that he himself might remain at peace and spend his time almost entirely in the gathering of wealth. Therefore, he neglected the military registers, or rather, he struck many of the bravest men from the rolls because of their high pay. The result was that the barbarian element became bolder, while the Roman legions became more timid and inferior, and the Roman hegemony diminished. For many of the Eastern provinces were at that time partly plundered and ravaged, and partly occupied by the enemy.
IX. Nor did things stand in a very good state in the Occident, because of both the scarcity and the cowardice of the soldiers. For the nation of the Uzes (this is also a Scythian tribe, which is preferred to the Patzinacs in those regions for both the excellence of its lineage and the magnitude of its numbers) crossed the Ister with their wives and children. The governors of the neighboring cities—these were the magister Nicephorus Botaniates and the magister Basilius Apocapes—tried in vain to prevent their crossing; but having been defeated, they were both captured and led away by the barbarians, who, having crossed the Danube, filled its entire tract. For the majority agree that those among them able to bear arms numbered over sixty thousand. Advancing thence from Macedonia,
they plundered, and proceeded as far as Greece. These things brought despair to the Emperor, yet he hesitated to levy forces and kept postponing it, so as not to spend a single obol. He kept saying that the nation was invincible. Having sent envoys with gifts to those who led them, he attempted to persuade them to make peace with the barbarian. But since he was being openly reviled by all for not marching against the barbarians out of stinginess, he departed from the city and encamped at Chorobacchos, taking with him, as it is said, not more than one hundred and fifty soldiers. While he was anxious about the gathering of an army, the destruction of that nation was announced to him. For a most severe pestilence having attacked it, and it having been greatly weakened thereby, the Patzinakoi and their neighbors, the Bulgarians, attacked them in their weakened state and utterly destroyed them, with only a few of the leaders barely managing to cross the Ister. Now the entire conduct of this affair was the work of divine power; for the Emperor, having despaired on all sides, fled to the Divine with tears, begging for His help with a contrite heart and calling upon the guidance from above. These things were done in the sixth year of the reign of this emperor. Before this, a great earthquake occurred on the twenty-third of the month of September, by which temples and houses were ruined in many places. At that time, the Hellenic temple at Cyzicus collapsed—a most beautiful work and worthy of admiration—and the great and renowned temple of the holy Fathers in Nicaea was shaken. And in the month of May, in the fourth indiction, a hairy star appeared, following the sun as it set. At first it was equal in magnitude to the full moon, then, as if growing a mane, it diminished, and decreased by as much as it had once grown. It was seen for forty days, with its rays turned towards the East. When, however, Patriarch Constantine Lichudes—having governed the Church for four years and six months—had passed to the other life, John Xiphilinus was raised to the pontifical see of Constantinople; he was by birth of Trapezus, but erudite in every kind of learning, and had been ranked among the principal senators. He, having of his own accord left all things behind and having shaved his head, frequented the Olympic monasteries
and there, having spent a considerable time, was judged worthy of the patriarchal throne. But the emperor, having been seized by a disease, and suffering and being afflicted by it for a long duration, and having now been brought low, and knowing that his death was inevitable, left his empire to his three sons, whom his spouse Eudocia bore to him; two of these, Michael and Andronicus, were still private individuals, but Constantine was already emperor, having been proclaimed Augustus himself. Whence he alone was Porphyrogenitus, whom he immediately, and before the others, adorned with the imperial insignia. Then he also proclaimed the others as emperors. These he set over the rule, but their mother he left as empress and nurse, and, to sum it up in a few words, as mistress, entrusting her with the administration of the empire, as it seemed moderate to him, and no less apt for the treatment of affairs than she was clever at educating children. But first he exacted a written oath from her, by which she promised that she would refrain from a second marriage. That document, signed by her, was handed over to the patriarch to be kept. But the senators also, all compelled by an oath, promised in writing that they would create no one else emperor but his own children. Having finished these things, he died. He reigned seven years and a half; he lived sixty years and more. Moreover, while alive he had called his own brother Caesar, and had had him as a counselor. C D
Various readings and notes
(63) John Xiphilinus. See what we observe concerning him in Anna Comnena, p. 267. 
(64) Psellus in his History writes that Constantine Ducas had three sons and two daughters before he obtained the empire, of whom the second son died while his father was reigning; another, Michael, was later emperor; the third, however, was Andronicus. The first of the daughters embraced the monastic life and was still alive when the same Psellus was writing; the other married a husband, perhaps Domenico Silvio, the Doge of the Venetians, as we have shown in Fam. Byzant.; for Psellus is silent about the spouse's family. But after Constantine obtained the empire, the Porphyrogenitus son was born, whom the same writer calls Constantios in two places, and Constantius elsewhere. 
(65) His spouse Eudocia. Psellus, in his History not yet published, reports that Constantine Ducas entered into marriage twice while still a private citizen: first with one born of noble lineage, as he hints using the words of the Dalassenos family: hence we previously wrote incorrectly in our Byzantine Families that the daughter of Constantine Dalassenos, who lived under Michael the Paphlagonian—whose daughter Constantine Ducas is said to be the son-in-law by Scylitzes and Zonaras—was the other wife of Ducas; and indeed, since the same writers are silent about these first nuptials, we wrongly wrote in our Byzantine Families that Eudocia was the other wife of Ducas and the daughter of Dalassenos. But Psellus does not reveal the lineage and family of Eudocia; but we have shown that she was called Macrembolitissa in our Byzantine August Families, with additions, and that she was of singular erudition, which is testified to by the works of hers preserved in libraries, in which it happened that I read that vast work from the Colbertine collection, which she inscribed and dedicated to the emperor Romanos Diogenes, her other husband, with this prefixed epigraph:
Ionians, a collection of gods, heroes, and heroic genealogies...
, etc. This is a work, I say, filled with a multifarious variety of matters and which testifies to the immense erudition of the princess, and her assiduous reading of books, both, as she herself says, from her own library, and those purchased elsewhere at great expense. Then she adds that this work should be joined to others she had hitherto completed: Where, in the margin, these things are written: Moreover, in the Ionians there are some things which she transcribed from Suidas in those places where he deals with writers, since he lived under John Tzimiskes, which she herself seems to hint at in the title. 
(66) And Andronicus. He writes soon after that he was gifted with the title along with his two other brothers. Therefore, he is that Andronicus whose death Psellus laments in a Monody in the Royal Codex, with this title: [in which he is called] ... Then he sufficiently hints that he ruled together with his brother, in these words: [where the word "alone" must be observed, for Constantine was the third son, the Porphyrogenitus]. Finally, in the eulogy of Andronicus, which the same Psellus placed at the very end of his History, he explicitly calls him emperor, in these words: the most delightful this emperor, etc.
He entrusted these men with the administration, while he left their mother to them as empress, nurse, and mistress—to say it all in sum—having placed the management of the empire in her hands, since she seemed to him prudent, skilled in the upbringing of children, and naturally gifted for the administration of affairs. First, however, he had demanded of her a written oath that she would not enter into a second marriage. This document, having been confirmed by her, was given to the patriarch for safekeeping; but all the members of the senate also pledged in writing that they would never proclaim any king other than his children. With these arrangements made, he departed this life, having reigned seven and a half years, and having lived sixty years or somewhat over. He had nevertheless honored his brother with the title of Caesar, and he was his counselor and a sharer of his secrets. Though not himself versed in learning 67, he loved it, and he revered the learned 68; he was accustomed to say that he preferred to be known for his learning rather than for his empire. And concerning those who plotted against him, he declared that he would deal with them in no other way than by using them as slaves, since the laws robbed them of their freedom.
X. Eudocia, therefore, succeeded to the empire together with her children according to the testament of the deceased emperor, and she undertook the management of public affairs, acting as a kind of teacher to her sons. She sat with them on the tribunal, occupying the center herself, with them sitting on either side of her; and thus they conducted themselves in assemblies, in the investigation of civil controversies, in receiving embassies, and in solemn processions. But the barbarians, who were accustomed to plundering the Orient, did not cease to overrun the Roman provinces and daily devastate them. For the soldiers, as I have already said, had been neglected and reduced to misery by the emperor's parsimony. Even if any still remained who were devoted to military service, they were unenthusiastic about engaging the enemy, being deprived of their customary pay. Thus, the barbarians would sometimes lie in wait in Mesopotamia, sometimes lay waste to the borders of Melitene, sometimes harass Cilicia, and at other times fall upon the Cappadocians, and occasionally those in Coelesyria. These reports caused the empress distress, and since many told her that the state needed an emperor, she, fearing that some man might be proclaimed emperor by the public, and that she and her children might in this way be cast out of the empire, decided to bring someone into the government, so that she might preserve the power for herself and her children, and that it might remain for them inviolate. For people deny that she married Diogenes out of licentiousness or desire for pleasure, but rather that she placed that man—as one strenuous and proven in military matters, and endowed with incomparable strength—in charge of supreme affairs, so that he might [repel] the attacks of the barbarians. Variæ lectiones et notæ. 67 And not versed in learning. He said: "I wish I were known for this rather than for the empire." 68 And the learned. [Referring to] Eudocia, his wife, who says in the Preface... when she addresses Romanus Diogenes: "And I am hopeful that this also... will be numbered among the many others who will rejoice at your return to the palace, O Triumphant Emperor; for you will find my administration of the empire not as I [have governed], but also [will find] the city adorned with every virtue and good customs. For I speak of learning, than which there is nothing more precious, and of the reading of learned men, and of the diligent study of such, which, like the garden of Adonis blooming with all manner of fragrant flowers, has adorned itself with the beauty of virtues, having become a rival to the Macedonian city [Constantinople] which has had many learned men, and in every way emulating the formerly glorious Athens, whose renown is heaven-high." For learning knows how to correct all things so well that it is able both to extinguish barbarism and to renew the sweetness of speech, and to order everything with decorum; for the daily exhortations of learned men are like an instrument that tears away the ignorance of the soul and implants a stable understanding; and this occurs not only in the queen of cities, but in all of Hellas; for the subject, by nature, is wont to model himself upon the will of the leader, aiming at him as at a target. From which one may gather with how much zeal for letters and disciplines Constantine Ducas began the restoration in the primary city and indeed in all of Greece, and his wife Eudocia likewise, after his passing, while she alone with her children held the reins of power. Hence that saying of Constantine, which I recalled above in Psellus' History and from him in Zonaras.
For they do not say that she attached Diogenes to herself out of licentiousness, or because she was overcome by pleasure, but in order to place a man of action, distinguished in warfare and of incomparable strength, at the head of the government, so that the barbarian onrush might be checked to some degree by his resisting them with his own arms. Now this man was also of illustrious birth and celebrated for his brave deeds. His father had been the brother-in-law of Romanos, of the house of Argyros, who governed as emperor long ago, and, having been convicted of seeking the throne, threw himself down from a height so that, if captured, he might not be forced by interrogation to reveal his accomplices and thus bring punishment upon them as well. The son had been honored with the rank of vestarches. For having been appointed Duke of Sardica by Doukas, and having happened upon the Patzinaks somewhere there, he engaged them, killed many, and took no fewer prisoner; he sent both the living and the heads of the slain to the emperor, for which reason he was appointed vestarches, with an honorific letter stating that this rank was not a gift to him, but a reward for an outstanding feat.
