While stationed there, he made preparations to seize the kingdom, having learned of the emperor's death. When his plan became known, the empress had him arrested. Thus, having been led in chains to Byzantium, he was convicted of the crime, condemned as a conspirator, and for a time confined within prescribed limits. Later, as one condemned, he was brought before the imperial tribunal so that the sentence might be confirmed against him. But mercy for the man came upon all who were standing by, and everyone was moved to pity. For he was not only incomparable in strength, but also fair to behold. The empress, too, felt for him along with the rest, and sympathy for the man took hold of her—though whether it was love, I cannot say—and in place of the vote fixing his punishment, she issued a decree freeing him from the punishment. Having been absolved of the charge, he left for Cappadocia (for that was his fatherland), but the empress summoned him back and appointed him Magister and commander of the forces, having already decided to proclaim him emperor, as she judged him to be the most noble of men. She was, however, hindered by the oaths and the written document which had been entrusted to the patriarch for safekeeping. And it was her particular concern how she might take this back. She therefore devised a wicked and deep-laid plan regarding this, and shared it with one of her eunuchs, employing the servant as a pander, and instructing him in how he should speak with the patriarch. The eunuch approached the high priest and said: "Know, my lord, that the empress is in love with your nephew" (for he had a nephew, Bardas, a young man, flourishing and given to sensual pleasures), and he added that if you consent—that is, if you return the written document of the oath to the empress and persuade her that great danger hangs over her from it—she will immediately marry your brother's son and hand over to him the imperial power. As the patriarch was beguiled by these deceits, he gave himself over entirely, and the eunuch went on to demand that he also sound out the minds of the senators. The eunuch, therefore, having summoned the senators one by one, said that the times demanded an emperor who would undertake the defense of the state. For if the Roman empire were to be administered only by a woman and children, not long afterward not only the remote provinces and those bordering on the enemy would perish, but even the suburban ones. To these things he added complaints about the oath and the written document, and spoke ill of the dead emperor, as if he had hindered the common good not out of concern for the republic, but out of private jealousy.
He demanded that this oath and the illegal documents be set aside. While he was summoning each of these men, he would say that he was seeking after the matters at hand, and that an emperor was needed who would take charge of them. For if these things were still to be administered by a woman alone and by mere children, the empire of the Romans would certainly perish before long, and not only the distant provinces, but even those near the borders and the very threshold of the empire, would fall to the enemies. And concerning this, he made many accusations against the oath and the document, and disparaged the dead emperor as having provided no care for the common interest, but as having, out of jealousy, demanded outlawed oaths and illegal documents. Having found most of those to whom he spoke to be in agreement—and if any did object, he persuaded them, some with gifts and others with promises, to come over to his side—he sent back the written document of the oath to the empress. A Having received this, the empress moved to execute her plan, and communicated the matter to the elder of her sons, and to the Caesar, who had been summoned to the palace; he participated in the wedding and tasted of the nuptial mixing-bowls. Thus she was married to Diogenes. So he was declared emperor, whereas Eudocia and her sons had reigned for seven months and a few days.
XI. When Romanus Diogenes had secured the Roman empire, he disappointed the hope of Eudocia, the author of such great status for him. For she, having rescued him not only from a manifest danger—when he was condemned and already almost saw the executioner standing over him—but having also elevated him to such a great empire, thought she would have him obedient to her word in all things, and that she would rule over the emperor himself. But he, being of an arrogant and untamable disposition, yielded to the will of the Augusta for a time by forcing his own nature, simulating submission. But when he could no longer bear the constraint, having quickly shaken off the yoke, he returned to his own character and wished to exercise power freely. Since, however, the Eastern provinces were in a bad state, and some things were despaired of while others were nearing desperation, he proclaimed an expedition and pitched the imperial tent in the region of the East, opposite Byzantium, for two reasons: that he might provide for the safety of his subjects as much as the circumstances allowed by prohibiting the incursions of the Barbarians, and that he might seize an opportunity for glory, and not be the empress's piece of property, but an emperor in his own right. Having therefore left the palaces, he crossed into the East, without having led out a proper army, and with the very troops he did lead out suffering from a shortage of arms, horses, and necessary supplies. For it was not easy to suddenly restore to full strength an army that had been neglected for a long time through sloth. When, therefore, the soldiers in this state had been assembled in the Eastern province—which is a part of Phrygia—the enemies, having learned that the emperor himself was present, and that he was such a man as not to shrink from readily braving danger for his subjects—a man warlike and endowed with immense strength (for the Barbarians did not accurately know the state of the army)—reasoned that they must act with caution and refused to decide the issue in battle. Whence the sultan withdrew, and two parts having been separated from the Barbarian army
he ordered one to rush into the southern part of interior Asia, and the other into the northern. The invaders plundered whatever they encountered, and having suddenly fallen upon Neocaesarea, they sacked it, and being loaded with spoils, they set out from there to return. News of this being brought to the emperor, it instilled in him despondency; he took the more active men of his army and set out, eager to reach the enemies beforehand through pathless regions and mountains. When he came upon them unexpectedly, he cast them all into fear, and they immediately turned to flight, abandoning there both the loot and their baggage. A great slaughter of the Barbarians, however, did not occur, because the soldiers, exhausted by the journey, were not able to pursue them for long. Yet the captives, and whatever else filled out the booty, were all set at liberty. From there he set out for Syria, and having sent one phalanx to Melitene, he himself hastened to Chalep, and brought away a great haul of men and other animals. From there he came to Hierapolis of Syria, and having undertaken to besiege it, he received the city by surrender. Subsequently, when the Barbarians had attacked a portion of the Roman army and defeated it, no one of the others showed a desire to come to the aid of the defeated. But the emperor, being inside the captured city and having learned what had happened, immediately sallied forth with those about him to the aid of the defeated. The leader of Chalep, having learned of the Roman defeat, added himself also to the other Barbarians with all his own force, expecting to lead the emperor away as a captive immediately. And they surrounded the Roman camp in a circle. The emperor, however, launched the battle against them without proclamation (for he went forth from the camp and led out the army, without the sound of trumpets or any other manifest signal) and turned the opponents, and forced them to turn to flight. For if the Roman army had pursued them for a longer time, it would have won a great and glorious victory. For the horses of the Arabs are indeed swift in the first charge, and faster than all the rest, but they do not extend their course for long, and they tire very quickly. But having been recalled—so that by not pursuing the enemies they kept them safe—they thus robbed themselves of a great success and glory. Still, it seemed sufficient to the emperor that the Romans had dared to invade the enemies, having cast off their former timidity. By order of the emperor, the citadel of Hierapolis was established. In his progress, after some towns were taken and others left behind because they were impregnable, he arrived at the city of Cilicia called Alexandria. When he departed from there, it was announced that Amorium had been plundered and many slain. Although the emperor felt grieved at these things, since he could offer no aid, and having led the soldiers into winter quarters where they would not be pressed by any lack of provisions, he himself returned to the queen of cities, acting more arrogantly, as if he had accomplished more, not only toward others but also toward the empress herself. And she, perceiving that her hopes had turned to the contrary, was vexed and had a heart swollen with grief, and could not endure his reproaches. For the horses of the Arabs, although they are swift in the first charge, and in their fleetness of foot surpass those of others, do not extend their run for long, but tire very quickly. Being recalled, and refraining from pursuit, they saved their opponents, while depriving themselves of a great achievement and glory. Yet to the emperor, the fact that the Romans had dared to attack the enemies at all, and had laid aside much of their former cowardice, seemed a sufficient success. By the emperor's order, the acropolis of Hierapolis was settled. Having attacked other smaller cities, and having passed by some because they were impregnable, while seizing others, he arrived at a city of Cilicia called Alexandria. And from there, as he was leaving, it was announced that Amorium had been plundered and many killed. The emperor indeed was distressed at these things, but not being able to assist those who had suffered, he managed the affairs of his own army so that they might pass the winter season without lack of necessities, and he himself returned to the queen of cities, bearing himself more arrogantly as if he had perhaps accomplished more, not only toward others, but also toward the empress herself. And she, discerning that her expectations had turned to the contrary, was vexed and had her heart swelling, unable to endure his reproaches.
XII. At the beginning of spring, the emperor undertook a second expedition, and when he had arrived at Caesarea and learned that a great multitude of Turks was raiding that province, he sent a portion of his troops against them, while he himself also charged; having routed them and captured or slain them all, he proceeded to the Euphrates, having appointed Philaretus as commander. He was of the race of the Brachamii, who, although he seemed to have sufficient military experience, was nevertheless of a depraved life, and utterly contrary to his name, which denotes someone zealous for virtue. Having given him half of the army, he himself turned toward the north. But the enemies, having struck terror into the soldiers of Philaretus, so that they abandoned the positions they were guarding and fled, pursued them, and seized all their baggage, which brought sadness to the emperor; then, having advanced into Cappadocia, they laid waste the fields and made for Iconium, a city populous with men and fortunate in all those things which are called fine. The emperor, having learned of this at Sebastia, also directed his journey toward the same place. But when news was brought of the devastation of that city, and of the departure of the Turks who feared his arrival, he sent a portion of the Roman legions to Cataturius, the duke of great Antioch, and commanded him to come to Mopsihestia and attack the Turks as they were passing through that way. However, the enemies, when they had come into the plain of Tarsus, were despoiled of almost all their booty by the Armenians, and having heard of the Roman ambushes at Mopsihestia, they set out on their way by night and escaped; this affair was a cause of grief to the emperor, so that, matters being desperate, he returned to Byzantium. For autumn was already pressing, during which season the great temple of the Blachernae also burned down, in the year from the creation of the world six thousand five hundred and seventy-eight. When spring had arrived, he placed Manuel Comnenus, the Protoproedros, nephew of the emperor Isaac by his brother and appointed Curopalates, in command of the legions, who, although a youth, governed the army with the prudence of an old man and defeated the Barbarians. Envious of him for this success, Diogenes took away a not insignificant part of the troops and sent him into Syria. Whence, as Comnenus was setting out for Sebastia with the remainder, a multitude of Turks appeared; who, by a feigned flight, as he pursued them, turned by prearrangement and attacked the dispersed Romans; and having slain many, and taken not a few captive, and having captured the leader Manuel himself, they plundered the camp. This affair—and no less the report concerning the republic of the Chonae and the celebrated temple of Saint Michael being occupied by the Barbarians—disturbed the emperor, so that he was wholly intent upon how he might, by waging war against them as soon as possible, save what was in danger. But while he hesitated on the advice of certain men, Comnenus arrived with the Turk by whom he had been captured. For, when the sultan...
for some reason had become hostile toward the man, he sent an army against him to arrest him. Fearing this, he chose to take refuge with the emperor, who immediately honored him with the rank of president, although he was of very short stature and most unsightly in appearance.
XIII. When spring approached, he boarded the imperial trireme. Scarcely had it departed from the harbor when a dove, not entirely black but inclining toward blackness, flew around his trireme, nor did it cease flapping its wings around him until he caught it with his hands and sent it to the empress; this was considered an inauspicious omen, both for him who had caught it and for her who had received it. Moreover, the imperial tent collapsed when the wood by which it was supported in the middle broke of its own accord; this also was considered an ominous sign. But neither of these things dampened the emperor’s resolve, or hindered him from reaching the Eastern provinces, being intent on frugality beyond his usual custom. While he was staying in certain small dwellings, fire from some unknown source fell upon them, consuming them and burning the imperial horses, harnesses, and vehicles, which also seemed to him a bad omen. Departing from there, having crossed the Halys and left Caesarea behind, he fortified a camp at the place called Frigid Fountain. There, when a legion had deserted because some of the Nenitzi had been punished more severely for their crimes, he himself immediately rode up on horseback and, having brought the army along, terrified the deserters and drew them back into his military alliance. Then he set out for Theodosiopolis, and ordered each man to carry provisions for two months with him, since he was about to travel through deserted regions; and having divided the army, he sent the Latin Ruselius, a warrior of great prowess, with one detachment against Chliat, and ordered another man, with a second detachment, to lay waste to Mantziciert, while he himself retained the rest. D
Since the inhabitants of Mantziciert were not able to wage war, they received pledges, abandoned the city, and surrendered. However, when the soldiers went out to forage, they were suddenly oppressed by their enemies. When the Emperor learned of this, he sent the magistros Nikephoros Bryennios with a few cohorts against them. He engaged the Turks, but seeing his own forces exhausted, he requested reinforcements. The Emperor, being ignorant of the enemy's strength, accused Bryennios of cowardice and was angry with the man (it happened at that time that a priest was reading the Gospel which says: "If they persecuted me, they will also persecute you" 1). Nevertheless, he sent the magistros Nikephoros Basilakes with a part of his forces. When these had joined forces with Bryennios, the battle was for a short time equal on both sides. Then, because Bryennios did not follow, Basilakes rushed in among the front-line troops with his own men, and put the enemies to flight. While he was pursuing them as far as the rampart, his horse was wounded, and he dismounted; and because he could not move easily on account of the weight of his arms, he was surrounded by the enemies and captured. When he was led to the sultan, he did not conduct himself in a way unworthy of a nobleman, nor did the sultan treat him as a captive. Instead, he summoned him frequently and questioned him about the Emperor, and showed him his own forces. Basilakes praised the sultan and expressed admiration, but told him that it was not to the sultan's advantage to engage the Emperor in battle. Meanwhile, the Emperor went out from the camp with the remainder of the army to observe what was happening; he stood upon some hills until evening and then returned. As soon as he had returned to the camp, the Turks surrounded it, and riding around it, they hurled missiles and resounded around the army with discordant shouts and ululations. Thus that night passed. At dawn, a detachment of Uzi defected to the enemies. This also made the remaining multitude of the Uzi objects of suspicion.
XIV. The Emperor, having very quickly sent to Chliat, recalled the armies stationed there. But when they did not arrive—for Tarchaniotes, who was one of the commanders there, having learned of the sultan's arrival, had persuaded Roussel to flee with his forces into the Roman borders—Diogenes resolved to test the fortune of war the next day with those who were with him, and at first light he began to prepare for the battle. In the meantime, envoys arrived from the sultan to discuss peace. The Emperor did not receive the envoys with much kindness, nevertheless, he gave them leave to speak, and urged them to report back to their master: if he wished to speak about conditions, he must abandon the place where he now had his camp and move his tents far away, so that he himself might fortify his rampart there, where the tents of the Barbarians currently stood.
His design, however, did not succeed, because it was hindered by divine Providence, which caused them to depart with their objectives unfulfilled, or rather, having experienced the wrath of the Divinity, to withdraw. But the Hagarenes from Crete were also plundering the Cyclades islands and the seacoast. Most violent earthquakes also occurred, the most terrifying of which shook the earth on the day of the Ascension of our Savior. Yet nothing of this led Michael to deviate from equestrian contests or from the zeal he had for them, for he was busy with these equestrian games himself in the location near the Strait, called that of Saint Mamas.
Hence he also suppressed the beacons 52 which the emperors of old had devised, so that those from the race of Hagar, when they suddenly invaded the Roman territories, might not plunder whatever was at hand and take people prisoner 53. For this reason, they had built a fortress on a high hill in Tarsus, called Lulum, and those inside it would light a beacon when they perceived an incursion of Ishmaelites; seeing this, those on the Argaeus mountain 54 would raise another, and those near Isamus another; and when those near Aegilus 55 saw this one, another would be raised, and another would be kindled near Mamas 56, and those near Cyzicus 57 would set fire to a different one, and those near Mocyllus 58 would similarly kindle one, and finally, upon the mountain of Saint Auxentius 59, a beacon was raised. Seeing these, the people in the territories would briefly enclose themselves in fortifications and escape the plundering of the barbarians. And from the mountain of Saint Auxentius, knowledge of the enemy's incursion was given to the reigning emperor.
Therefore, in order that the equestrian contests might not be interrupted at the time of—
Having spoken these things to the envoys quite arrogantly, he ordered them to return quickly. They, therefore, reported the emperor’s words to the sultan, and he began to deliberate with those around him concerning the terms of peace. But the emperor, having become arrogant and persuaded by some of those intimate with him who said that the sultan was afraid and because he did not have adequate forces, was therefore seeking peace so that, after the battle was postponed, he might bring up another force—and not waiting for the return of his envoys, nor considering anything else, he ordered the signals for battle to be sounded. Although the unexpected event disturbed the Barbarians, they nevertheless drew up a battle line, and even though they were being attacked by the Romans, they did not counter-attack, but retreated little by little, neither fleeing nor fighting.
But when the day was far spent, the emperor, knowing that the guard around the camp was not significant and fearing that it might be plundered by the enemy if they attacked, decided to abandon the battle and march back to the rampart. Therefore, having turned the imperial standard, he himself turned back and signaled the army to do the same. Those who were around him carried out the order without commotion, but those whose ranks were further away interpreted the emperor’s withdrawal as a flight, due to the word spread among the phalanxes by Andronikos, the son of the Caesar. For both the Caesar and his sons were always lurking against the emperor and were secretly plotting against him. Therefore, Andronikos at once, with those around him (for he was in command of no small portion of the soldiers), returned to the rampart with his horse at a gallop. This also turned the rest to flight, and when the emperor saw them returning in such a disorderly fashion, he stood still and exhorted those who were leaving to stand firm. But they had all become deaf to his commands and did not cease their flight.
When the enemies realized that the unexpected flight of the Romans was a misfortune and a divine manifestation of wrath, they immediately fell upon the emperor. He, with those around him, did not refuse the battle and for a time resisted bravely; then, as some fell and others were captured, the emperor was surrounded by the Barbarians. Yet not even then did he give in. Having struck and killed many, he himself was wounded in the hand, and being thus exhausted, and being no longer able to defend himself against those coming upon him, nor yet able to flee, his horse having already fallen from the arrows, the emperor of the Romans was captured and led away as a prisoner to the Barbarians.
When this news was received, the sultan, by the name of Axan, felt joy, as is natural, yet he was not carried away by pride. Many things are recounted concerning his moderation and justice. For this reason, he did not believe in the emperor’s captivity, due to such excessive success; and when he saw him, he did not rest until the envoys, whom he had sent some time before, recognized him; and Basilacius, who was held there, was brought in and prostrated himself at his feet, weeping. Then, as if moved by a divine impulse, he leapt from his throne and, according to custom, trampled upon him as he lay on the ground. Then he raised him up and embraced him, with words to this effect: "Do not grieve, O Emperor, for such are human affairs. But I will treat you not as a captive, but as an emperor." And immediately he designated a tent for him and provided imperial services and a table.
He made him sit upon a throne as an equal, and released to him as many of the captives as he requested. And so, having conversed and lived with the man for several days, and having honored him excessively, he then made a covenant for perpetual peace and promised that an alliance should be contracted between their children, and he sent Diogenes away with as much escort and honor as one could never have hoped for. When he had arrived at Theodosiopolis, having been dressed in barbarian fashion (for the sultan had given him his own national robes), he waited there while the wound in his hand was being treated, and he changed his dress according to Roman custom. From there, he set out for the city with the sultan's envoys. But his captivity having been announced in the imperial capital, a dispute arose among the nobles, some thinking that authority should be restored to the empress, others assigning the empire to the eldest of her sons, and others granting common authority to mother and sons.
XV.
Meanwhile, the liberation of Diogenes was announced, and a letter written by his own hand was brought, which instructed them in what had happened to him; this stirred up a greater commotion, and there was more doubt as to what should be done. Therefore Caesar John and his sons, always hostile to Diogenes (as has been said), seized the opportunity, and having taken certain members of the senate into their crime—of whom Psellus the Hypertimus was the leader, being himself also an enemy to Diogenes—they relegated the empress Eudocia to the monastery which she herself had built in the vicinity of the Propontis strait, and proclaimed Michael emperor. Then they sent imperial letters everywhere, by which the provincials were ordered neither to receive Diogenes upon his return, nor to deem him worthy of imperial honor: of which counsel Psellus was the author, as he himself recounts in his history. When Diogenes learned of this, having pitched his camp at the castle of Docea, Caesar Con-
Constantine, the son of the Caesar, sent his younger son, the *proedros* Constantine, against Diogenes with an army. Diogenes arrived in Cappadocia and, when Crispinus (a Frank by race) arrived with his fellow countrymen and joined Constantine, Diogenes sent Theodore Halyates against them. Engaging them, he was defeated, captured, and had his eyes put out. This troubled Diogenes greatly. While he was in the Tyropoion (a very well-fortified castle), the *doux* of Antioch—who was the Armenian Chataturius—came to him, bringing many soldiers. Having taken Diogenes and brought him into Cilicia, he waited for help from the Sultan, and he himself also gathered forces. Therefore, the eldest son of the Caesar, the *proedros* Andronicus, was sent again against them. A battle was joined, and Chataturius was slain, but his soldiers fled to Diogenes at Adana, where Diogenes was staying. Andronicus besieged the city, and eventually, Diogenes surrendered himself under certain conditions. These conditions were that he should lay down his empire, shave his head, and live a private life. Indeed, chief priests were sent by the reigning emperor, giving him a sworn assurance that he would suffer no harm. Trusting in these, Diogenes donned a dark garment and surrendered himself to the protection of Andronicus. Taking him, Andronicus returned, and upon reaching Cotyaeum, he waited there for orders on what should be done, while Diogenes was suffering illness from drinking poison, which had been treacherously mixed into his drink, as was believed. There, therefore, an imperial decree arrived, commanding that the innocent man be deprived of his sight. And this was done immediately in the presence of the chief priests, who took the man’s calamity sorely, but no one paid them any heed. Thus, crudely deprived of his vision, with no proper care applied, his head swelled and worms teemed from his wounds, so that from that putrefaction the very air around him was filled with a most terrible stench. In such a calamity, he was taken to the island of Prote, in a higher part of which he had built a monastery. Not long surviving there, he gave his body to the earth, his funeral being most splendidly arranged by the Empress Eudocia, having reigned for three years and eight months. All these things that this man suffered are said to have been done by the order of the Caesar, while Emperor Michael was unaware, he being of a soft and sluggish nature, so much so... C ...the commotion and running about the palace, with some wondering at the event and others refusing to believe the matter—for the Empress too was seized with doubt and was uncertain what she should do—in the midst of those who were at a loss, I myself having become one of them, and all urging me to express what was advantageous, and especially with the most excellent emperor himself pressing and urging me, I was of the opinion that he should not be received upon the throne, but should be removed from the middle, and edicts sent everywhere prohibiting this rule to him. This seemed advantageous to those who were established, while to others it seemed differently. While matters were in this state, Emperor Michael, fearing for himself and suspecting the cruelty of Diogenes, took counsel that was most secure for himself, and one might say, even more sagacious; he ran away from his mother and became his own master. Making use of his cousins as advisors—I mean the sons of the Caesar—he made those guards about the palace his own. He was pained when he heard it, he groaned deeply, he lamented, and he beat his breast passionately. Do not distrust me in writing this; the story is not false, nor is it told for favor, but is true and clearer than light.
His eyes had been gouged out—in the presence of the high priests, who felt great distress at this misfortune befalling a man, yet did not deem him worthy of any care. Having had his eyes cut out in a most brutal manner and receiving none of the necessary attention, his head swelled up, his wounds festered with worms, and the air around him was heavy with the stench of decay. Thus, in such a piteous state, he was taken to the island called Proti, where he had himself built a shrine on higher ground. Having lived a short time longer, he there laid aside his earthly remains, having been buried with the greatest splendor by the empress Eudoxia; he had reigned for three years and eight months. It is said that all the events that befell this man were at the ordering of the Caesar, without the emperor Michael knowing anything of it. For the emperor was of a character entirely too soft and lax, and unskilled in the management of affairs and of common business, to say nothing of the administration of an empire. And from this, his uncle the Caesar took advantage of his innocence, and concocted everything against Diogenes.
XVI. Michael being ill-suited for the empire, he entrusted the administration to John, the metropolitan of Side, who, though a eunuch, was a man of action. Afterward, he installed another eunuch, Nicephorus, whom the Monomachos had acquired in his youth for the palace, and who was nicknamed "Nicephoritzes" on account of his youth—a title which he kept—and whom he summoned from Greece and the Peloponnese where he had been officiating in judicial matters. This man had even reached the point of serving the emperor's father, the emperor Constantine Ducas. He was skilled in the transaction of business and practiced in speech, but possessed a cringing character and was full of craftiness. This, then, is the man to whom he entrusted the reins of the empire, naming him Logothete. From this moment, the man from Side was pushed aside, the Caesar became suspect, and the entire administration of the empire fell under the Logothete, and the emperor was led by him like a slave. There was nothing that did not happen through the Logothete, and many were deprived of their properties, as one accusation was piled upon another to seize them; the crushing and misery of the people were great, and there was no one to turn to for relief. For the emperor spent his time in childlike pursuits, while the head of the philosophers and Hypertimos, Michael Psellos, instructed him in words—or so it seemed—and taught him, now the art of grammar, now meters and dialects, and now other matters. For this reason, it seems, the metropolitan of Side managed the affairs of the empire without hindrance in the name of the emperor, lord Michael Ducas. For the emperor gave himself to literary studies and was taught by Psellos to compose iambic verses. The Synopsis of the laws in iambic and political verses, by the most wise Michael Psellos and Hypertimos, addressed to the emperor Caesar Michael Ducas, by order of his father the emperor. He was unsuited and useless for any task whatsoever. But one might ask, what were the pastimes of the emperor, and upon what might
he busy himself? At one moment he would converse with an orator; at another, he would organize the hearing of philosophical theorems. He, however, had no natural aptitude for any of these. The Roman emperor, obsessively clinging to such things, entrusted the administration of public affairs to another, and this person was one solitary individual; for it became law that whatever seemed good to the Logothete should be done. Thus, the affairs of the state were in a wretched condition. And the situation in the East was worse than before. For the Sultan [(91)], having learned what had happened regarding Diogenes, and that he had honored the man whom he himself had taken captive and sent back honorably to his own empire, and that the peace conditions established with him remained unfulfilled, was affected with great sorrow; he incited his own forces to march against the Roman provinces, which they were no longer invading merely to plunder, but as those who intended to subdue and possess them, there being no one to resist. While they were doing this, the emperor, having raised an army, appointed Isaac Comnenus as its commander, and gave him as an associate the Latin, Ruselius, who was in command of four hundred men of his own race. But when the army had been led to Iconium, Ruselius for some known reason defected from his own side, and by himself fought against the Turks and ravaged other Roman provinces. Isaac Comnenus, however, with the rest of the army, was defeated by the Turks; many were killed and many were captured, the camp was plundered, and he himself fell into the power of the enemy, from whom he redeemed himself for a large sum of money. The emperor then placed his uncle, the Caesar, in charge of the war. Learning of this, Ruselius occupied the area before the Caesar could cross the Sangarius, and pitched his camp against him. Although the Caesar promised him forgiveness for his offense if he would lay down his arms and return to the emperor, Ruselius, in his barbaric insolence, did not think at all of surrender. War was joined, therefore, and those around the Caesar were defeated; the Caesar himself was captured, along with many others, except for Botaneiates, who had been campaigning with the Caesar and escaped with a few men. Ruselius then set out for Byzantium, bringing the Caesar in chains, and Basil Malesas, who had been liberated from captivity with Diogenes a little while before. But Botaneiates had escaped with a few men and retreated to Chrysopolis, where he immediately set fire to the houses. Nor was this barbarian man mollified by anything whatever, even when the emperor, promising the honor of Curopalates if he would lay down his arms, sent his wife and children to him. And so, having summoned the Turks against him for pay, since he realized he was not a match for them, he released the Caesar from his chains and saluted the emperor, thinking that many would join him. But when he attacked the Turks inconsiderately with his barbaric ferocity, the Franks killed many of them, and those who were driven into flight were continuously pursued; yet many stood their ground, as their horses were so exhausted that they could not endure a longer pursuit. But Ruselius and the Caesar, pressing on with a few companions, and having advanced too far from their own men, fell into the hands of another band of Turks...
Having been released from captivity, the defector was in Chrysopolis, and he immediately set fire to the houses there. The emperor promised him the dignity of curopalates if he would lay down his arms and send away his wife and children. But the barbarian remained untamed. The emperor therefore sent for the Turks to fight against Ruselios for a fee. But he, not being equal to the war, released the Caesar from his chains and proclaimed him emperor with royal acclamations, hoping that many would join him. Suddenly, however, the Turks appeared, and Ruselios moved against them with barbarian rashness and without foresight. The Franks killed many of the Turks, put the others to flight, and pursued them relentlessly. Most of them were left far behind, as their horses could not keep up with the long pursuit. Ruselios, with the Caesar and a few men, pressed on. When they had traveled a great distance from their own men, another multitude of Turks appeared, numbering many thousands. As those who had appeared fell upon them, they were forced into combat against their will. Surrounded by the multitude and having lost their horses, both the Caesar and Ruselios were captured. But the wife of Ruselios managed to ransom him, and the emperor ransomed the Caesar. The latter, being led to the emperor and arriving at the Propontis, had his hair shorn and his attire changed, adopting a monastic robe; for he feared that he might suffer some harm at the hands of his nephew because of the acclamation. Ruselios, having been released from captivity as has been said, took his wife and the remaining Franks and went to the theme of the Armeniacs, where he had lived before. The emperor therefore sent against him the proedros Alexios Komnenos, a young man, but prudent and energetic. Upon arriving at Amaseia, he secretly signaled to the Turks that if they would capture Ruselios and deliver him up to him, he would fill them with much money. They, therefore, feigning friendship toward Ruselios, seized the man and traded him for much money; he was immediately led away in chains by Alexios to Byzantium, and after being severely tortured, was imprisoned in a tower. Since there was a scarcity of grain in the days of this emperor, such that a whole medimnos was not sold for a gold coin, but rather a pinakion — which is a quarter of a medimnos — the public calamity gave the emperor a nickname, so that he is called by it even to this day; for no one would recognize him when people speak of him, unless they call him Parapinakios.
XVII. In the third year of his reign, the nation of the Croats, whom some also call Serbs, was stirred up and attempted to seize the land of the Bulgarians. They did indeed seize some of it, but after many battles and the slaughter of many on both sides, they fell from what they had held, and being overcome, were driven out of the Roman frontiers and forced to remain in their own customs. A certain slave of the father of this emperor, Nestor by name, vestarches in rank, having been appointed doux of the Paristrion, took up arms against the emperor. For having made a pact with a certain commander of the Patzinaks, who was called Tatous, they arrived at the queen of cities and encamped before it. When the emperor indicated to him that he should lay down his arms, he said he was ready to do so if the logothete Nikephoros were handed over to him, or were at least removed from the scene, as a common calamity and a pestilence to the people. This, for the
emperor, was a matter of no concern; he would step down neither from the city, nor from the provinces, but instead he plundered the regions of the Thracians and Macedonians, as well as those parts of Bulgaria adjacent to them, and retreated into the territory of the Patzinaks. When a son was born to him by Maria from the Alans, whom he also crowned as empress, he named him Constantine after his father, and bestowed upon him the imperial ribbon. He also sent to Robert, who ruled over Lombardy, and betrothed that man’s daughter to his son; upon her arrival, he renamed her Helena (92). B It was at that time that a child was born in Byzantium, having one eye in the middle of its forehead and the legs of a goat. However, as the barbarians were plundering the East and putting everyone they encountered to the sword, the Asians fled and approached the city of Constantine. As no one was taking any thought for the common good (for the emperor was preoccupied with his studies and was being tutored by Psellos in the composition of iambics), a shortage of grain began to press upon the people in the city. To describe this affliction more tragically: it was not merely an evil, but a famine, and a pestilence followed closely in its wake; deaths ensued, so frequent and consecutive that the living were unable to provide burial for the dead. Indeed, they were so numerous that often several were carried out on a single litter, and many others lay unburied in the thoroughfares. Consequently, everything was filled with gloom, both for these reasons and because the populace was oppressed by unjust exactions. As matters stood in this state, the leading men of the East, having gathered together, conceived a rebellion and chose the curopalates Nikephoros Botaneiates, whom they acclaimed as emperor. This filled the emperor (93) with alarm and terror; for he used to rejoice in the belief that the letter N would one day prevail over the letter M. Botaneiates was among the nobility, and was believed to trace his lineage back to the Phokases (94). D The emperor therefore sent envoys to the Turks, promising them lavish rewards if they would catch the rebels and deliver them to him. Botaneiates, however, having (as has been said) taken upon himself the imperial name and dress, set out for Byzantium. From the West, meanwhile, another wave of sedition surged up. For the proedros Nikephoros Bryennios, who happened to be the Doux of Dyrrachium, upon learning that he had been stripped of his office, likewise defected; he assumed the imperial regalia and, guarded by the divisions of troops present there, marched toward Adrianople, from which he had set out. Having joined there with his brother John, he gathered a multitude of soldiers and embarked upon his tyranny.
Having persuaded some of the heathens and Macedonians to assist him—they having advanced from there—he began his tyranny. When Theodorocanus joined with some of those of Bryennius, he was defeated and captured. The people of Raidestos also flocked to Bryennius, and indeed even those dwelling in Penion. Bryennius therefore held firmly to his rebellion, having honored his brother with the dignity of kouropalates, and having sent him with an army toward Byzantium, with the expectation of immediately seizing it. For he was inflated with hopes, as if they were slack—as the saying goes—that the members of the senate would receive him into their hands because of their hatred for the logothete Nikephoros and because of the emperor’s simplicity; but affairs did not turn out according to his design. For those who were sent, having advanced to the wall of the Blachernae, engaged in a skirmish, but achieved nothing. Wherefore, having crossed the bridge (95) to the continent opposite the city, they delivered the buildings found there to the fire, and from there returned to Athyra. Thus the emperor, being besieged on all sides, released Ruselios from his bonds, and by mollifying the man with words and money, persuaded him to advance against the blood-brother of Bryennius, along with the proedros Alexios Komnenos, who was commanding the army remaining with the emperor at Byzantium. Having learned of this, the brother of Bryennius fled in time. Many of the Macedonians, however, who were left behind, were either killed or captured.
XVIII. In the meantime, also, a multitude of Patzinaks came against Adrianople, and having encamped around it, they plundered the surrounding country. Bryennius, however, having greeted them with money, persuaded them to move away from the city. Regarding his brother who had gone to Cyzicus and was attempting to win over those there, Ruselios encountered him and routed him completely. Just as the patriarch John Xiphilinos (96) had been translated to the eternal mansions, having shepherded the Church for ten years, one month, and seven days, Cosmas the monk (97) was appointed patriarch, a man unlearned in rhetoric, but adorned with all kinds of virtues, and for this reason he was held in the highest esteem by the emperor. Botaneiates, however, having acquired for himself regiments from various nations, also won over the Turks, of whom Cutlumus was the leader, being among the famous Persians; he was related by kin to the sultan, and was at odds with him over the rule, and they were already preparing to go to war. This, having been reported to the caliph (who was greatly honored among them, as he was thought to be descended from the lineage of Muhammad), cast him into anxiety, lest a civil war arise among them. Immediately, therefore, even though it was not considered proper for the caliph to leave his own residence or to appear in public, he, despising the custom, went out and came to those who were contending over the rule, and persuaded them to refrain from battle, and that the sultan should hold his own rule intact, but should assist his kinsman in every way to get possession of Roman territory, and rule over it. Having confirmed these compacts, Cutlumus entered the Roman domain and provided his services to Botaneiates, who, being equipped with regular troops, was hastening to occupy Nicaea; for the garrison soldiers, having betrayed the city, had already joined themselves to him of their own accord, and some were flocking to him from the great city day by day. Thus, with matters proceeding according to his wishes, Botaneiates in the city D
of so many people, when a very large crowd had gathered, the common people of the city, their superiors in magistracy, and indeed the most distinguished of those serving in the Church, proclaimed Botaneiates emperor on the day of the festival of the Annunciation, with the patriarch of Antioch, called Aemilianus, and the metropolitan of Iconium themselves presiding over such assembly. The leading men, then, having divided themselves according to the city’s factions and having locked shields in phalanxes, advanced towards the part of the palace facing the rising sun (99), and having taken control of these, they stationed their own soldiers to guard them. As for the emperor, who had fled with the empress Maria and his son to the palace at Blachernae, they stripped him of the empire, clothed him in a monk's habit, and led him away to the monastery of Stoudios, after he had held the empire for six years and as many months. This happened on the Saturday on which the Church has been accustomed, according to the tradition received from our ancestors, to celebrate the miracle of our Lord and God and Savior in the resurrection of Lazarus (1). Michael having thus fallen from the empire, Botaneiates found the autocracy uncontested and proceeded to the Queen of Cities. However, Nicephorus the logothete, having slipped out of the city, went off to join Roussel, who was encamped at Heraclea. But those in the city, having cast down Michael and having maintained the palace for three days while it was bereft of an emperor, sent word to Botaneiates that he should hasten to come. He, having sent one of his own servants—I mean Borilas—ahead to occupy it, himself arrived not much later and took possession of the palace.
XIX
. When Botaneiates arrived at the royal palace (2), he was crowned on Great Tuesday by the patriarch with the imperial diadem, and he first provided all with a bounty by remitting all debts owed to the public treasury without exception and by magnificently instituting new tax registers by decree. And yet, to Bryennios, who still held onto his tyranny in the West, he sent envoys offering to give him the dignity of Caesar if he would desist from his tyranny and promising the confirmation of the honors which he had already bestowed upon those who aided him. But since the man was burning with desire for the empire, he would not yield, and having haughtily rejected a second and third embassy, and having received the envoys otherwise than is customary and dismissed them with ignominy, Botaneiates, having despaired of conditions for peace, unwillingly began a war and sent against him Alexios Komnenos, whom he had honored with the title of Nobbelisimos (3) and appointed as Grand Domestic and commander of the Roman legions. Having engaged him in the place which, from the frequent and good springs of water, is called Calabrya,
he easily overcame his army, and with some killed and others put to flight, he captured the man who had played the tyrant, now left alone; he deprived the wretch of his sight and led him, blinded, into the imperial city. This was the end to which the rebellion of Bryennius came. The Varangians also rose up against the emperor, having plotted to kill him. A second Roman hand having been drawn up against them, they turned to supplication and obtained pardon. When the wife of this emperor had died, many maidens were offered for marriage, and above all others, Zoe, the daughter of the emperor Doukas. But it pleased him either to take the empress Eudocia as his wife or Maria of the Alans, who had lived with his predecessor. He sends, therefore, to summon Eudocia. She, as the story goes, did not refuse, but the emperor was prevented from doing so by certain monks. And he takes the empress Maria, who had lived with his predecessor, having committed no less an illegality than if he had taken Eudocia. For it was an act of impudent adultery that was dared. For this reason, the one who had celebrated the holy nuptial ritual for them also fell from his priesthood. Michael, however, who had previously been emperor, having shaved his hair, as has been said, had been appointed metropolitan of Ephesus by synodal vote, and once having gone there, he returned and made his residence in the monastery of Manuel. After the empire had been lost by Botaneiates, this Michael, as he was dying, at the earnest supplication of the empress Maria—she herself having by then become a nun—granted her forgiveness, and prayed to God that He might likewise forgive her. C Furthermore, the Protoproedrus Nicephorus Basilacius, who had succeeded Bryennius in the prefecture of Dyrrachium, and who was himself burning with the desire for the empire, gathered an army and with it went up toward Thessalonica. There, learning that Botaneiates had reigned, he sent him a submissive letter, while secretly plotting insurrection and summoning the Patzinaks to his aid. Botaneiates, having learned of this, sent him a golden bull, at once ensuring him immunity for what he had dared, while also awarding him the honor of Nobilissimus; but he remained unmoved. Whence also Alexios Komnenos is sent against him, having been honored as Sebastos; and having arrived at Thessalonica and engaged Basilacius, those around him—and the others who had moved sedition against the emperor—having become more moderate, laid down their arms. D Furthermore, against the Turks who were invading the Orient, the emperor assigned a suitable army and sent Constantine, the son of the former emperor Constantine Doukas, with command. But he, when he had crossed to Chrysopolis, immediately studied revolution, and by the military throng that was there, he was hailed as emperor. But the emperor, by having won over the soldiers partly by gifts, partly by the bestowing of dignities, and partly in other ways,
beacon-fires—those proximity-fires in the Royal City—that wonderful man decided. He also condemned those who had reigned before him, because they did not enjoy a modest life, but instead claimed for themselves austerity and royal grandiosity. Whence he himself, thinking to pursue modesty out of simplicity, once met a woman who had just bathed and was returning home; dismounting from his horse, he walked away with the woman along with a few young men who shared in his secrets, and having arrived at her house and shared a meal with her, he departed for the palace on foot. And while he boasted of such things as if he were achieving something great, to others it was judged as foolishness, and they nourished hatred against him. He was not least hated because of the impious young men whom he nourished and who incited him toward every evil. They even fashioned themselves as chief priests and feigned that they were performing the unbloodied sacrifice, and they held that Michael himself was, in appearance, sacrificing with them; playing at what was truly no game, but6 rather something horrible, they mixed vinegar and mustard in golden and gem-encrusted vessels provided by him, and served this concoction to their fellow-mystics and accomplices. But to recount everything about such a person, and the places where the emperor himself happened to be a fellow-reveller, would be a matter of much chatter and unpleasantness. But the narrative shall now direct itself toward recounting the death of Bardas, and that of his sovereign, and before these, the kinship of Basil the Macedonian with Michael—who was indeed from Macedonia, even if some of those who have written about him invent tales that he was descended from the line of the Arsacids. When Krum (the chieftain of the Bulgarians, for he was their leader) captured Adrianople, his parents were taken captive and carried off to that region, bringing him along as a newborn and nursing infant. When Krum had died, his successor, having received many defeats from the Romans, made peace with the emperor, and agreed to release the captives whom he held (as that was the agreement); at this time Basil was in the prime of his youth. Yet they say that from infancy many signs appeared presaging his rule, of which one was what I shall now relate. Basil was still a baby, and his parents were occupied with the harvest, and the infant was sleeping under the sun; an eagle, flying low to the ground with wings spread, fashioned a shade for the infant. The mother, when she saw the eagle approaching the child, lifted up her voice and ran to her son, and chased the eagle away with stones. Upon returning to her work, the same eagle was present, performing the same service for the sleeping infant, and the mother, terrified, chased it away again. But when this happened more often, upon reflection,
conquered him, and having besieged him when he fled to the acropolis of Thessalonica, he had the man’s eyes gouged out by imperial command. Other men also had raised their hands against this emperor, but after being chastened by the events involving Bryennius and Basilacius, they laid down their arms. Since the Turks were ravaging the East, the emperor sent against them with an able-bodied army Constantine, the son of the former emperor Constantine Ducas, appointing him commander-in-chief of the army against the Turks. When he had crossed over to what is called Chrysopolis, he immediately attempted a coup, and was hailed as emperor by the military multitude that was there. But the emperor won over some of the soldiers with gifts, attached others to himself with the honors of dignities, and dealt with others in different ways; and having been arrested by them, Constantine was handed over to the emperor. The youth was then tonsured and confined to an island. Rumor also has it that the man was anointed as a priest. When Aemilianus, the patriarch of Antioch, had departed this life, another, Nicephorus surnamed Maurus, had been appointed in his place. This emperor, partly on account of his advanced age and partly because of a natural weakness of character, was not very involved in the administration of affairs; but he also took on the metropolitan of Side and entrusted to him the care of public business. This emperor also had two servants, one named Borilus and the other Germanus; these men turned everything upside down as they pleased, and they even lorded it over the emperor and their own master; on account of their audacity and arrogance, he himself also became loathed by men of rank. Nicephorus the logothete, when he had betaken himself to Roussel, was kept in bonds by him, and upon the sudden death of Roussel, he incurred the suspicion of having caused him to be poisoned. Wherefore, being handed over to Botaniates by the relatives of the latter, he was relegated to one of the islands lying off the Queen of Cities. But those around the emperor, fearing that if he were to come into the emperor’s sight he would be taken up and would set everyone aside, suggested to Botaniates that the logothete possessed immense wealth, and that this ought to be brought into the imperial treasury. Straboromanus, who had then become grand hetairiarch, was therefore sent to ask the man where his assets were hidden. The same people, fearing the logothete’s presence—as the account has already related—persuaded Straboromanus to do away with the man by any means. Having arrived at the island where he was confined, he interrogated the man so severely that he expired under the very tortures, even though he promised that he would disclose everything in writing if he were freed from the examination. And that is how he perished.
XX. The Comneni, Isaac and Alexius, were honored and beloved by the emperor beyond measure, and he named them successors to the empire. For this reason, they were deeply envied by those around Botaniates, and even more so by his aforementioned two servants, who slandered the men to him, alleging that they were plotting a tyranny and undermining and shaking the emperor's disposition toward them. Knowing this, and fearing that they might suffer some harm, and—as rumor has it—already long nurturing within themselves a desire for the empire and having shared the plan with others whom they trusted, they left the city, and having seized the city of Hadrian, they drew to themselves almost the entire military force. Alexius, however, was hailed as emperor, having been preferred over his elder brother, since
the soldiers were more devoted to him as being more skilled in warfare, and since they had been won over by his zeal and promises. Thus proclaimed, Alexius—Isaac not taking the ascent particularly ill—seized the imperial city, having prepared for the siege. Botaniates had stationed guards upon the walls. Now, there were in a certain tower near the city gate, which is called Charsius, guards known as Nemitzi (the Nemitzi are a Celtic race); these had secretly communicated with the associates of the Comneni regarding the betrayal of the city. Therefore, on the morning of Great Thursday of the week of the saving Passion of our Savior, the associates of the Comneni made an assault by agreement upon the outer wall opposite the tower held by the Nemitzi, and those stationed there kept the attackers at bay with missiles. But when those on the walls were struck from the tower by the Nemitzi—being unable to resist simultaneously both those outside and the internal enemy throwing missiles from a higher position—they slackened their attack. B Those who were besieging the walls, however, immediately scaled the wall using ladders, and having broken the locks of the gates with axes, they provided a free entrance to the conspirators. When those guarding the inner fortifications saw this—a confused rabble, mostly inexperienced in war, or rather gathered from the marketplaces and the common populace—some began to descend, while others threw themselves down at great peril and took to flight. Thus, with no one left to resist, the associates of the Comneni occupied the inner enclosure as well; and having likewise thrown open its gates, they made the city accessible from everywhere. The invaders immediately rushed to the plunder of wealth—a motley crowd composed of Thracians, Macedonians, other Romans, and Barbarians—behaving toward their fellow citizens no better than enemies. For the evil proceeded even to the shedding of blood; virgins consecrated to God were impiously defiled, matrons were violated, divine temples were stripped of their ornaments, and not even the sacred vessels were spared. But these brazen men even plundered the bowls containing the bloodless and fearful sacrifice, pouring the Holy Things upon the ground. They dragged senators whom they encountered off their mules, and having stripped some of them, left them half-naked and on foot in the streets. These things were perpetrated throughout the whole day, and the evil, roaming publicly and without discrimination, had reached as far as the Bous; some had already run on to the Philadelphium or even further. The Comneni, as everyone had scattered for the plunder, were left alone with a very small number, and when they reached the Taurus, they did not dare to proceed further, being so stripped of their own proper forces that, had anyone attacked them, they might easily have been captured, bound, and handed over to Botaniates. But the divine decree could no longer be overturned. Wherefore Botaniates, once he learned of the entry of the Comneni, immediately left the palace, and having recklessly cast aside his purple buskins, entered the monastery of Peribleptos, of which he was called the second founder after the Emperor Romanus Argyrus; he shaved his hair, changed his clothing, and put on the monastic cloak. There, having lived for some time, he was buried, dust returned to dust. The Comneni, however, with no one resisting, entered the royal palace without labor, by a flat and open path. Such was the end of his reign.
Such was the beginning of their reign, such their entry into the Queen of Cities, such the auspices of their empire.
XXI. Now that the Comneni had secured their own position, they summoned their mother and their wives from the monastery of the Kanikleion to the royal palace. For as soon as they had initiated their revolt, the women had immediately fled to the great temple of the Word of God, Hagia Sophia. From there, they had been transferred by Botaneiates to the aforementioned monastery, so that they might not be able to send messengers to the rebels, though he caused them no other annoyance. Yet, almost before they had firmly set foot in the empire, they immediately annulled many of Botaneiates’ acts; not beginning from the time they had seized the tyranny, but reaching back beyond it, they abolished many of his decrees as tyrannical in a brief, short-worded document. Then, Alexios was adorned with the imperial diadem, and the second place of honor was assigned to Isaakios, a new title being devised for him—for he was named *Sebastokrator*—and he took precedence even over the *Caesar*. Their mother, because of her monastic habit, partook neither of the imperial insignia nor of the public acclamation and proclamation; she shared only the name of Empress. Nevertheless, she took up the administration of all state affairs no less effectively. Thus, the affairs of the empire were distributed between the two brothers and their mother. They also had two other, younger brothers, whom they declared protagonists: Adrianos as *Protosebastos* and Great Domestic, and Nikephoros as *Sebastos* and Drungarios of the fleet. They also had as brothers-in-law, husbands of their sisters, Nikephoros Melissenos and Michael Taronites. The latter, being joined to their eldest sister, who was very dear to their mother and honored by them, they named *Panhypersebastos*, a title also newly created. Melissenos, however, who had himself defected from Botaneiates in the East and had not yet laid down his arms, they drew to their party by bestowing upon him the honor of *Caesar* and assigning him Thessaloniki as a place to dwell, and they assigned to him, as well as to all the others, revenues equal to such fortune and such a height of dignity. Consequently, with the imperial—or rather, common and public—revenues distributed in this way, the imperial treasury, or public exchequer, was exhausted; and the Emperor, being in need of money, not only abolished the annual donatives that had been customary of old to be given to men adorned with dignity, but even seized the wealth of the senators. D
Caesar, and assigned to him the city of Thessalonica as a residence, and set aside a grant of money for him and all those related to him by blood, in proportion to such a fortune and such weight of dignity. Consequently, as the imperial revenues—or rather, the common and public ones—were distributed in this way, the imperial treasury or the public purse was tightened. And the sovereign, being short of funds, cut off the annual payments that had long belonged to the dignities (12), and took away the pensions of the senators. He also crowned his own partner in life, Irene (13), and named her Augusta; she was the granddaughter of the Caesar John Doukas. Now, the former empress, Maria of the Alans, together with her own son, Constantine Doukas, whom she had borne to Michael Doukas in the purple, and whom his own father had crowned while he was still an infant and wearing the purple buskins, departed from the palace and went off to the imperial residences in the monastery of the Mangana. For she had already received these and the monastery; but indeed she had also received the monastery of the Hebdomon (14) by means of a golden-sealed document from Botaneiates, where she lived with her son, having set aside imperial service for herself and her son. For as yet her child wore the insignia of the empire, and he was acclaimed after the emperor, and he signed the golden-sealed documents after him. But after some time, his mother also changed her clothing, put on black, as is the custom for monks, partly willingly and partly compelled by the tyrant; and the child was stripped of his purple buskins, and the title of autocracy and the empire were left to the Comnenus alone. Such, then, were these matters. But the high priest of the Queen of Cities, that renowned Cosmas (15), disapproving of the handling of public affairs and despairing of any amendment, told his servant to take the psalter and follow him, and withdrew from the Church. Although often urged by the rulers to return, he did not yield, having shepherded the faithful people for five years and nine months. Therefore, they set up another high priest in his place in the Church, a certain eunuch monk named Eustratius, a man who was neither conversant in words nor exercised in the handling of affairs, but a simple man, more suited to a life of quiet and to a corner. And when he had usurped that dignity for three years, he was deposed from the sacred residence by the rulers for a reason not sufficiently known; and the next person appointed in his place was the monk Nicholas, surnamed the Grammarian, who, having spent his life in the study of virtue, was not unversed in letters, C although not exquisitely learned.
Variæ lectiones et notæ.
(12) He cut off the annual payments... (13) Irene: She was the granddaughter of the Caesar John Doukas, the daughter of Andronikos Doukas, the protovestiarios and grand duke of the schools of the East. Thus she is inscribed in the manuscript typikon of the monastery founded by Irene. Andronikos died, as is held therein, in October, when at the end of his life he had put on the monastic habit, being called Antony. His wife was Mary, a noble Bulgarian, who at the end of her life likewise put on the angelic habit and was called Xene, and died in November. Michael was indeed the brother of Irene, who took the monastic habit and was called Antony. Finally, Theodora the nun, sister of the same Irene, died in February. Thus the same Typikon. (14) Of the Hebdomon: See below on the Hebdomon and our Constantinople, where there is more on this monastery and its location. See also the Alexiad, p. 270. Whatever follows concerning the affairs managed by the Emperor Alexios is narrated by Zonaras, which we have set forth more fully in the Notes to the Alexiad of Anna; wherefore here we shall only skim what will seem necessary for illustrating our author. (15) Cosmas: Praised from the codex of the library of Montchal, archbishop of Toulouse, which is now of the most illustrious archbishop of Reims, by which title it could be gathered that Cosmas did not resign the patriarchate voluntarily, but was deposed, although Anna Comnena says the contrary, lib. III, p. 19. The Greeks celebrate his memory on January 9: as it is in the Menaia and Synaxaries, he is called Antony after taking the monastic habit.
XXII. Comnenus, having recently attained power, was informed of the expedition of the Frank Robert, a crafty and most warlike man, against Epidamnus; he himself also set out for that place, but having joined battle he was defeated, fled shamefully, many soldiers not only of the rank and file but also men distinguished in rank and of royal blood were slain, and the entire camp was captured by the enemies. And when the emperor had returned defeated to Byzantium, the Barbarians, puffed up by the victory, marched confidently against all, and took certain towns, among which were Castoria and Larissa. They even dreamed of seizing the Queen of Cities herself. But the emperor took the field again, expelled the Barbarians who were guarding Castoria, and marched against Robert, countering his craftiness with artifice. For B he put the imperial habit upon his brother Hadrian, and having given him an army, he ordered him to stand in battle array against Robert, and, should he incite a fight, to turn his back and flee immediately. These things he commanded to the sham emperor; while he, having traveled with the rest of the army by obscure paths, fell upon the ramparts of the Franks, plundered their tents and everything in them, and wrought a great slaughter. But when Robert made an attack against the sham emperor, he and the army with him fled with loosened reins. For this reason, the Barbarian was even more boastful and arrogant, as if no one dared to engage with him. In the meantime, however, news was brought to him of the destruction of the rampart and the slaughter of those who had been there; immediately, losing both spirit and strength, he withdrew. The emperor returned to Constantinople, and the plots having been detected, he seized the principal authors, among whom were some commanders of legions and many of the commoners and of the conscript fathers, who were thought to be privy to the conspiracy, though most, as it is said, were falsely accused for that reason, so that they might be deprived of their goods. The insults of Robert having been suppressed, enemies of the Roman empire appeared from elsewhere. For the Turks, having subdued the East, did not even abstain from the islands. For Tzachas, a Turk—not one who was illustrious, but a man sharp and of great wickedness—having suddenly captured the island of Chios with a few small ships and having built a fleet there, occupied Lesbos, Samos, Rhodes, and many other islands; from which, however, he was repelled by the Roman fleet. Moreover, the island of Crete and also Cyprus defected, the one occupied by Carice, who stood against the emperor, the other by Rhapsommatos. But these too were recovered for the Roman empire a long time after. From which the emperor, lacking the means to pay, devised ways of making money, by the method of [index: entries preserved]
collecting it, and his appointed officials introduced unjust debts against the people, and those who rightfully owed nothing were stripped of their own possessions. From this, tax-collectors were sent everywhere to register the property of the subjects in the fields and villages, and new names were devised, which I call *Hypertima* (17) and *Hyperplea*, and other modes of harassment against the subjects were introduced from time to time, and sacred items were taken away from holy temples. This emperor, having found the coinage already debased by his predecessors, issued copper currency, which he used for the expenses of the empire, but for taxes he demanded sound gold coins, and sometimes even half-gold pieces, and at other times copper ones. Since he was in need of more copper, he minted the obols into currency, and having torn down some of the public works made of copper, I mean, he struck them into staters, and introduced new tithes. The Augusta gave birth to a daughter for the emperor, who was named Anna after her grandmother, to whom the emperor betrothed Constantine, the son of the Empress Maria, of the Alans; but he having died before the marriage, when the daughter had reached a marriageable age, the emperor provided her another suitor, the eldest son of B Nicephorus Bryennius, who, as the narrative set forth, had attempted a usurpation, and was captured and blinded. Having betrothed this daughter to him, he honored the man with the title of *Panhypersebastos*. A son was also born to the emperor, whom the patriarch baptized with the holy baptism in the divine temple of the Word of God, the Sophia, and he was named John, and the father immediately crowned him with a diadem. Two other sons were born to the emperor, Andronicus and Isaacius, and three other daughters, Maria, Eudoxia, and Theodore. He betrothed Maria to Gregory, the son of that Theodore Gabras, the *sebastos* and martyr. Then, having dissolved this betrothal according to his own whim, and having dismissed him, he adopted as another suitor for this daughter Nicephorus, the son of Catacalon, of the family of Phorbenus. To Eudoxia he joined in marriage the son of Iasites, Constantine. He did not treat his consort as the daughter of an emperor, but acted toward her and conducted himself as if from a superior position; and having very often given offense to the empress and his mother-in-law, he caused her, while the daughter was ailing, to tonsure her immediately, and he drove Iasites out of the palace. Regarding the youngest daughter, the father placed her with a youth not indeed of illustrious family, but endowed with most excellent beauty. Under this emperor, a horrendous earthquake occurred on the day on which the memory of Saint Nicholas, famous for his miracles, is celebrated, and many dwellings, many temples, and many porticoes, by which the public streets are covered, collapsed and crushed a great many mortals. This, moreover, his mother Irene did, being hostile to Iasites, her son-in-law, so that she might remove him from all hope of reconciliation with his wife, since once the monastic garment had been taken by one who was ailing, it was not permitted to put it off, if they were to regain their former health, as we have taught in the *Glossarium mediae Latinitatis* under the entry *Monachus ad succurrendum*.
Finally, the emperor gave the youngest of his daughters in marriage to a youth [(19)] who was a statue in appearance, but not of noble birth. During this emperor’s reign, there occurred a most terrifying earthquake on the day of the feast of the wonder-working Saint Nicholas, by which many houses and churches were razed, as well as the stoas through which the city’s thoroughfares are vaulted, and very many people were buried in the collapses and died.
XXIII. At the same times, the nation of the Patzinaks, having abandoned its own lands, migrated into the Roman provinces and ravaged all of Thrace and Macedonia. When the emperor attacked them in war, he was shamefully defeated because of the arrogance of his soldiers. Then he marched again against the barbarians, and once the army had been humbled and was relying entirely on divine favor, he attacked the enemy. They, not even enduring the first onset, cast their weapons to the ground and with lamentations called upon the Romans for mercy. A great multitude of the Scythian nation perished, while others were taken in chains and sold at a pittance. The emperor, choosing a multitude of robust and sturdy Scythians, settled them with their wives and children in the theme of Moglena [(20)] and formed them into a most distinct regiment; they remain there to this day, having inherited for successive generations the name from the place in which they were settled, and are called Patzinak-Moglenites.
When a conspiracy was revealed to him, led by Umbertopoulon [(21)] and Ariebes—of whom the one drew his lineage from the Franks and Ariebes from the Armenians, and both were men of note and held military commands—they and all who participated with them in the plot were seized and, having been condemned to confiscation of goods and an ignominious triumph, were banished. He also disbanded the legion of the Manichaeans, which until that time had been serving unlawfully, as an ancient law utterly forbade the Manichaeans from soldiering. Again, another plot was hatched against this emperor, the leader of which was the son of that former emperor Diogenes, Nicephorus Porphyrogenitus, a man of truly invincible strength, but not of a similarly fortunate or noble mind and character. Since he had delayed the crime he had begun, he did not carry it out, but suffered for the things he had planned; for he was captured and subjected to interrogation to force him to identify the conspirators, and he was punished with the removal of his eyes and the confiscation of his property. Subsequently, the Frankish nation set out from the West toward the imperial city in order to cross over from there into the East: their movement was heralded by portents. For [a swarm] of locusts...
Variant readings and notes
...Zonaras disapproves of this custom, accepted especially among the Greeks, of taking up the monastic life at the very end of life, or as others wish, Michael Glycas, in his Epistle to the monk Isaiah, from which it is pleasing to insert a few things here: he says, etc. I omit the rest; I observe only that this custom was introduced because they thought that the monastic life could accomplish the same thing as baptism, and just as baptism remits all sins for those who are baptized, so too does the profession of religion itself for those who have entered into it—a view which Zonaras, or Glycas, refutes in the same epistle using many arguments. Eudoxia, therefore, recovering from her illness, retreated to the monastery founded by her mother not far from the church of Sophia, the specific location of which is described in the manuscript *Typikon* of that same monastery in the final chapter, and she died while her parents were still living, as we gather from the same *Typikon*. [(19)] Constantine Angelos, a nobleman of Philadelphia, betrothed the youngest of his daughters, Theodora, to Alexius, whom he honored with the dignity of *panhypersebastohypertatos*, as we are taught by the same manuscript *Typikon* of the monastery. [(20)] Regarding the theme of Moglena. See the notes on the *Alexiad*, p. 297. [(21)] Umbertopoulon. One of the royal codices has this; others have Humbertopoulon, whose lineage and family we discuss in the Notes on the *Alexiad*.
the shoot of the purple, a man matchless in strength, but not having acquired the constitution of his mind and the nobility of his soul in proportion to his bodily strength.
