This man, having organized the drama but putting off the action, received what he suffered or did, as he had planned. For he was caught, and having been delivered to the necessities of torture in order to reveal the conspirators, eventually his eyes were put out, and he was deprived of his wealth. Then, the nation of the Franks, having been stirred up from the Western lands, marched toward the Queen of Cities, as if to cross over from it to the East; a certain divine sign foreshadowed their movement. For an infinite number of locusts, having had their origin from the West, and so numerous that they could be likened to flying clouds and obscuring the sun, made their flight through the greatest of cities and its borders, and were rushing toward the East, and there they settled. Having crossed, therefore, and attacking Nicaea in Bithynia, which was held by the Turks, after a long time and with great slaughter on both sides, they nevertheless captured the city and surrendered it to the emperor for a great deal of money. They proceeded forward. And some captured Antioch, which is by the Orontes, with great labor and slaughter, while others gained control of the city of Jerusalem after great struggles. But these were the affairs of the nation of the Franks, to speak succinctly.
certain disgusting heresy appeared during these times, that of the Bogomils. The herald and great teacher, and the leader of the apostles among them, was a certain man called Basil, a physician, wearing the habit of a monk, but clothed with Satan himself; he, having learned the destructive dogmas of this error in fifteen years, and having taught them for fifty-two, filled almost the whole world with his own pestilence. Then he also entered the Queen of Cities, but the enemy of the truth was caught in the act. For the sovereign, having known his impiety, pretended to have faith in the wretched man, and called him teacher and savior of erring souls. Through these and many other things, with difficulty and slowly, he finally brought it about that the pestilential man vomited forth the poison of his own wickedness; and once the wild beast was entangled in the nets, he made public his doctrine, despised his dogmas, and by a common vote ordered the impostor to be consumed by fire in the Hippodrome. Furthermore, another insurrection arose against the emperor, from a certain author who claimed to be the son of the Emperor Diogenes, who, having secured a multitude of Comans, came to the regions of Thrace, and plundering and looting the land, demanded that those in the cities receive him and acclaim him emperor. Then, having gone to a small town in Thrace, and having been deceived by those in it, and having been acclaimed, and having entered with a few men, he was seized and had his eyes put out. And thus the tyranny attempted by him was dissolved, the Comans who survived having returned to their own lands.
XXIV. This emperor, with the orphanotropheion and...
other gerocomia—having settled the possessions of all upon the orphanotropheion and assigned others from his own means—restored it immediately, and renovated it at great expense, and placed therein a multitude of those receiving old-age care. Moreover, he built dwellings for monks and nuns within it; and besides these, a school of grammarians, in which orphan children or those of poor parents might be taught, having appointed teachers and pedagogues for it, and having arranged for the sustenance of both those who were teaching and those who were learning, which things are observed up to this day.
Another conspiracy was again formed against the emperor, the leader of which was Michael Anemas, but many of the first dignitaries of the military were accomplices. But before they could set about the deed, having been discovered and arrested, they had the hair of their heads and beards removed, not with a razor, but with a depilatory. Anemas, however, was condemned to the plucking out of his eyes, but while the triumph over them was being celebrated, the imperial edict was interposed and he was freed from the punishment of blinding. And thus, being fined by the confiscation of their property, they were scattered here and there.
Various readings and notes
(24) Anemas Michael. These Constantinopolitans traced their descent from Anemas Burupa, a Cretan, the son of Ameras, who died fighting bravely against the Russians under John Tzimiskes; Scylitzes extols his military prowess on pages 678 and 681. Lambecius, Book V of his *Commentaries on the Imperial Library*, p. 233, praises the Consolatory Epistle of Gregory, abbot of the monastery on the island of Oxia. Furthermore, he is called Manuel in the iambic verses of an uncertain author in the Royal Codex, under this title: *Anemas*, in which he is celebrated primarily for his greatness of spirit and his noble deeds, as in these verses:
[Here follows the lamentation for the lady Theodora Porphyrogenita upon the death of Manuel Anemas, the Lord Panhyperprotosebastos, and his eulogy as a brother-in-law to the emperors:]
Noble general, great light of the Romans.
You were deprived of the great general,
The truly adamant blade,
The brazen rampart of Byzantium.
Lost to you is the trench of the scepter,
Gone from you is the support of the monarchy,
Your copper-dyed spear has been shattered,
And the blade that slaughtered barbarians has been broken.
O vast mind, and breadth of counsel,
Height of fortune, unyielding, mass of dignity,
The ornament of character, most acute nature,
And tireless one in the governance of battle;
The royal gaze, the sovereign aspect,
Strength of arm, valor of heart,
And the prime example worthy of tyranny.
Mourn, O thrice-blessed purple,
Your fair sprout, your golden gift,
Beholding Theodora in black attire.
For her own [light-bringer] shone together with her,
And set together with her in the garments of mourning.
And having gathered many of the earth’s treasures in heaven,
And having tested this hair below,
And having put on the hair-shirt,
You did not find a better end there.
We, who composed paeans and wedding songs
For the marriage of your children,
Were destined to weep for you also in death.
Daughters, mourn the one who planted the seed,
O joint-in-law assembly, you co-masters,
Mourn the breach in the quaternary;
For the four-sided phalanx has been torn away,
The brotherly concord of the purple,
Measure the sighing of your sister.
Brothers-in-law, surrender the whole panoply,
For it did not ward off the mother-in-law’s harm.
King Manuel, purple-born branch,
If ever you sighed in the present grief,
And [now] with many-fold tears, not in the way
You were deprived of the great general.
From these verses we are finally taught whose daughter the Theodora Porphyrogenita was, namely John Comnenus’, of whom writers mention only three—the ones married to Roger of Sicily the Caesar, another to Stephen Contostephanus, and the third to Theodore Vatatzes—to whom a fourth, Theodora, must be added; consequently, she was the sister of Manuel Comnenus, who was then reigning when Manuel Anemas died. The same poet:
For Manuel, nearing death, had put on the monastic or angelic habit, as was customary for Greeks of that age (25).
(25) Depilatory, the ointment by which hair is removed; such is that substance composed of it, which Peter Belonius mentions in his *Observations*, Book III, chapter 33, by which women among the Turks pull out the hair of their private parts. See what the critics adduce regarding this word.
His head and beard, however, were kept untouched, but the deprivation of his eyes had been decreed. While they were still celebrating their triumph, a royal decree arrived, exempting Anemas from the penalty and granting him the cutting off of his eyes. After being deprived of their property, they were sentenced to exile in different places. The mother of this emperor, having undertaken, as has been said, the administration of state affairs, managed them for a long time; and for this reason, most people attributed to her the evils which were happening to the subjects at that time. Now, the emperor was vexed, as he was enjoying the title of emperor almost in name only, but he stood in need of his mother, and although she heard this, she did not wish to be deprived of her power. But when she perceived that her son was displeased, fearing lest she might be forced and appear to have made her departure against her will, she yielded everything to her son and emperor, and withdrew from the palace, making her residence the monastery of the Pantepoptes, which she herself had built. Having lived there for some years honorably and as became an empress, and having reached a ripe old age, she departed this life. And the sebastocrator, having lived for a year and a little more after his mother, also paid the debt of nature, having shaved his hair and put on the monastic habit when he knew that the end of his life was at hand, leaving behind many children; the emperor took charge of them, as their mother also, not having survived her husband for even a full year, had departed the world. He gave wives to the young men and husbands to the maidens, and he cherished and benefited his nephews. Now, as to the companion of his life, this emperor was neither averse to her at first, nor did he excessively love her, but being given to passion, he was said to observe the laws of matrimony with little sanctity: whence the Augusta was wounded by the darts of jealousy. But as a greater age had somewhat blunted those fiery arrows of lust, he then turned his love toward the Augusta, and rested entirely in her affection, nor could he easily be torn away from her. From that time on, the empress possessed great power, even while the emperor was still in good health. But when he began to suffer in his feet, so that he could not walk, and corrupted humors flowed to his joints, he lay in bed: she herself ruled, with him approving everything, and she was already turning over in her mind how, after her husband's death, the whole power and administration of the empire might be transferred to herself, so that she might have the emperor-son obedient to her commands; but for him, who was already a man and had long ago been joined to a wife, the daughter of the leader of the Hungarians, and had begotten children, this was not to be endured. For he feared lest he might be excluded from the empire or even deprived of life, as he perceived with what love his mother pursued her eldest daughter and her son-in-law Bryennius. Therefore, he met with his kinsmen, and even with the people, and by bewailing privately to each one the state of his affairs, he reminded sworn men of their oaths; and many of them, having been obligated, had promised that they would not bestow the empire upon anyone but him. They, indeed, encouraged him, promising aid and confirming what they promised with an oath. When these things did not escape the notice of the empress, she was enraged and issued an edict that no one should meet with him, and she scattered spies and observers everywhere. Nevertheless, he did not cease to try to draw almost everyone to himself, some through himself, others through those devoted to him. Moreover, he possessed the support of his younger brother too.
she came to realize the truth, and she perceived the event to be a good omen. When he had already passed beyond the age of childhood, his father ended his life, and his mother was hard pressed by the hardships of poverty and the additional evils of widowhood. Being without any means of subsistence, he decided to hire out his labor to someone in return for a wage. He set out, therefore, for the great city, and arriving in the evening, he entered through the gate of the city which is called the Golden A Gate; he lay down near the church of Saint Diomedes and fell asleep. During the night, the martyr appeared in a dream to the verger of the church (for there was not yet a monastery there) and ordered him to bring the emperor inside. The verger went out, and seeing Basil sleeping on the bare ground, he supposed the dream to be idle fancy. When the saint appeared to be giving the same order again, he went out a second time; finding no one but Basil, he returned with the same opinion. Yet a third time the martyr appeared to the verger, and urged him to bring inside the man who was lying out of doors. "For this," he said, "is the emperor." The verger therefore went out, raised Basil, welcomed him into his own living quarters, and shared with him what he had. Now the verger had a brother who was a physician in the service of Theophilitzes. This Theophilitzes was related by blood to the Caesar Bardas and the Emperor Michael; because of his very small stature, he was called by the diminutive form, Theophilitzes. The verger, therefore, revealed the secret dream to his brother and requested that he recommend this man to one of the officials for service; he accordingly introduced him to his own master. But first, both brothers communicated the prophetic decree about the kingdom to Basil, and bound him with oaths that, once he attained power, he would remember them. Theophilitzes, being pleased with Basil (for he was handsome, tall, brave of hand, and agile, and he wore his hair long and curly), placed him in charge of his own stable-boys; the one who performs this duty is commonly called the "protostrator." The emperor, too, was looking for a brave and skillful stable-boy. For a horse had been brought to the Emperor Michael from somewhere, a horse possessing every virtue befitting a mount, but it was stiff-necked and not easily ridden. When the emperor came to inspect it, he ordered someone to mount it. Though many attempted to do so, it was fierce and insolent, and it threw them all. This was bothersome to the emperor, who was indignant that he lacked a suitable stable-boy. At that point, Theophilitzes himself said that he had such a person, and that if the emperor wished, he could be present. By his order, therefore, Basil was summoned. He seized the bridle, calmed the horse with a chirping sound and by stroking it, then lightly swinging himself up, he mounted. After initially letting it move a little, then stimulating it with a whip, he rode off at a run. Delighted by this, the emperor enrolled Basil among the imperial stable-boys and, as time progressed, designated him protostrator—a position that is honorable and not alienated from the emperor's familiarity. Moreover, the emperor's benevolence toward him grew day by day, to the extent that he even became a source of envy for the Caesar.
The other, however—namely Andronicus A (27)—was opposed to his own brother and the emperor. For by this time they, too, had reached manhood and had been joined to wives. Andronicus was honored as *sebastocrator*, Isaac and Bryennius as *Caesars*, while the husband of the emperor's second daughter, Phorbenus, was *panhypersebastos*. Matters stood thus, even if, out of chronological order, we have related later events first.
XXV. Now, one of the Latins who had set out from the West to the East was the son of that Robert whom history has already mentioned, named Bohemond. He offered his service to the emperor and, having established pacts with an oath and received money in an almost uncountable amount, he settled in Antioch of Coele-Syria. Having taken it by siege, he not only violated the signed treaties and agreements but also meditated on waging war against the Romans. Indeed, as he planned his return to the West, he feared that in crossing through Roman provinces he might be seized by some governor and led in chains to the emperor as a treaty-breaker; what then does he devise? He arranges for a rumor of his own death to be spread, and placing himself in a coffin, he instructs his servants to say that his corpse was being carried home. By this ruse, he returned to his own country and, having complained much about the emperor to many of his fellow countrymen, he stirred them up to take arms against the Romans. Appointed as their leader, he crossed the sea and strove with great zeal to capture Epidamnos. But when the siege was drawn out, and after great damage had been inflicted on the citizens and no fewer harms had been received in return, he despaired of the taking of the city and advanced to other places with equally futile effort. Finally, he sought peace from the emperor through envoys, the latter being at that time in Thessalonica. The emperor, having departed from there, fortified his camp near the European Coloneia, where he also conferred with the Barbarian and arranged the terms of peace. And the Bohemondic war was settled in nearly this way.
The Patriarch Nicholas, having governed the Church for twenty-seven years, died while extremely elderly. The emperor honored his funeral with magnificent rites and installed another upon the patriarchal throne, a man from the number of the clergy of the Church, but of the order of deacons, and one of the patriarchal officials, the nephew—through his brother—of him who then presided over the Chalcedonian Church, a man well-versed from an early age in both profane and sacred letters, whom the emperor himself declared in the Church. Some time...
later, the sovereign fell ill, and the disease mastered him to such a degree that it seemed he had passed away. Yet when the holy veil, which hangs before the image of our Savior erected in the Chalke, was brought and spread out upon the bed on which the sufferer lay, and touched his body, the intensity of the disease was somewhat mitigated, and he moved, sat up, spoke, asked for water to wash his hands, and tasted food. And while he began to recover, the disease began to wane. To the majority, however, this recovery was incredible. To provide full assurance to those who doubted it, he rode on horseback through the forum shortly thereafter. Thus was the emperor delivered from that disease. But again an idle fear crept into the city-dwelling populace; for a rumor was circulating everywhere that the emperor was fated to die on the Great Sabbath of that year. This rumor held sway not only over the common people, but also over those in positions of authority and those around the sovereign, and it disturbed even the soul of the emperor himself, and this though he was not sick nor suffering from any affliction, until that day passed and the vanity of that report was refuted, and the fear of evils was removed from everyone.
XXVI. Not long after these events, the emperor departed from Byzantium, taking the women’s quarters with him, even though it was the winter season, and he encamped in the Thracian Chersonese. When winter had passed and spring began to shine, and summer was already at hand, the sovereign did not move from there until the empress was struck by disease. Then the Augusta was sent ahead in the imperial trireme. The emperor remained staying there for a while, reviewing the military rolls, until he learned that the empress had arrived at the Palace. Leaving there, he reached the Great City in a single day. As she recovered, he turned his attention to the administration of affairs, spending most of his time at the Philopation. He pursued a practice of this kind: he set appointed days on which he sat in public there before a tribunal, overlooking a wide plain. Access to him was permitted to anyone who wished, and each of those in need was allowed to present their petitions to him. He ordered these to be placed before him, read by the secretaries, the request of each person to be stated, and the responses to be written down immediately and, once confirmed, returned to the petitioners. Having observed this procedure for a sufficiently long time, he went out again with the empress and the women’s quarters, and dwelt with his whole family near the foothills of Mt. Papycius throughout the entire winter.
Having spent the winter season of that time there with his whole household, he departed thence as soon as spring began, and arrived at Philippopolis. Having pitched his camp in the vicinity of this city, the Emperor Alexios spent there the time of spring, summer, and a good part of autumn as well, and his occupation during his stay there was a disputation with the Manichaeans, whom the common speech names Paulicians (for a great multitude of this race had settled in that region, King John Tzimiskes having moved them from the East and re-settled them there); by debating with them, he brought many over to the orthodox faith.
Now, when autumn was already half-spent, he returned to the imperial palace. The empress had become very powerful, and her son-in-law, the Caesar Bryennios, possessed great influence, and by his nod everything in the palace was managed; consequently, all were approaching him, and he was entrusted with the power of judging and rendering justice with imperial authority. For the man was devoted to the study of letters, and his wife, no less—or rather, even more—than he, applied herself to the same pursuits; she Atticized exquisitely and was endowed with an extremely sharp intellect for the most abstruse contemplations, having obtained that capacity partly through natural goodness and partly through diligence. She was also devoted to books and did not merely dabble in the company of the learned. With his affairs in such a state, the Caesar was celebrated on all sides. Though these things vexed and tormented the Emperor’s son, who was himself also emperor, he nevertheless endured them; but these matters will be explained in the progress of the narrative.
For now, let those things which were omitted be recounted. In the days of this emperor, many conflagrations occurred, laying waste to various parts of the city with fire. Moreover, at a certain time in the spring, a violent storm wind caused many things to collapse, and among them the statue set upon that great porphyry column in the round square which they call the Placotum; it fell and crushed many who happened to be there. That statue, both huge in size and wondrous in its beauty, was shattered into many fragments by that disaster. At another time, a very great downpour erupted on the day when the memory of the princes of the apostles, Peter and Paul, is celebrated; beginning in the evening, it lasted without intermission until the same hour the next day. Then, by the force of the waters, many buildings were destroyed, and the valleys, overflowing with water, were no different from the sea, and not a few men and many animals perished. And these things happened in this way.
XXVII. When it was reported to the emperor that a Turkish army was being gathered to invade the Roman provinces and plunder them, and he himself was suffering from gout in his feet, the flows having surged more violently and pressing upon him with most acute pains and agony, he could not, in person, make a military expedition against the Barbarians. He therefore sent a phalanx of soldiers against them, placing at their head Eustathios Camitzes, a man counted among the foremost of his leaders. This man, having engaged a not insignificant portion of the enemy, was defeated and captured, his soldiers being in part slain and in part saved by flight. When this was reported to the emperor, he could no longer be restrained from setting out himself; but he did not encounter the Turks anywhere, for, having heard of his approach, they had immediately loosened their reins and fled. But he saw Camitzes, whom he had persuaded with the hope of great rewards to desert to him along with his guards, and he gladly received him, and, having bestowed money upon his liberators, he returned to Byzantium.
Not much time passed before he undertook another expedition against the Barbarians and captured the city of Philomelion, which he found empty of armed men (for all, having learned of his approach, had departed after taking their most precious belongings), without any labor. But he also received by surrender certain fortresses and caves, which the inhabitants call *Cataphygia*—that is, "refuges"—and, because it seemed advantageous to do so, he took them with him into the colonies, as there was no one left with whom he might wage war. Those who were of sound age and vigor of strength made the journey on foot; to those who were slower in walking due to old age, he provided pack animals. If any were afflicted with diseases or other calamities, or were exhausted from old age and lethargic from infirmity, he ordered them to be carried on shields by robust youths who were equal to the task of bearing them, and he himself, riding alongside, if he saw any of them suffering from thirst or desiring food, or otherwise in a bad way, would refresh them as best he could. As he was thus proceeding and detouring, the Western Turks met him, saying that their prince wished to strike a treaty with the Romans. As this did not displease the emperor, in the morning the prince of the Barbarians came—not a satrap, but one of the sultan’s line, who was himself called *sultan* by his own people.
He met the emperor on horseback, but dismounted some distance away, approached on foot, and in this manner performed his obeisance to him. Treaties were established, and having been honored with large sums of money, he and those who had come with him departed, he himself returning to his own customs, and the emperor returning to the palace. From that time on, the pain in his feet increased, and he was bedridden for the most part. The empress took the lead, and she seemed likely to usurp the sole authority. But her son, who was also emperor, did not remain quiet; he resisted his mother’s perceived attempt in every way.
[XXVIII.] As the emperor was worn down by his illness, he was transferred to the great palace that is toward the east, where he was under the care of physicians. The gathering of the Asclepiadae around him took place daily. There was not one diagnosis among them all; at length, however, they finally arrived at one opinion, which was to cauterize the emperor’s stomach with iron. Thus, the emperor received the burning iron against his stomach. The air of the palace did not seem temperate to the physicians, as it was somewhat too southern. Therefore, the ruler was moved to the Mangana palace, whether this came about by chance or by the Fates driving the dying man to exhale his soul there. For those who marvel at such things interpreted the phrase "He shall fall in Acesodyna" as referring to him, for they explained and called the residence of the Mangana "Acesodyna" on account of the medical workshop that was there, as though it were a remedy for severe pains. Indeed, those who are fond of such studies argued that the cautery was not applied to the emperor rashly, but that this too was done according to an ancient foresight and prediction. For it is written somewhere about it by those who described the fates of kings wrapped in riddles, that he would fall "burnt by a hook," as those writers likened the iron with which a stomach is wont to be cauterized to a hook, because of its curved point. But neither the cauterization nor the change of place did the sick man any good; gradually, toward the end,
his strength failed him, nor did the physicians say his life was despaired of; and certain monks predicted to him that he would not otherwise depart unless he first arrived at Jerusalem to worship at the life-giving sepulcher of our Lord and Savior, where they prophesied that he would lay down his crown. He believed these promises; for we are wont easily to credit that for which we have a desire. Thus, deluded, he did not notice that he had reached the end of his life before he reached Jerusalem, though he laid down his crown—yet not willingly. It might be said that he departed to the Jerusalem above, the mother city of the firstborn, whose names are inscribed in heaven. The Augustus was in the fifteenth day of the eleventh indiction, and the Augustus was breathing his last; he lay there, drawing breath frequently and feebly. The empress was entirely given over to grief, and her daughters were gathered around her. The greater part of the day had already passed, and the sun was not standing over his head, nor, so to speak, at its zenith, but was inclined somewhat toward the west; and it was signified to the emperor’s son and emperor that his father was breathing his last. He therefore entered the house where the dying man lay, not to lament the departing, but to be confirmed in the news of his death; and having seen him, he immediately went out, and mounting his horse, left the Mangana with those who were with him, and many who were leaving with him followed. As he had just left the enclosure of the Mangana, the Abasgi met him—these were they who had been sent from Abasgia with the girl brought to be wed to the eldest of the Caesar's sons—and having met him, they raised a shout and made obeisance. Then they say that the empress tearfully reported the son’s departure to the dying emperor, and that he answered nothing to this, either not wishing or not able, but lifted up his hands—I know not whether praying for or against his son. Others deny that he lifted his hands, for it was not possible for one already expiring to do so; but that when the empress repeatedly cried out that his son was departing to wrest the empire from him while he still lived, he smiled slightly and faintly, either deriding that speech, if it were thought that he, already expiring and about to leave earthly things, was still anxious about the empire, or indicating what he felt about the act. For it was said by others and by the Porphyrogenitus emperor himself that he had not entered the palace without his father’s counsel, but that his departure had been granted to him by his father, and that he had received his father’s ring as a token of this; and that these things were done while the empress was away and ignorant of it. When, therefore, he departed, the rumor of what had been done spreading quickly, many kin, and many from the military tribunes and the senate, hastened to this emperor. D
Various readings and notes.
He shall hold power for twenty-eight years,
As one already dead and viewed as a spectacle,
Many know, and do not even see this one,
Who, having been revealed such from drunkenness,
Shall hold the scepter of this empire,
And again shall come its power of six-winged [might].
XXIX. But as he was hurrying toward the great palace, it was reported to him that the Varangians, having occupied the road to the guardhouse where they had their quarters, would not allow anyone to pass that way, or even to approach the palace. Perturbed by this news, he sent someone to them to inquire what they intended. He also sent to the Church to say that the Emperor was dead, and he asked to be acclaimed himself as emperor; this was achieved, and the clergy of the Church, with the consent of the Chief Shepherd, proclaimed him emperor. The person sent to the Varangians asked them for what reason they forbade the Emperor passage into the palace. They replied that they would never yield the palace to another while the Emperor was still alive. He insisted that the Emperor was dead. The Varangians demanded that he confirm this to them by an oath. He swore that the Emperor was indeed dead, and they yielded and granted the Emperor passage. He went on to the palace, and having entered it, he deliberated with those about him how he should treat his mother, his brothers and sisters, and his brother-in-law, Bryennius; for he still feared them, and held them in suspicion, fearing they might attempt some revolution.
For his father was still breathing throughout that entire day, and was dying with difficulty, passing away only toward evening. He lived about seventy years, and reigned for thirty-seven years, four months, and some days. He died in the year of the world 6626, having administered the empire fortunately, but having met with an end to his life that was not equally prosperous. For he was deserted by almost all his attendants, so that there was scarcely anyone to cleanse his corpse with the final ablutions, nor was there any imperial ornamentation at hand whereby his body might be adorned with the dignity due his station, nor was there a funeral conducted for him fitting an emperor. And this was not by a stranger, but by his son who succeeded to the empire—and that a son whom he himself had honored with the succession. Thus, there is nothing in human affairs that is stable, firm, or trustworthy, but all things are faithless, and more mutable than dice.
The man himself was—in order that we may characterize his manner for those who come after—neither haughty nor arrogant, nor quick to anger, nor was he enslaved by money, nor so overly greedy for wealth as to wish to bury and hoard it, so that there would be treasures hidden for him, and subterranean riches—whence, even after his death, no great amount of money was found in the treasuries. He was inclined toward mercy, slow to inflict punishment, moderate in character, easy to approach, not intemperate in his manner of living, and not given to wine. He was accustomed to listen to and respect those possessing virtue. He cultivated learning, not as was fitting, but in his own way; he was accommodating, not insolent toward those with whom he lived, but affable and pleasant, as if he were living among equals. Whence they were of untroubled mind, and did not stand there stunned with fear, especially in the absence of the Empress, who bore herself with a certain imperiousness and severity, and would sharply rebuke those who forgot their modesty. And these things are indeed noble; for who would deny it? And they are such as might complete the praises of a private citizen, yet they are not such as might demonstrate an entirely good emperor. For in a private man, moderation, equity, and placability are sufficient, and in every—
respect he should not be difficult to move to anger, and should possess sobriety in his way of life. But for an emperor, in addition to these, there is the concern for justice, the provision for his subjects, and the preservation of the ancient customs of the state.
But his concern was rather for the alteration of the ancient customs of the state, and to reorganize these was his most earnest task. He did not treat affairs as common or public, and he considered himself not merely their administrator, but their master, and he thought of and called the imperial palace his own private house. He did not grant the members of the senate the honor they deserved, nor did he pay them the attention that right reason demanded; rather, he endeavored to debase them. Neither did he observe the rule of justice in all things, the proper function of which is to distribute to each what he deserves. He, however, provided public money, whole wagonloads of it, to his relatives and some of his servants, and assigned them generous annual grants, so that they might abound in great wealth and maintain an entourage suitable not for private individuals, but for emperors, and acquire houses equal to cities in size, and not at all unequal to the royal palaces in magnificence. Toward the rest of the nobility, he did not show himself similarly kindly—lest I say something else about the man, whose honor I wish to consult. These things do not allow the Comnenian [emperor] to be judged a perfect emperor. Nor, however, would anyone call the Comnenian emperor bad; for if one should inquire more strictly into the emperors, I do not believe one would find any among those who held the Roman princedom in antiquity in whom nothing is lacking for consummate praise. Whence it happens that anyone's government is judged by the greater part of their character and actions. Certainly no one will be found to be completely free of blame and of inferior inclinations; for this would be proper to divine blessedness, and would never fall to human nature.
Here I shall make an end of writing and shall check the course of my history, which has progressed a long way. For I have judged that it is neither useful nor timely to commit to writing what still remains. But if this work shall seem useful to anyone, let there be thanks to God, by whose aid all good things are accomplished. But if it is superfluous, the fault shall be mine, and may the product return to me, its author, who might stir up its memory.
EPITOME OF THE ANNALS OF LIB. XVIII
Regarding cult and manner of life, there is a limit. But for an emperor, in addition to these, justice must be cultivated, the concerns of the citizens must be kept in mind, and ancient institutions must be guarded. He, however, labored more in this, using the utmost effort, to change and abolish ancient customs; and he treated affairs not as common and public, and did not consider himself their administrator, but their master, and he thought of and called the imperial palace his own house. He neither treated the senators with the honor that was just, nor gave them the consideration that right reason required, but rather strove to suppress them. Nor did he observe the rule of justice in all things, the proper function of which is to distribute to each what he deserves. He, however, bestowed onto his relatives and certain ministers whole wagonloads of public money and provided most generous annual expenditures, so that they might abound in great wealth and feed a retinue suitable not for private men but for emperors, and might acquire houses matching cities in magnitude, and not unequal to royal palaces in magnificence. Toward the rest of the nobility, he did not show himself so benign, lest I say something else of the man, whose honor I wish to consult. These things do not allow the Comnenian to be judged a perfect emperor. Nor yet would anyone say that the Comnenian emperor was bad: for if one should inquire more severely into the emperors, I do not believe he will find any who possessed the Roman principate in antiquity in whom nothing is lacking for finished glory. Whence it comes about that anyone's governance is judged by the greater part of his morals and actions. Indeed, certainly no one will be found entirely free of faults and of baser passions: for that would be the property of divine happiness, and it would never fall into human nature. Here I will make an end of writing, and will inhibit the course of history, which has progressed far. For I judged that to commit to letters those things which remain would be neither useful nor timely. But if this work shall seem useful to anyone, let there be thanks to God, by whose help all good things are perfected. But if it be superfluous, the fault is mine, and let the product return to me, the author, whose memory it might excite.
VII. When the emperor at one time had his mother to dinner, at her request Basilius the protostrator was also summoned. The empress kept looking at him with fixed eyes, and having scrutinized him more curiously, as if inspired by a divine power, she said that he was the plague of her family; and that she knew this from certain signs formerly made known to her by her husband. But Michael considered this nonsense, and allowed nothing to lead him away from his benevolence toward Basilius, and he even appointed him cubicularius. This caused the Caesar's envy toward Basilius to grow, so that he began to plot against the man, and was in turn plotted against by him. When an expedition was proclaimed by Michael against the Agarenes who held Crete, the Caesar went to the temple of the Theotokos, called the Hodegetria, to bid farewell to the Mother of God. And as he was already approaching the sanctuary, for no evident reason his chlamys slipped from his shoulders. This sign seemed inauspicious both to the Caesar and to those who saw it. Since many were blaming the emperor for having surrendered all power to the Caesar, so that he might do whatever he wished and perform many things contrary to duty, through the persistence of these reproaches that dull man seemed at length to awake somewhat from his deep lethargy, and he corrected and rescinded certain things that had been ordained by the Caesar. This was intolerable to Bardas, who bore the diminution of his authority heavily and with an ill spirit. Nevertheless, he accompanied the emperor who had set out on the expedition; Basilius and his associates frequently denounced him to Michael, and the plot against him was already prepared; but those to whom the task had been committed, fearing the wealth and power of the Caesar, held back. It happened one morning that Bardas, having emerged from his own tent with great splendor and a large retinue, approached the emperor, and sitting beside him, conversed with him. Then Basilius, standing behind him, threateningly shook his hand. When the Caesar suddenly looked around for some other reason, and caught sight of the hand of Basilius, and understood what was intended, he threw himself at the feet of the emperor. Those who had conspired against him were hesitant to make the attempt, until Basilius himself was the first to strike him with his sword. For so, the others taking courage, surrounded Bardas and cut him to pieces, and thus such was the end that befell his life. The emperor, however, abandoning the expedition, returned to Byzantium and betrothed to Basilius Eudocia Ingerina, who had long been his own concubine. Afterwards, he even designated him emperor in the Great Church, and had him crowned with the diadem by the patriarch Photius. To Basilius, a son Leo was born of Eudocia, who was considered to be Michael’s.
Variæ lectiones et notæ.
(63) Concerning this word, see the Gloss. med. Graecit. We have spoken at length about the temple of the Hodegetria in our *Constantinopolis*, book IV.
(64) Concerning Bardas having been cut to pieces, see Nicetas the Paphlagonian in *Vita Ignatii patr. Constantinopol*. Below: Since Michael was hastening toward Crete, and Bardas himself was marching with him as far as the so-called Gardens, there he paid the ultimate penalty; for the divine wrath overtook him on the pretext that he was plotting against the emperor, and he was miserably cut to pieces, limb by limb, by swords. Then the autokrator returned immediately to the imperial city.
Relinquishing his expedition, the emperor returned to Byzantium and joined Eudocia, the daughter of Inger, to Basil—she who had previously been a concubine to him who was then reigning. Then he himself proclaimed this man as emperor, having arrived at the Great Church (65), and through the patriarch Photius, the diadem was placed upon him. A male child, Leo, was born to Basil from Eudocia, although it was said that he was actually the son of Michael (66), as Eudocia was pregnant when she was joined to Basil. But Michael, always devoting himself to horse racing and symposia, and constantly getting drunk and deranged (67)—a state he was unable to control even when sober—incited Basil to kill him. For after a horse race took place at Saint Mamas, in which he himself and his dignitaries had driven and he had won, he reclined for a banquet, and both Basil and Eudocia reclined with him. A certain Basilinus (68), who had formerly been a rower on an imperial trireme but had been admitted into the emperor’s inner circle for his physical beauty and had become intimate with him, was standing by while the emperor was dining and began to praise him for having driven so skillfully and artfully. D The emperor (for he had already been drinking heavily and his mind was clouded) was pleased by the praises and gave him his purple-red sandals and ordered him to put them on, and proclaimed him emperor. When the man hesitated and looked toward Basil, the emperor became angry. Basil nodded to him to obey and put on the sandals; and he took them and put them on. Then the emperor, angry at Basil, said, “These imperial insignia befit him more than you. Is it not permitted for me to make another, just as I made you emperor?” From this, Basil (68), fearing both for his position and his life, pondered how to act before he suffered harm, and shared his plan with others. When Michael, having again indulged in heavy drinking at the banquet, was already intoxicated and was being led to his own bedchamber in the palaces at Saint Mamas (69), and had lain down and was overcome by a very deep sleep, Basil, first of all, broke the locks of the imperial bedchamber, lest the door be locked by those attending the imperial bedchamber. Then he arrived with his fellow conspirators. But even as the few who were keeping watch before the bedchamber tried to hinder the attackers, a tumult arose; the emperor was awakened and, with a drawn sword in his hand, stood and raised his hands, which were both cut off, and he retreated toward the others. But Michael, staggering from the wine and hindered by his drunkenness, could not flee and lay there crying out miserably. Another one of the Basilians, seeing that he was still alive, pounced on him and drove his sword into his chest in such a way that, with his belly also pierced, his intestines spilled out.
Thus did Michael’s life depart, in a manner worthy of his conduct. Basilius immediately arrived at the palace, and having taken control of it, he sent one of the chamberlains to provide for Michael’s funeral. He went, found the wretch wrapped in a horse’s saddle cloth, and, having led him away, buried him in the monastery of Chrysopolis. Michael had reigned with his mother for fourteen years, and had ruled alone for eleven.
Once Basilius had seized the throne and been proclaimed emperor by all, he summoned the leading men of the Senate and opened the imperial treasuries, in which nothing was found but a mere three centenaria of gold. After the matter was deliberated, it was decreed by the consent of all that those who had received money without reasonable cause should return it, or at least half of it. Consequently, as the half was exacted by this decree, no less than three hundred centenaria were brought into the treasury. When Basilius went to the Great Church on a feast day, wishing to partake of the unbloody sacrifice, he was barred by the patriarch Photius, who called him a murderer. Enraged by this rebuff, he convened a council and expelled Photius from the Church, under the pretext that the holy Ignatius had been removed from the episcopal throne illegally by Bardas Caesar, and that Photius had occupied the divine throne uncanonically while Ignatius was still alive. Using this as a pretext, he restored the holy Ignatius to the apex of the pontificate.
When Basilius had recently taken hold of the scepter, some of the patricians—Georgios and Sabbatios—opposed him; they were discovered and seized, and paid the penalty with their bodies. He adorned his three sons, Constantine, Leo, and Alexander, with imperial crowns. Wishing to make his fourth, the youngest, Stephen, patriarch, he enrolled him into the ranks of the clergy of the Church of God. He placed his four daughters in the imperial monastery of Saint Euphemia. He campaigned against the Agarenes in Crete and was defeated; many were killed, and he himself would have been captured had he not been rescued by Theophylactus Abastactus, the father of Romanus Lacapenus, who later became emperor. Having returned to the Great City, he sent his relative, Christopher, to Crete against the Agarenes, who, having defeated them in battle, broke their barbaric audacity. The same Basilius also set out against the Eastern Agarenes and Manichaeans, who obeyed a certain Chrysocheir, and having captured certain fortresses and taken booty, he even determined to besiege Tephrica, their metropolis, but when it became clear that it required a long siege and was, furthermore, difficult to storm...
(70) Lamenting in misery. Gregorius in *The Life of Basil the Younger*, n. 2, regarding Basil: He who also slew Michael, who had reigned before him, and thus held the scepter, whom he even saw while he was dying, accusing him and saying: "What did I do to you, O Basilius, or what wrong did I do to you, that you slaughtered me so mercilessly?" And thus he confessed at the end of his life.
(71) And he expelled Photius from the Church. From then on he was treated very badly and was consigned to prison, with the books he might have occupied himself with in the meantime denied to him, as Photius himself relates in his 97th epistle to the Emperor Basil, and in another to Baanes the praepositus and patrician.
And many fell; but he himself would have been captured, had not the unshakeable Theophylact (72), the father of Romanus who later reigned, saved him. B Having returned to the Great City, he sent his son-in-law, Christopher, against the Hagarenes to Crete; who, having engaged them and routed them, humbled their insolence. The Emperor Basil also campaigned against the Hagarenes in the eastern region, and against the Manichaeans, whom Chrysochir (73) led. Having plundered their goods, he attempted to besiege Tephrike, their metropolis. But learning that it required a long blockade and was otherwise difficult to take, he departed from there. Finding the Euphrates flowing high, he bridged it with ships and crossed over, having seized many things there and captured other fortresses. Some he made peace with, others who submitted to him he received, and he returned to the queen of cities; and having celebrated a triumph through the middle of the city, he returned to the palace. But Chrysochir, the leader of the Manichaeans, invaded the Roman provinces and was plundering them. The emperor therefore sends the domestic of the schools against him. Because he was not bringing a force sufficient to engage him in a pitched battle, he did not think it best to join combat. But wherever he would linger, by engaging those who were wandering and making raids, he would harass them, so that they could not make their excursions and plunderings without fear. For this reason, even the booty that Chrysochir had already managed to seize, he would bring back as he returned home. The domestic of the schools ordered two generals to accompany him with their regiments, and not to allow the barbarians to scatter and loot. In the evening, with Chrysochir and his men encamped at the base of a mountain, and the Romans having occupied the higher ground, a dispute arose concerning the primacy and the martial strength of the soldiers of both generals. As each side boasted that they were superior to the others, someone, wishing to resolve the dispute, said: "Why, fellow soldiers, do we boast in vain, when with the enemy near, it is possible to demonstrate who are the brave men not in words, but in deeds?" This advice being approved, they immediately attacked the enemy. Terrified by the sudden aggression, they turned their backs to the attackers, and as the pursuit continued for a long time, the intervening plain was strewn with corpses. Chrysochir himself fell at that time, and many were captured and bound and sent to the emperor, along with the head of Chrysochir. D Ignatius (74) the patriarch having departed to the life beyond, having governed the Church for eleven years, Photius was placed by Basil a second time on the pontifical throne. The plots formed against the emperor were discovered; their leader, Romanus Curcuas, was deprived of his eyes, and the others were scourged and, having had their hair shaved, were banished.
Various readings and notes
(72) Theophylact. I do not know if this is Theophylact, patrician and praetor of the Armeniacs, to whom Photius’s 21st epistle is addressed.
(73) Chrysochir. Constantine also narrates the war of Basil against Chrysochir in *Basil*, ch. 30, and in the book *De Thematibus*, ch. 10, besides Scylitzes, Glycas, and others.
(74) Ignatius, etc. Ignatius died on October 13, on the day sacred to St. James, as Nicetas writes. Stylianus, Bishop of Neocaesarea on the Euphrates, in an epistle to the Orientals which exists in the *Appendix to the Council of Constantinople*, ch. 4, seems to indicate that Ignatius was removed and extinguished by the evil arts of Photius, a thing which the same Nicetas ignores. He was, however, buried in the monastery of Satyros, founded by him, which S. Michael dedicated, as he hands down the origin of the name; Symeon Logothete in his ms. *Chronicle* on Basil: In the 6th year of his reign, the patriarch Ignatius made the church of Satyros in the name of the chief captain of the Rising [Archistrategus], and then made it also a monastery for men, where he was buried. It is called Satyros because at a small distance from such a monastery the old [palace] is called Satyros. There was a shrine there built by the Greeks to the same Satyros, and from the homonymy, because the aforementioned monastery was nearby, it is called by such a name; from which also the palace of Bryas, which Theophilus the emperor built, having removed the materials, he constructed it. It is called 'Rising' [Anatellon] for such a reason: once when Nicephorus was hunting... [index: entries preserved]
all the steers of the Church, Photius is brought a second time by Basil to the throne of the high priesthood. A plot concocted against the emperor did not escape notice, whence the ringleader of it, Romanos Kourkouas, had his eyes put out; those who had sworn oaths with him were tortured, shorn of their hair, and condemned to exile.
IX. Now, as spring burst forth, he campaigns against Syria, having with him one of his sons, Constantine, and he took some of the fortresses; others also surrendered to him, fearing the capture of the rest. Then he attacks Germanicia, and after burning its suburbs, he reached the city of Adana, and was besieging it. As the citizens were acting carelessly regarding the siege, Basil asked them on what they relied that they did not surrender, when the city was already on the verge of being captured. And one of the older men said he knew for certain that it was not fated for the city to be captured by him at that time, but by another who would descend from his loins, by the name of Constantine. When the emperor showed him his son and said that this is Constantine, the old man denied that he was the Constantine by whom their city was to be captured, but [claimed it would be] another of his descendants. Basil, therefore, angered by the old man's predictions, pressed the siege more vigorously. But since it was accomplishing nothing, and the cold that had set in was greatly distressing the army, he raised the siege and planned his return, ordering the captives to be put to death, so that there might be no need for guarding them, and lest they should seize any opportunity that arose to cause some revolutionary act.
Now, the Agarenes from Tarsus and Melitene were raiding the Roman provinces, against whom the commander Andrew had marched, and was curbing their attacks. When the Emir of Tarsus sent him a letter filled with blasphemy, in which he wrote that if he himself were to attack him, the Son of Mary would be of no help to him, Andrew took the blasphemous letter and hung it upon an icon of the Mother of God, saying, “Lady, repay the arrogance of this man.” Having said this, he marched against the blasphemer; a battle was joined, the enemies were turned to flight, and a great slaughter of them took place, and the Emir himself was cut down, with only a few escaping by flight. But the commander was slandered out of envy, as if he could have captured Tarsus too, but was unwilling. For this reason, the emperor took the command away from him and gave it to Stypiotes, who was promising to sack Tarsus, and boasting with youthful arrogance that he had achieved many other great things; who not only did not achieve anything of what he boasted, but also fell into disaster out of negligence and ill-considered encampment. For the Barbarians, having learned that his camp was kept without guards, attacked it at night and slew many, and the majority perished by being trampled by one another, and thus the Agarenes were the victors.
In such a state were the affairs of the East; the West was faring even worse. For Italy, which obeyed the Roman emperors, and the greatest part of Sicily, and many other nations, had been made tributary to the Carthaginians under Michael. For the Carthaginian Agarenes, having equipped a fleet and captured many small towns, were besieging Ragusa. When the Ragusans sought aid through envoys, Basil sent one hundred warships to their relief; when the Agarenes learned that these were coming against them, they raised the siege.
Various readings and notes
to the emperor, in the place where the monastery currently is, in the very spot where a deer was caught, where the sanctuary of the monastery is founded, a table was found of old, supported by a column, which read as follows: “This is the holy sanctuary of the commander-in-chief Michael the Rising, which the holy apostle Andrew enthroned.” See our *Constantinopolis*, book IV, page 189.
The other nations, moreover, who were oppressed by these Agarenes—as has been related regarding what happened at Rhagousion, and how the enemies, fearing the alliance of the Emperor, withdrew from the siege—also sent embassies seeking an alliance and subjecting themselves to the hegemony of the Romans. The Emperor accepted the embassy and assisted the petitioners both by the aforementioned fleet and by the Frankish king. (78) For he sent to him, to collaborate in the war against the Agarenes with the Roman army, by whom the host of the Agarenes was defeated, and their commander, called the Sultan, was taken, whom the King of the Franks led away to Capua. Having spent two years there, he was never seen by anyone to smile, so that the king [of the Franks] was struck with wonder, and if anyone reported to him that the barbarian was laughing, he would promise to give a reward for the news. Someone therefore said that he had seen the sultan laughing. The king, having summoned the barbarian, asked for the cause of his laughter. He [replied]: "I saw the wheels of a chariot, of which one was pressing against the ground while the other was being lifted on high; then the one that was high went down to the ground, and the one that was low was lifted up. C Having figured that human affairs are also moved and transferred in this way, I laughed, reasoning that it was not impossible that my own affairs might be disposed in this manner, and just as I was brought down from high to low estate, so perhaps I might again be raised from lowliness to a high position and become exalted." These things that man said. The king, having brought the truth of his words into his mind, and pitying the sultan for his change of fortune, made him his intimate and gave him freedom of speech, and, thinking the man to be prudent, he shared his counsels with him. D Having thus forged the signatures of all, he proclaimed and hailed Louis as Emperor and Engelberga as Augusta in the synod he had fabricated for himself; to whom he even wrote an epistle filled with praise, as one deemed worthy of coronation in an ecumenical synod, as he thought, similar to Pulcheria. And he importuned and conspired to persuade her own husband Louis to exclude Pope Nicholas from Rome, as one deposed by an ecumenical and catholic synod, a copy of which he sent to her as well with gifts.
He, however, being a meddling man, proceeds against the king with deceit. For when the King had newly acquired the cities of Capua and Beneventum, he was afraid concerning them, lest they should ever assert their freedom. But that Barbarian, having learned this, comes to the King and says: "If you wish for your rule over these cities to be secure, transfer the more influential men in them elsewhere; then the multitude, left to itself, will not cherish any revolutionary thoughts." The King, thinking this counsel beneficial, set his mind to it, and chains were secretly forged. Then that wicked man conversed with the prominent men in the cities, and acting as if he were very friendly, he said to them in secret that the King wished to transport the more notable of the citizens into his own land while in chains. "And," he said, "the proof of my words is the forging of iron chains, shackles, and other bonds." Having found this to be true, they supposed he was well-disposed toward them; and when the King went out for a hunt, they barred the gates of the cities, and no longer admitted him. And he departed to his own place; but the Sultan gained their freedom as a reward from those citizens. Having gone away and re-conquered his former domain, he marched against the aforementioned cities of Capua and Beneventum. They, pressed by the siege, sent to the King begging him earnestly to forgive them and to ally with them; but when he would not accept the petition, saying that he rejoiced at their destruction, they sent another embassy to Emperor Basil, imploring him to assist them in their danger. He promised to do so, and the envoy returned to encourage his fellow citizens. But he was captured by the enemies; and the Sultan, having learned whence he came and what good news he was bringing to the citizens, said to him: "If you wish to live, announce to your people, who are looking down from the wall, that the Emperor has refused the alliance. For if you do not do this, know that you shall die instantly." He agreed to do as he was ordered; and standing before the wall while held in chains, he declared that he had fulfilled his embassy and that the Emperor's aid would arrive immediately. And as he said these things, he was cut to pieces by swords. The Sultan, despairing of the capture of the cities, withdrew. B The Emir of Tarsus, Esman, with large ships—which the people from Hagar call *cumparia*—attacked the city of Euripus, but achieved nothing; indeed, he himself perished, having been struck a mortal blow, along with most of his army. Furthermore, Saet Apochaps, the ruler of Crete, prepared another fleet and placed in command of it a certain energetic man named Photius, who ravaged the coastal regions of the Aegean and the islands. When the Roman fleet, whose commander was the *drungarius* of the fleet, the patrician Nicetas Ooryphas, encountered them, it reduced many of the enemy ships to ashes along with their crews by means of Greek fire, slew many with the sword, and submerged more. Those who escaped this manifold danger, becoming inured to their shame, were not content with their safety, but, having prepared pirate ships, ravaged the Peloponnese and the islands there. But the aforementioned *drungarius* came upon them again. For having docked at the port of Cenchreae, and having learned that the enemy ships were frequently stationing themselves around Methone, Pylos, and Patras, he quickly moved his triremes across the Isthmus of Corinth by land, and attacked the enemy unexpectedly. D Struck by the surprise assault, he burned the pirate ships, and some he submerged along with the men themselves, the leader of the enemies, Photius, also being killed. And thus the fleet of the Cretans perished.
A 172 X. Further, from Africa, sixty other very large ships set out, and they were ravaging the Roman provinces, and they proceeded as far as Cephalenia and Zacynthus. Therefore, a navy was sent against them, with Narsas commanding as admiral. But since many of those who were toiling at the rowing had deserted, the triremes were half-empty; for which reason the admiral judged it not appropriate to join battle with the enemies in such a state, but reported the occurrence to the emperor, and the deserters, having been sought out, were quickly captured. But in order that he might also instill fear into his navy, and yet might not punish any Christian, he ordered thirty of the captured Agarenes who were being guarded in prisons to be brought out by night, and for their faces to be smeared with soot so that they would not be recognized, then to be scourged and led through the marketplace, and finally to be taken away to the Peloponnesus to be impaled. When this was done, the sailors of the fleet were terrified, and thus they attacked the enemies by night and raised a trophy, with some of the enemy being killed and others being captured. Then they also crossed into Sicily, and they harassed and plundered the cities there that belonged to the Carthaginians.
But the Arabs from Mesembria, having learned that the Roman navy was idle, sailed with their ships and harassed the coasts of Phoenicia and Syria. For the emperor, intending to expiate the murder of Michael, his predecessor, built new temples in many places of the royal city to Michael, the prince of the heavenly militia; and in the palace itself he built a magnificent and expensive sanctuary to this same Michael, which he called the New Church. Now, while the sailors were occupied with these building works—for their backs were never free from carrying loads, and their hands were occupied with baskets C — the fleet remained idle; for which reason the Agarenes, as has been said, were fearlessly plundering the seacoasts; and they did not only harass these, but they also sacked Syracuse. However, the emperor was exhausting the sailors of the fleet with these building works, and he even built a temple to the prophet Elijah the Thesbit, for he honored this man exceptionally, and hoped that he might one day be taken up by him and carried off into heaven with the chariot of fire. Nevertheless, having learned of the capture and destruction of Syracuse, he sent men to resist the wicked Agarenes; but he accomplished nothing. Then he sent Nicephorus Phocas, the grandfather of that Phocas who reigned afterwards, with adequate forces—a man most brave and most experienced in military affairs, who often routed the Agarenes. And while the Emperor Basil was still serving with Theophilitzi, as has been mentioned above, he descended to the Peloponnesus and entered the temple of Andrew, in which lived a monk.
and the state of those cohabiting with us, are, moreover, made wretched by bugs and lice and public swarms, and other things similar to these little beasts crawling along this dark pavement. There are also others locked in the same prison, making, along with you, a trade of these miseries: Ethiopians, Tarsians, Hebrews, Lombards, and then our own Christians who have set out from various places, among whom was also the most holy [Manas, or Menas (see Pyrrhus, vol. II, p. 592)], the Bishop of Malta, with his feet bound in two sets of fetters. Then the bishops embraced one another and, having kissed each other with a holy kiss, wept a little for what had happened to them; soon, giving thanks to the Lord for the same matter, they resisted the sense of pain with arguments drawn from our philosophy. While we are occupied in these things, that detestable day of their sacrifice recurs among them; on this day they boast that they are commemorating that holy sacrifice which Abraham once performed, when he offered to God the ram given as a victim in place of a rational being. They call this "Pascha" out of ignorance; and not without reason do they designate the day thus: for their crossing is not from the Egypt of this world into the land of promise, according to the ancient nomenclature of the Pasch; nor is it from this earth into the heavenly one, or from death to life, as the Christian faith teaches us to use this term; but it is from life to death, and from this bodily destruction, and from that which falls under the senses, to that eternal conflagration, and to that which shall never be wanting its end. On this feast D (what singular madness!) a plan is made to burn the archbishop, and to offer the most holy bishop of Christ as a victim to evil demons. For someone among those who presided over the people, having a mouth breathing like an open grave, said, turning to those standing around, "It is right, O citizens, to lay hands on this prelate of the Christians for our safety, both so that we may celebrate our feast of Pascha more festively than at any other time; for I trust that thus our affairs will turn out prosperously for us, and that it will bring about greater growth." This said he. But some old men, having white hair just as they were seniors, and very honorable in their toga, held a counsel to the people and disapproved of the deed. For they said that these things were not enough; they thought the celebration of that day should be honored by the notable vigil of the destruction of the city of Syracuse. And so the evil counselors were dissipated, God willing, regarding the archbishop. From that time even until this day, we have persisted, held by many hardships, and daily awaiting death itself, which always hangs over us captives. But you, O beloved and venerable head, be always mindful of your Theodosius, and render God placated and propitious, so that He may calm these waves of ours, steady and quell them, and turn our captivity as a torrent in the south, according to the Prophet-King and parent of God. Amen.
Following these are poems written in Latin from the Greek.
Accept the fruits of my labors, Father,
For they are indeed full of groans and wet with tears—
Accept, Father, the commands, accept in these letters
The destructions which the fierce enemy has brought upon us.
Entreat God, that He may stretch out His friendly hands.
Already, already, to this dear funeral of yours that is drawing near. And since he had seen Basil, and stood, and did obeisance, he received him kindly. A certain widow, chancing to be in that country, and the foremost of the natives, and abounding in much wealth, being familiar with the monk, and having learned what happened, sent for him and asked how she had deigned neither to greet nor to welcome the lord Theophilitzes, who was illustrious and kin to the emperors by birth, while she had both addressed and bestowed honor upon that servant there, which did not correspond to his station, but was even far exceeding it; for she conjectured, knowing the monk, that he had not done what was done without reason. But he said, "I saw Theophilitzes as a private citizen, but Basil as emperor, and for this reason I have honored him as emperor. For know that that man has been marked out by God as emperor." Having heard these things, the woman, since she understood that Basil had been left behind there, and having been sent by her lord, took him into her house and deemed him worthy of care, and when he had recovered, she persuaded him to become an adopted brother to her son, and provided money; then she even foretold the empire to him, and asked that when he should obtain the rule, he not forget her child, nor her, if she were still numbered among the living. Basil went away upon these conditions. But she, when Basil had just begun to reign, arrived at the queen of cities with her son, bringing gifts both opulent and many; which the emperor received honorably, and her son, whom he had gained as a spiritual brother, he adorned with the dignity of protospatharios. He restored sacred buildings collapsed by various earthquakes, and also held fast and strengthened the great arch of the temple of Holy Sophia, which was broken and threatening ruin, with the machines of artisans. By providing and promising money, he persuaded many Jews to become Christians. Peace having been made with the Russian people, he brought it about that they should arrive at the knowledge of our mysteries; and when they promised that they wished to be baptized, he sent a bishop to them. But being about to pass from their religion to ours, they were stalling and doubting: "Unless we see a miracle," they said to the bishop, "such as you teach many were done by Christ, we will never embrace your doctrine." Then he said: "Ask what you wish." But they said: "The book which teaches about Christ is to be cast into the fire: whoever..."
if it remains unconsumed, this shall be to us proof that the God preached by you is truly God." The bishop agreed to do this; a fire was kindled, and the bishop, raising his hands and eyes to heaven, said: "Glorify thy name, Christ our God." He placed the holy Gospel in the fire, and it remained for a long time in the flame, yet it remained unharmed. The Barbarians were astonished at the preaching, and urged that they be initiated by holy baptism. And these things happened in this way.
XI. But one of the emperor's sons, Constantine, who was dearer to him than the others, fell ill and died; and the emperor bore his death heavily, and was inconsolable, and sought to have him appear to him alive. Now there was a certain monk, Theodore, by surname Santabarenus; this man had endeared himself to the emperor, who was persuaded of his extreme virtue, to the point of believing that he could even work miracles. This Santabarenus, who had also served as bishop of the metropolis of the Euchaita, is said to have shown his dead son to the emperor as if alive. For in a certain thicket, he caused him to imagine that Constantine appeared to his father on horseback, whom the emperor thought he embraced and kissed, but then saw no more. By doing other similar things, Santabarenus brought the emperor into amazement, and he brought him entirely under his influence, so that he placed his trust in him and distrusted him in nothing. The emperor, therefore, was thus disposed toward Santabarenus. But Leo, his second son, already grown to young manhood, and having taken as his wife the daughter of Martinacius, and having been proclaimed emperor by his father, was not pleased with Santabarenus, but called him a poisoner, a sorcerer, and a deceiver of the emperor. Struck by these accusations as if by some mirrors, he rose up in vengeance, and with simulated benevolence toward Leo: "Since you are," he said, "a young man...
O Emperor, and ride to the hunt with your father and join him in the pursuit, you ought also to carry a small dagger so that you might use it against wild beasts when necessary, or perhaps occasionally give it to your father, or even defend him should anyone plot against him." Leo, not detecting the trap, was persuaded by this advice to carry a small blade inside his shoe. With this very dagger, Santabarenus executed his vengeance against Leo. He said to the Emperor, "Your son is plotting against you; the proof of this is that when he goes hunting with you, he carries a concealed dagger." Basil went out to hunt, and Leo accompanied his father; the dagger was found concealed beneath Leo’s sandal. The accuser appeared to have spoken the truth. The defense offered by the accused was not accepted, and the father burned with rage against his son. He imprisoned him in one of the imperial chambers, and stripped him of the insignia of his power. As some historians record, he even contemplated blinding his son, being incited to this by Santabarenus; and the plot would have been carried out had not the patriarch and the leading members of the senate, with many entreaties, deterred the Emperor from his purpose. For a time the anger persisted, and Leo remained under guard. A long period passed, until the Emperor attended a banquet with certain senators, a thing which was done at certain times by an ancient custom. In the house where the banquet was being held, a parrot was suspended in a cage. As that species of bird is prone to imitation, having continually heard someone lamenting and calling for Leo, it imitated the voice and would repeatedly cry out, "Leo, Leo." The guests, seizing upon this opportunity afforded by the bird's voice to intercede for Leo, stood up from the banquet, and filled with tears, said: "Will not this bird accuse us, O Emperor, when it calls for its own master, while we, enjoying the banquet of your majesty, forget him who has been imprisoned for such a long time? Relinquish, Lord, your anger against him. Remove his confinement, and look upon him with a placid and, rather, a fatherly countenance." By these words, the Emperor's anger was mitigated, and he returned to favor with his son, restoring him to his former dignity. Afterward, when he had gone out to hunt, he came upon a stag of unusual size, carrying its antlers aloft. Following it, he caught up to it, and as he was about to strike it with a drawn sword, the beast defended itself; catching the Emperor’s belt on one of the branches of its antlers, it lifted him—suspended high—upon its horns. He might perhaps have perished in this way, had he not been rescued by someone who cut through the belt with a sword. The man received from him a "noble" reward for his own salvation: decapitation. A specious reason was contrived: that he had brandished a sword against the Emperor; nor did he consider that, instead of...
branding a sword against the Emperor, the man had wielded it on the Emperor's behalf, and was more worthy of reward and royal gifts. Thus did he profit from his zeal for the Emperor. As for the man who was struck, his internal organs were twisted by the horn; he survived indeed, but not for long, and soon died, having reigned jointly with Michael for one year and ruled alone for nineteen, leaving the empire to his son Leo, the eldest of his surviving sons.
XII. Once Leo had become Emperor (91*), he immediately thought of taking vengeance on Santabarenus. But since he feared that he might be defended by the Patriarch Photius, who was favorably disposed toward him, he fabricated charges against the Patriarch. He expelled him from the Church and restricted him to the monastery of the Armenians, and designated his own blood relative, Stephanus, as patriarch (95); and because he was not the hierarch of Heraclea, he was consecrated by the protothronos. He ordered that Santabarenus be brought from Euchaita, for he presided over the Church there. He also sent to the monastery of Philippicus in Chrysopolis, where the Emperor Michael was buried, and took his body out of the coffin, and with a great procession and royal honor, placed it in a marble sarcophagus in the church of the Holy Apostles, his brothers, Alexander and the Patriarch Stephanus, being present at the procession. As for Stylianus Zautzes (96), he debauched his daughter while his own wife, Theophano, was still alive, and honored her father with the title of magister and logothete of the dromos, and later bestowed on him the title of basileopator, a name he himself devised. After the fire, he restored among other things the church of the holy apostle Thomas. Having already brought Santabarenus to the palace at Pegara, he held him in custody, and having had him pitilessly beaten with scourges, he relegated him to Athens, and not long after ordered his eyes to be gouged out. But after many years had passed, [he recalled] the man from exile.
Variæ lectiones et notæ.
(94) If not... refer to Symeon the logothete in [the notes on] Basil n. 3 and Leo the Grammarian.
(94*) "Leo the Philosopher." Added in the margin in manuscript codices. Allatius denies that the Emperor Leo is called "the Philosopher," and says this epithet applies only to Leo the Deacon, of whom Byzantine writers speak in [the Life of] Theophilus, [and maintain that] he was so called. Certainly Constantine Porphyrogenitus, the son of [Leo], in the Life of his grandfather, n. 48 and 76 and 89 in the Combesis edition, always calls him Leo. But he is indiscriminately designated as such in Balsamon. Moreover, in an Alexius Comnenus Novella in the same Balsamon ad Nomocanen Photius n. 140, 142, ed. 1. He is called "the Most Philosophical." In the Royal codex, in which the 11 books concerning the themes are described, this title is preferred: "A work of Constantine, son of the Emperor Leo the Wise."
