(95) He was designated [Patriarch] of Constantinople, a man who excelled in every virtue; though he served in that office for a short time, and having received frequent purgings from the physicians of the children, on account of the burning (i.e., fever) which troubled him, by which, being poorly cooled in the stomach, he fell into a difficult disease and died.
(96) Stylianus, the first of the secretis, iambics of Symeon the logothete and master, commonly called Metaphrastes; and described by Allatius in his Dissertation on the writings of the Symeons. This Stylianus, of whom Zonaras writes, died during the reign of Leo, as is found in the anonymous Combefisianus n. 14.
Hagio, the duke of Longobardia, a kinsman of the king of the Franks, upon hearing the news of Basil’s death, terminated the treaty with the Romans and claimed the entire province for himself. Wherefore, the emperor sent the prefect of the table D with the Western legions against him; but he, being defeated in battle and having lost almost all those he had brought with him, escaped with difficulty by flight. Upon the death of the patriarch Stephen, Antonius, surnamed Cauleas, succeeded him. Since the Bulgarians were at peace and maintained commerce with the Romans, and those in charge of the customs duties were exacting unjust tolls from them, this proved sufficient grounds for their prince, Symeon, who had long been seeking an occasion to wage war against the Romans. And truly, he did wage it. A Roman army that had armed itself against him was defeated, and many of the Romans, including the commander himself, fell; many others were captured, whose noses Symeon cut off before allowing them to return to Byzantium. Irritated by this disgrace, the emperor bribed with gifts the Turks who dwell along the Ister, who are also called Hungarians, that they might take vengeance upon the Bulgarians and afflict them with all their might. He himself also prepared to bring war upon them by land and by sea, committing the war against them to the patrician Nicephorus Phocas, whom he appointed Domestic of the Schools. But before the war, the emperor sent a quaestor as an envoy to Symeon concerning peace. But the barbarian, suspecting that he had come with treachery, cast the man into chains and imprisoned him, and offered resistance to Phocas. While he was occupied in this war, the Hungarians ravaged his territory. Wherefore Phocas, leaving that aside, set out against them and, entering into battle array, was defeated, many Bulgarians being killed and more captured. Symeon himself barely escaped to Dorostolum, which is Dristra. The emperor bought the captured Bulgarians from the Hungarians, and to him Symeon sent envoys regarding peace, in whose words Leo, having placed his trust, sent Chærosphartes to establish peace. But the barbarian, having detained and chained him, attacked the Turks, and putting them to flight and devastating their lands, wrote to Leo that he would not make peace with him unless he received the captured Bulgarians; and when he had received them, he still did not make peace. Therefore, the emperor, having gathered into one place both the Oriental and Occidental legions, launched them against the Bulgarians; yet they, having engaged with Symeon, were defeated. Afterwards, as he was consorting in certain fields with Zoe, the daughter of Zautzis, he was attacked by certain men in an ambush; having perceived these from the murmuring, Zoe awakened the emperor, who was sleeping, and he immediately returned to the palace and escaped; and shortly after the death of the empress Thephanonis, he named the same Zoe Augusta and took her, who had long been his concubine, as his lawful wife. But that happiness was brief. For when she had lived in the empire for one year and eight months, she died. There were other plots as well, reported to the emperor, which Zautzis’ cousin, Basilius Pectes, was concocting. He communicated the secret to the eunuch Samonas, having first bound him by oath,
that he would keep the things said unrevealed. Now Samonas was an Agarene, and having learned the plot against the ruler, he immediately revealed the mystery to him. Basilius and the accomplices to the plot were arrested, and Samonas was honored with the rank of protospatharios and made intimate with the emperor.
XIII. Leo also took a third wife, named Eudoxia, of the Opiscian clan, who was of excellent form. But she did not live long with him. For while she was pregnant, she died in labor along with the child to which she had given birth. But the emperor, being desirous of producing an heir, especially since it had been predicted to him by seers (for he was a lover of learning of every kind, and indeed of that secret kind which divines the future through incantations; he was also versed in the science concerning the movements of the stars and their effects, in which he found that he would have a son as a successor to the empire), he took a fourth wife, Zoe Carbonopsina. He did not, however, immediately grant her the honors of the imperial rank, but for a long time kept her without a diadem, until she had given birth to a young son, who was initiated into sacred baptism by the patriarch Nicholas. For when Antonius had departed, having governed the Constantinopolitan throne for [1] years, the patriarch Nicholas, named Mysticus, was designated. After the birth of his son, to whom the emperor gave the name Constantine, he also proclaimed his consort Augusta. But because of this fourth marriage, Leo was censured by the patriarch with an ecclesiastical penalty. And in honor of his first wife, the blessed Theophano, he erected a temple in her name, very close to the church of the Holy Apostles, in which he also deposited her body. And he built another temple in the name of the holy Lazarus, in which he stored her sacred body brought from Cyprus, and indeed also that of Mary Magdalen. But when the sailors were impeded in their building and the fleet was idle, the fleet of the Agarenes devastated Tauromenium and occupied the island of Lemnos, in which many mortals perished. The emperor, however, during Pentecost at the church of Saint Mocius...
A, as was the custom, also made his procession; and as he was already approaching the cancelli of the sanctuary, someone emerged from the ambo holding a very heavy staff in his hands and struck the emperor upon his head. He would have crushed it completely, had the staff not struck a hanging lamp, thereby losing its greatest force. The nobles, seeing the blood flowing from the emperor’s head, were greatly disturbed; and because his brother Alexander was not present, a suspicion prevailed among many that he had been an accomplice to the plot. But the man who had dared to commit this crime, after being put to the question and naming no accomplice, had his hands and feet amputated, and was finally given over to the fire.
The monk Mark, who had composed the *Tetraodion* for Great Saturday, being present, said to the emperor: "This was foretold by David, who said: 'What thy enemy hath done wickedly in thy holy place, and they that hate thee have boasted in the midst of thy solemnity' (Ps. LXXIII, 3-4). Know therefore, O emperor, that from this time you will reign for ten years." And the word became deed; for Leo died ten years later, on the very same day that he had been struck on the head.
Samonas, whom we mentioned above, having great influence with the emperor and having amassed abundant wealth, took this and fled toward the Agarenes, from whence he had originated. But he was caught while crossing the Halys; and although he claimed he was making a pilgrimage to fulfill a vow to the Sirichan cross, he was nonetheless brought back to Byzantium by Constantine, the son of Andronicus the Doux. The emperor, preserving his former affection for Samonas, commanded the Doux that, when interrogated before the senate, he should not say that he had fled, but that he had traveled to the Sirichan cross. When, however, on the following day, the emperor asked Constantine, while the senate was present and under oath, whether Samonas had fled, he, fearing to commit perjury, said that he had gone to his own homeland, Melitene. The emperor sent Constantine away in anger.
Samonas, moreover, beyond his long-standing close relationship, being also adorned with the dignity of patrician and appointed cubicularius, often pushed the emperor to step down from his office. One such occasion was when he forced the patriarch to withdraw and retire from his oversight: having summoned him and being unable to persuade him by that reasoning, they immediately saw to it that he was transported by ship from the palace to Iria, and from there by an overland journey to the monastery of Galacrenae, which he himself had built, after he had presided over the Church for eleven years. In his place, the syncellus Euthymius, a holy man, was designated patriarch, who resisted with all his might that a law be promulgated by the emperor.
it would have been permitted to anyone to take a second, third, and even fourth wife.
Samonas, moreover, was thereafter promoted by him to earlier intimacy, and then was honored as patrician, and appointed parakoimomenos; he pushed the emperor toward many things that were not appropriate, among which was the compulsion to force the patriarch to accept him and to release him from excommunication. The emperor, therefore, having summoned the high priest, begged him to receive him. But as the latter was inexorable, he was immediately transported from the palace to Hiera [(5)], and from there they led him on foot to the monastery in Galakrenai, which he himself had built, after he had presided over the Church for eleven years. Euthymius, the synkellos, a holy man, was appointed patriarch, who also, when the emperor wished to enact by law that anyone who wished might take two, three, or even four wives, prevented it with all diligence.
XIV. When the Hagarenes [(6)] were plundering the Roman provinces with their ships, the emperor appointed Himerius, the logothete of the dromos [(7)], as commander of the fleet and sent him against those from Hagar, ordering him also to take along the Doux Andronicus. But the wily Samonas, harboring an implacable hatred against the Ducal family, suborned one of Andronicus’s friends to warn him not to sail with Himerius, as the latter was intending—by order of the emperor—to gouge out his eyes. Therefore, Andronicus did not consent to join Himerius; hence it happened that Himerius, fighting the naval battle alone, was victorious and overcame the enemy’s fleet. From that point, Andronicus, in despair, taking with him his kinsmen and servants, seized Kabala, a fortified stronghold situated not very far from Iconium. But Samonas did not cease to provoke the emperor and to incite him to anger against the man. When the latter learned of this, and also of the expulsion of the patriarch Nicholas from the Church, he despaired of all things and went over to the Hagarenes with his whole family. The emperor, upon learning this, was grieved beyond measure, fearing that he would have as an enemy the man whom he had used as a defender. He wrote a letter, therefore, by which amnesty for his actions was provided to Andronicus, and a return was permitted, and the gift of many good things was promised. Having placed this letter into wax molded like a candle, and having shaped the wax [(8)] with a flame, they handed it over to a Saracen, one of the captives who had been released from prison, to be delivered to Andronicus. Samonas, having summoned him, said secretly: "Do you know that you are carrying the destruction of all of Syria in your hands? In order, therefore, that you may save your country and your people, deliver this wax to Uzer." For this was the name of Andronicus’s host. He did as he had been ordered. The Hagarenes
[index: entries preserved]
upon learning the content of the writing, cast Andronicus and all who were with him into chains. Andronicus departed this life in bonds. Some of those with him, unable to endure the hardship of the prison, abjured their faith. But Constantine and some of those with him escaped from prison and made their way to the Roman borders, and, having bravely routed the Hagarenes who were pursuing them, they were saved. The emperor received him with great joy, welcomed him magnificently, and distinguished him with exquisite honors. Then he also said to him: "Do not let the name, Constantine, deceive you, nor think that because a Constantine is to rule over the Romans, you will possess the empire; for know precisely that this one is my son. If you adorn your own Sparta, it will be well with you; but if you desire new things and attempt tyranny, do not doubt that through this gate"—pointing out to him the western gate of the Golden Triclinium—"your head will pass apart from the rest of your body." This is what indeed happened later.
When the father of Samonas had come from Melitene with some others, the emperor received them with honor, though he improperly introduced them, being unfaithful, into the Great Church. But the father of Samonas, seeing the honor his son enjoyed in the palace and the wealth with which he was surrounded, wished to live with him. He was prevented by his son, who advised him to preserve his own religion and return to his homeland, saying that he himself would eventually do the same when an opportunity arose. At the feast of Pentecost, Leo crowned his son and proclaimed him emperor.
Now the loathsome Samonas had a castrated servant named Constantine, a native of Paphlagonia, whom he had given to the mistress for service. Since Constantine was very dear to both the empress and the ruler himself, the profane Samonas was stirred to jealousy and slandered him to the emperor, as if he were an object of the empress’s love. Leo, therefore, moved by jealousy, ordered him to be shorn as a monk. Not long after, he recalled him, ordered him to strip off his monastic habit and put on secular clothing, and treated him even more familiarly than before. This appeared intolerable to Samonas. He therefore arranged for a document full of insults to be written by one of his own men, and cast it where the emperor was to pass. When the emperor found and read it, he was distressed and sought its author. One of those who was privy to the matter indicated that Samonas was the author of the writing.
Now, since there had been an eclipse of the moon at that time, the emperor summoned the Metropolitan of Synada to inquire about it. When Samonas asked him about it privately, he replied, "This affection of the moon portends your misfortune. But if the thirteenth day of July passes before your calamity, you will avoid the misfortune." Then, when the Bishop of Synada was also questioned by the emperor, he replied that this evil would fall upon the person who was closest to him. The emperor, for his part, judged the closest person to be his brother Alexander, but falsely; for it was fitting that the wicked man should eventually fare ill. Leo, therefore, when he acknowledged him to be the author of the infamous document, cast him out of the palace, and...
he was tonsured and confined within strictly defined boundaries. And this came to pass even before the thirteenth of July had slipped away. Constantine, who was envied and plotted against by Samonas, was appointed *parakoimomenos* in his stead, and the emperor built a monastery in the Nossiai. But Leo, having fallen ill with an intestinal disease, had his strength so exhausted that he could not even deliver the customary address to the Senate regarding the Fast, which is called the Silention. Nevertheless, he did speak a few words, and begged the Conscript Fathers to be mindful of him, and to keep their faith to his wife and son. Although he said that he was now addressing them for the last time, he did not die immediately, but survived until May. Then, at the point of death, he left the sovereign power to his brother Alexander, and having handed over his son, requested that he look after him, and having educated him in a royal manner, leave the empire to him. Seeing him coming toward him when he was already expiring, it is said that he remarked: "Behold the ill-fated time, after thirteen months." Leo reigned for twenty-five years and three months.
XV. Alexander, once he had firmly established his rule, immediately brought back the Patriarch Nicholas from the Galacrenai to the throne of Constantinople, having driven Euthymius into exile and stripped him of his dignity from the *ambon*, while the followers of Nicholas heaped many insults upon the man. Once in power, he did nothing worthy of an emperor, but [abandoned himself] to pleasures, hunting, drunkenness, and revelling.
Various readings and notes
(10) For "the thirteenth of July," *Leo Grammaticus*, the *Anonymous Combetisianus* (no. 31), *Scylitzes*, and *Glycas* read [in the same manner].
(11) In the *Nossiai*, it is omitted by others; see Symeon Logotheta in the manuscript *Chronicle*. It is worth saying a few words as to the manner in which the monastery of the Nossiai was built. This Constantine had a pious father who feared God, and who possessed a very small estate near the sea, in the very place of the Nossiai, where he had built a cistern and provided refreshment for travelers by pouring water into it. It happened that a soldier passed by this water, and after refreshing himself, he pulled out some coins from a rightful source—and they were counted as being three in number—and then, as happens, he arose and, having mounted his horse, went on his way, leaving the gold at that spot. Therefore, as was the old man's custom, he went out to the cistern and, finding the gold, he was saddened by the grief of the one who had lost it; yet, keeping it by him, he did not cease, night after night, to pray to God that this person might return and receive his own property. The soldier, however, passed through the gates and, remembering [what had happened], although he could do nothing, he went on his way grieving. After three years, he came back to the place of the Nossiai, and dismounting from his horse, he watered it, and sitting down himself, he was pensive and sighed, saying that in this place I lost my livelihood. The venerable old man, hearing this, asked him, "What, then, is the matter, my master?" And he said, "In this place I lost fifty [coins]." And immediately he pointed out the spot, the signs of the purse, and the amount he was speaking of. And the man of God immediately draws it out from his bosom and says, "Do you recognize this?" And he, being struck speechless for a long time, nevertheless said, "It is that which I lost." And he says, "Take it, assured that I have not opened it, nor do I know what is inside." The man, having taken this, urged him to take whatever he wished from it, but he did not wish to at all, whence the soldier goes on his way, rejoicing and giving thanks to God. On that very night, he sees in a dream Christ coming and saying to him: "In return for what you did for the soldier, behold, tomorrow you shall hear that your son has become *parakoimomenos*, and that this estate shall become a great monastery for the glorification of my name; and you yourself shall receive the reward for these things." When morning came, the message arrived, and after a few days, by the will of the emperor and by his departure, as has been said, the monastery was built and he was enthroned.
addicted to his wife. He entrusted the affairs of the empire and the state to vagabonds and men from the crossroads, whom he had previously had as accomplices in his clandestine crimes before his reign—one of whom, called Basilitzes, he intended to appoint as successor to the scepter. For he wished both to relegate his nephew Constantine to private life and to deprive him of his procreative organs. And if his life had not quickly come to an end, and his plans had not been overturned by divine Providence, then, as far as it depended upon him, this might well have come to pass in reality. When he inquired of sorcerers whether he would live long, he heard from them that he would have a long life if he would add teeth and genitals to the bronze boar in the theater. "For this," they said, "is your element, because you stood against your brother Leo." And they judged nothing else correctly, except that they compared this man—who was enslaved to gluttony and banquets and constantly flowing in luxury—to the boar. The man, more senseless than the swine, believed their words and added those parts to the bronze work. When the ruler of the Bulgarians, Symeon, asked through envoys sent by him whether he would maintain the peace, Alexander received them contemptuously, spoke to them haughtily, and threatened them arrogantly. Symeon, impatient of this insult, armed himself against the Romans. Alexander, however, perpetually indulging in banquets and drunkenness, having once dined after a bath, and having used his hollow stomach, and having gorged himself insatiably, set out to play ball; and from the exertion of the riding and the contortions of the ball game, he suffered a rupture, and after discharging much blood through his nose and his genitals, he expired after one day. He had by document appointed as guardians for his nephew and administrators of the empire: the Patriarch Nicholas, the Magister Stephen, the Magister John Eladas, the Rector John, and two others whom he had summoned from the public circles and raised to senatorial and patrician dignity, Basilitzes and Gabrielopolus. C XVI. When Alexander, having trifled with his imperial rule for one year and one month, had departed, the power devolved to Constantine, the son of Leo, who was still a very young boy; for he was in his seventh year. Indeed, Constantine, the son of Andronicus Ducas, the domestic of the legions, upon hearing of Alexander’s death (for the son of Andronicus Ducas, who [index: entries preserved]
was at that time away from the city), partly uncovering and fanning into a flame, as it were, the sparks of desire for imperial rule that he kept hidden beneath the ashes—and partly also being incited to this by others, one of whom is said to be the patriarch Nicolaus (for since Alexander's will had not yet been read, he did not know that he himself had also been left as a guardian)—attempted a usurpation. Having taken up the elite of the troops, he arrived at Byzantium, and having entered by a certain postern gate at night, he seized the gate of the Hippodrome with torches, and having won over certain senators, and with a great crowd of the populace having gathered, who also cheered and proclaimed him emperor. But since those within would not open the gates, the Protos-strator of Constantine, trusting in his hands, and trying more boldly to strike and throw down the gates, was pierced by a spear by one of those inside through a small opening, and he was immediately dead. But Constantine went from there into the race-course theater, then came to the place called the Chalké, and proceeded as far as the station of the Excubitors. But one of the guardians, the magister John Eladas, having gathered some from the guilds and the naval forces, sent them to resist the tyrant. And a battle having taken place, many fell on both sides; and Gregoras, the son of Ducas, was slaughtered, as well as his cousin Michael, and Curticius the Armenian. Grieved at these losses, and wishing to strengthen his own regiment, Constantine drove his horse, desiring to join those in front; but as the ground was paved with slabs, the horse slipped and fell and threw its rider, and someone immediately rushed up to him and cut off his head and brought it to the emperor Constantine, having entered through the western gate of the Chrysotriclinium, just as the emperor Leo had prophesied.
That Constantine Ducas would not obtain the empire was known elsewhere to those around the emperor as well. For there was a certain tax-collector, Nicolaus, who, being encumbered by large debts to the public treasury, took refuge with the Ishmaelites, abjured his religion, and practiced astrology. He had sent a missive to the logothete of the drome, the summary of which was this: "Do not fear Ducas: for he will rashly attempt a revolution, and will immediately be utterly destroyed." Now, the rebellion having been suppressed, the seditious magister Gregoras, the father-in-law, and Leo Chrærosphactes betook themselves to the Great Church: these the guardians drove out from there and tonsured, so that they might be monks in the monastery of Studios. Others they beat with lashes and led around in a triumph; from some they gouged out their eyes, some they beheaded, and others they fastened to beams from Chrysopolis as far as Leucate. And many more would have perished from their license, had not some among the judges held them back from that rashness of punishing, with words of this sort: "Why do you..."
do these things without a command from the emperor, since the emperor is a child and does not consent to what is being done?" Then the guardians tonsured Constantine’s wife, and having castrated his son Stephen, they sent them off to their house in Paphlagonia. The emperor’s guardians—both the patriarch and the others—managing affairs according to their own will, did many things contrary to what was fitting, and they did not agree among themselves; some were abrogating what others had decided, so that the government was not of one, but of many.
But Symeon the Bulgar, thinking that because there was no emperor, he would easily take the Royal City, attacked Byzantium with massive forces, and having pitched camp outside its walls, meditated an assault. But when he saw the strength of the walls, and the multitude of those defending them, and the abundance of engines on the ramparts, as if struck with vertigo, he retreated to the Hebdomon, seeking to make terms. The patriarch and the rest of the guardians, therefore, took the emperor and went to the palace at Blachernae, where Symeon also arrived; hostages having been given and received, he bowed his head to the patriarch and received his blessing, and dined with the emperor. But since the conditions of peace were displeasing to him, he departed without an agreement, though he and his children were gifted with presents.
Now, because the emperor was constantly calling for his mother, and lamenting her absence—for Alexander had previously removed her from the palace—the guardians were compelled to bring her back. Upon her return, she took charge of affairs, having taken on as assistants both Constantine the Parakoimomenos and both the brothers of the Gongylios family; and first she removed the patriarch from the palace, then she expelled the attendants of Alexander—the rector Basilitzes and Gabrielopolus, as well as the others. Constantine the Parakoimomenos, however, had full power.
But the Bulgar Symeon was overrunning and plundering Thrace, and having raised an embankment, he trusted that he would take Adrianople. When he saw that he was being frustrated in this attempt, he attacked the matter by another path, and having corrupted certain of the garrison with money, he laid waste to the city, which had been betrayed; the empress’s zeal, however, recovered it for the Roman Empire. And since she could not bear the frequent incursions of Symeon into Thrace, she made peace with the Ismaelites and, having gathered the legions from both the East and the West, she committed the war against Symeon to the Magister Leo Phocas, the Domestic of the Schools, who, having joined battle, overcame the Bulgars with a signal victory, many of them being slain. But from fatigue and sweat, he was barely master of himself; he dismounted his horse and refreshed himself at a certain spring. Meanwhile, the horse escaped from the hands of the attendant and was galloping across the plain of the camp without a rider. Being a remarkable animal, it struck such terror into the armies—for they supposed the Domestic had fallen—that both the battle and the pursuit
ceased. When Symeon saw this from a certain vantage point, he encouraged those with him, and set out against the Romans. Attacking them while they were in disarray, he put them all to flight in a body. The Bulgarians pursued and slaughtered some, while others perished trampling one another. The Domestic of the Schools was able to escape to Mesembria with difficulty, and many of the generals and commanders of units were destroyed at that time.
XVII. John, known as Bogas, was sent to bring the Patzinaks into an alliance against the Bulgarians. And the patrician Romanos Lekapenos, being then Drungarios of the Fleet, was ordered to sail along the coast with the fleet, at once to provide assistance to the Domestic of the Schools and to transport the allies once they had been brought by Bogas. Bogas therefore arrived with the allies. But when a quarrel broke out between him and Lekapenos, the Patzinaks, seeing them bickering with one another and being neglectful regarding their transport, returned to their own lands. B Some therefore relate these matters thus, and state that the lack of aid from the allies was the cause of the Roman defeat. Others relate it differently; for they say that when Phocas had defeated the Barbarians and was pursuing them, a rumor arose that the Drungarios of the Fleet was departing with the fleet to seize the throne. When the Domestic heard this, he was so disturbed that he both desisted from the pursuit and returned to the camp to verify the rumor more certainly; and thus, the fortune of battle having shifted, the Romans fled, interpreting the return of the Domestic to the camp as a flight. The Bulgarians, however, [c] realizing the state of affairs, were emboldened and, launching a counter-attack, forced the Romans to retreat in panic and were thus the cause of the slaughter.
That the domestic [of the schools] returned to the camp was caused by the ambition for imperial power; for he was aflame with it, and if the rumor about the Drungary were true, he was eager to anticipate him and secure the authority for himself. When Lacapenus and Bogas had returned from the slaughter, and a judgment had been rendered regarding their dispute, Romanus was condemned to have his eyes gouged out, as he was the cause of the defeat, having acted either through malice or through negligence. But some—those possessing great influence with the Empress—rescued him from that sentence.
Bulgar Symeon, elated by his victory, advanced against the Queen of Cities. Once again, Phocas, the Domestic of the Schools, stood against him, and after a battle was engaged at Calasyrtes, the Barbarians were defeated. Many prominent men were consumed by a desire for the Empire, but none burned more fiercely with this love than Leo Phocas, the Domestic of the Schools. He was confident in himself, both on account of his lineage and his strength, and not least because of the Parakoimomenos, Constantine the Eunuch, who at that time possessed all power at the palace, and whose sister he had taken as his wife.
When the tutor of the Emperor Constantine saw these things and feared for him, he persuaded him to win over Romanus, the aforementioned Drungary of the fleet, and to take him on as a guardian. The Emperor therefore wrote a letter in his own hand and sent it to the Drungary. Having received it, the man promised that he would—if it were possible—overthrow the power of the Parakoimomenos. Once, when the Parakoimomenos arrived where the fleet was moored and was urging him to set sail, the Drungary met him in a servile manner, spoke with submission, and gradually led the man toward the triremes. As he approached the flagship, having many brave men ready whom he had kept with him, he said, "Seize him." They grabbed him and placed him on the trireme, while those who were with him were immediately scattered. The news of this deed alarmed both the Empress and the nobles; and when they had sent to the Drungary to inquire what he meant by this, they were repelled with stones by the sailors. The next day, the Emperor, having summoned the Patriarch and the Magister Stephen, sent men to lead his mother from the palace. But, overcome by pity as she clung to him with tears and embraces, he left her behind, and having transferred the power to himself, he appointed John Garidas as Magister and Domestic of the Legions. The Drungary Lacapenus, meanwhile, having prepared his fleet just as if for battle, that day...
of the Annunciation anchored at the palace in the Bucoleon. The patriarch and the magister Stephen straightway departed from the palace. Romanus, however, having previously sworn most fearful oaths that he would never plot against the emperor nor desire the empire, ascended to the palace. Entering the temple of the Pharos with Constantine, and having given and received pledges, he was appointed Grand Hetaireiarch. The emperor, for his part, commanded Phocas to be of good heart and not to abandon hope, but to wait a little while, for his case would be considered shortly thereafter. Helena, the daughter of Romanus, was betrothed to the emperor Constantine, and on the third day of Easter the marriage was celebrated, while the patriarch Nicholas performed the holy rites. Immediately, therefore, Romanus, the father of the emperor, was appointed father-in-law to the emperor, and his son, Christopher, was appointed Grand Hetaireiarch. Learning of these things, Phocas resorted to tyranny. Taking with him Constantine the parakoimomenos and other officials, he asserted that he was acting on behalf of the emperor Constantine in doing what he was doing. Romanus, having written a letter containing an overthrow of Phocas's pretext—and having confirmed this with the emperor's signature and the golden seal—sent it to the army with Phocas by the hand of a certain brave member of the hetaireia. That army, upon seeing the hidden letter with the golden seal, was caused by it to abandon Phocas in great numbers and to cross over to the emperor. Phocas, having reached Chrysopolis and being abandoned by the multitude that was with him, which had gone over to the emperor, turned to flight and, reaching a certain village which from ancient times had been called Goeleon by the locals, was captured, blinded, and brought to the queen of cities; and this was the end of his tyranny. But indeed, another plot against the emperor was also brought about; for some youths were hired to lie in wait for him while he was hunting, so that they might attack him during the hunt. But the scheme was discovered, and the ringleaders were punished. The emperor's mother, Zoe, was also cast out of the palace on the pretext that she was plotting against the emperor's father-in-law, and she was shorn in the monastery of Saint Euphemia. On the twenty-fourth day of the month of September, Romanus was raised to the dignity of Caesar, and in December he was crowned with the imperial diadem by the patriarch Nicholas, by the permission of Constantine, supposedly. XVIII. And thus Romanus Lacapenus, with those honors...
content, shortly after graced his own wife Theodora with a diadem. Then he did the same for his son, Christopher, by the will, as he wished to appear, of Constantine, but in reality while Constantine was unwilling and complaining to those whom he trusted, and loathing those actions, although he did not dare to resist. The plots which the sacellarius Anastasius, the leader of the conspiracy, had prepared for the defense of Constantine, having been detected, the accomplices were punished at the judgment of Romanus, the sacellarius was tonsured, and that was an occasion for Romanus to place Constantine beneath himself, since until then he had held the first place in proclamations and salutations. And when Symeon had sent an army against the city, Romanus also opposed soldiers to him, so that the suburban amenities might not be ravaged by the enemy. When the Bulgarians had attacked them, many also of the leaders fell, and the soldiers, partly hurrying to climb into the triremes sailing along the side, slipped into the sea and were carried by the waves, partly perished at the hands of the enemies, and some were captured alive. With the Roman army having been thus dealt with at Pegas, the Bulgarians burned the palace of that place, and the rest that was on that shore across from the city, with the channel lying between. With the death of Romanus’s wife, Theodora Augusta, Sophia, the wife of the emperor Christopher, received the name of Augusta. The leader of the Bulgarians, Symeon, attacked Adrianople again and was laying siege to it. But he would have accomplished nothing if hunger had not forced the citizens, destitute of provisions, to surrender both themselves and the city to the enemy. Then also Leo of Tripoli invaded the Romans with naval forces; but the Roman triremes having gone to meet them near the island of Lemnos, killed almost all the Agarenes and sank their ships, with the Tripolitan having with difficulty escaped by flight. Symeon, however, having devastated Macedonia and Thrace with a huge army, proceeded to the city, and having pitched camp near the Blachernae, requested a colloquy with the Roman emperor, who, since he did not shrink from it, went with the fleet to the shore of Cosmidium, and Symeon came to the same place with his own forces; and they spoke with one another. But although Romanus had given magnificent gifts to Symeon, they departed with nothing accomplished, which a pair of eagles seemed to signify, which they say flew above them and, clashing with a clangor among themselves, immediately separated, one flying towards the city, the other heading into Thrace. Romanus, insatiable in the power of the empire, and as if having forgotten the oath by which he had bound himself, not content with the diadem of himself and his eldest son, also crowned the other two sons, and his grandson by Christopher, with the Roman empire, which until then had been accustomed to be in the hands of one, being distributed into many heads. He made his youngest son, Theophylact, whom he was canvassing for the pontificate of the imperial city, a cleric by tonsuring his hair, and from the patriarch he even requested the Syncellus.
Variæ lectiones et notæ.
(29) Of Augusta Theodora. This was the first of the other empresses; for the second wife of her father Romanus, Theodora, had died; or Sophia, the wife of that Christopher, after he died, was brought down from the empire.
After the death of Nicholas, who, having been restored, held the see of Constantinople for thirteen years, Stephen, metropolitan of Amasea, was designated patriarch. But the prince of the Bulgarians, Symeon, a man of blood and impatient of rest, brought war upon the race of the Croats, but being defeated by them, he lost his army in the mountain passes. Meanwhile, someone announced to the emperor that a statue placed at the top of the arch of the Xerolophos, facing the west, had been turned toward the fate of Symeon the Bulgarian, and that if the head of this statue were cut off, the death of Symeon would follow. This having been done a little later, at the same hour Symeon departed from life due to pain in his stomach, as was signified to the emperor afterward when he examined the time of his death with diligence. Symeon having been removed from human affairs, the principate of the Bulgarians came to Peter, his son by a second wife, who, since his nation was pressed by famine and feared the arms of bordering nations, and especially of the Romans, sent envoys to the emperor about peace and, if it were agreeable, about entering into an affinity. Since both were to his liking, Peter himself came to Byzantium and, peace having been made, took as wife the granddaughter of the emperor, the daughter of his eldest son, Christopher.
19. Romanus, moreover, not content to have placed Constantine below him, sought an occasion to prefer even Christopher to him. Therefore, having instructed the Bulgarian throng to demand through a sedition that Christopher be named before Constantine, he acceded to their requests, as if, on account of that disturbance, it were necessary to yield to necessity. Then, fearing neither God, by whose divine power the pacts had been invoked and strengthened, nor deterred by the shame of his subjects, he ordered his two remaining sons also to be acclaimed before Constantine; and the first became the fifth, and a younger Romanus would also have been placed over him, had he not died beforehand. Thus the native emperor, to whom the empire belonged by hereditary right, was like an enrolled dependent. But vengeance, having not forgotten its duty, even if it proceeded with a slow step, nonetheless came at last; but this shall be narrated in another place.
Variæ lectiones et notæ.
(30) Concerning Peter. Regarding the statement, "For the time between is not long, and Symeon, that wanderer and cause of many Christian deaths, departs from human affairs; and Peter, his son, succeeds to his rule, not appearing as an heir of his glory and wealth, nor of his cruelty and misanthropy, but rather being very far from them."
Stephen of Amasea, having occupied the see of the capital city for three years, died; and a certain monk, Tryphon, was ordained on the condition that after a specified time he would willingly yield the throne to Theophylact, the son of the Emperor, for he was still a boy. During this time, there was a most severe winter, a greatest famine, and a most horrific conflagration. At that time, too, the Emperor Christopher passed away. When the promised time had already elapsed, the patriarch Tryphon did not wish to yield the pontifical throne as had been agreed, but endeavored to retain it with both hands and feet. B The Emperor, however, taking it ill that the promise given to him had been broken, the Bishop of Caesarea, in order to curry favor with the Emperor, tricked the patriarch’s simplicity with craft; assuming the guise of a friend, he said to him: "The Emperor is indeed vehemently attacking you, but he lacks charges by which you could be removed from your position, and already those who stand against you have been brought to the point that they say you are completely illiterate. But make haste so that you may also snatch this calumny from your accusers, which you will easily repel if, taking a sheet of paper before many, you write your name and the dignity of the pontificate; this writing having been brought to the Emperor, there will no longer be anything for which they may accuse you." He obeyed this counsel, and in the presence of many wrote these words on a clean sheet of paper: "Tryphon, by the mercy of God, Archbishop of New Rome and Ecumenical Patriarch." The Bishop of Caesarea took this paper and went off to present it to the Emperor; thus a document of resignation was woven into it above the patriarch’s signature, and as one who had resigned, he was cast out of the Church, and the Emperor’s son was designated patriarch. After this, a Russian fleet is said to have attacked the city, not with one thousand, but with fifteen thousand ships. Against them the Roman fleet stood in battle array near the Pharos, and, suddenly attacking, routed the Barbarians, burning many of their ships with Greek fire. The rest set out towards the east and fell in with the patrician Bardas Phocas, who was in command of the army; he destroyed many who had gone ashore to acquire provisions. The Domestic of the Schools, Curcuas, did the same, and many were both killed and captured. Thus, being ill-treated by land and by sea, they kept themselves on their ships until, destitute of supplies, they decided to return to their own lands. But when they had set sail, the Roman triremes again attacked them and defeated them in two naval battles. D A few of the Barbarian ships, having escaped destruction, returned to their own people, messengers of evils and of the defeat they had suffered. The Russians, therefore, having received more evils than they had hoped for, refrained from provoking the Romans.
XX. But the Agarenes were also often defeated and humbled by the Domestic of the Schools, John Kourkouas—the grandfather of that John who later obtained the empire—and his brother Theophilus, and they were deprived of not a few of the cities which they held. The Emperor Romanus, perhaps having come to a realization of his perjury and of the violation of the agreement he had made with Emperor Constantine, hastened to appease God through acts of bounty. He made other distributions and also paid off the debts owed by many to creditors, releasing the debtors from the weight of their obligation; he took the documents and consumed them with fire in the middle of the forum, the sum of these debts having reached nineteen centenaria. He also provided the rent for those living in rented houses in the Great City. These things indeed benefited the debtors, but to Romanus, I think, they brought little or no advantage: for the things he was giving away belonged to others—since they were public funds—and because he was enjoying the empire which he held through seizure, and while he was defrauding him to whom it belonged, and was possessing the whole of it with imperial prosperity, he was distributing a very small portion as bounty. Doing such a thing is as if someone were to steal a neighbor’s ox and sacrifice it, and then distribute the mere trotters to the poor while he himself feasted on the whole body; or as if one were to steal his neighbor’s wife and abuse her, disregarding her lawful husband, and then distribute alms to the poor from that man’s own money, so that, perhaps, for the sake of his charity, the charge of adultery might be forgiven him. But these things are according to human judgment. If, however, one were to look toward divine goodness, perhaps one might even say these things were not without effect. While he held the scepter, the icon of the Savior Christ, not made by hands, the divine [δ] (napkin or) *cheiromaktron*, was brought to the preeminent of cities. For Edessa had held it for a long time; but when it was in danger of destruction by the Romans who were besieging it, held as it was by the Agarenes, in order that the Edessenes might escape pillage, they provided the divine icon as a ransom for the city. At that time, there arrived from Armenia the two adolescents who were joined together; when one died and physicians severed the connection, the other, too, helped nothing by this, did not long survive his brother, and died. How Romanus claimed the empire for himself without any just title, and adorned his own children with imperial honor and name, and, having occupied the leading position with his own, placed the natural successor to the empire, Constantine, in almost no account, below all the others, has already been set forth by me. Now the rest must be followed, so as to show that, even if D
providence perhaps reaches the unjust somewhat slowly, granting space for repentance, yet if they do not depart from their malice, she follows them with a slow foot and exacts penalties. Romanus indeed was then reigning for the twenty-sixth year. But Constantine, in order to recover the empire for himself, kept turning it over in his mind, and that he might accomplish what he desired, he incited the sons against their father through others, or rather the one, namely Stephen. For Constantine, happening to have a more stable mind, did not accept the solicitation. The other, however, having called upon associates, set upon his father, and seized him and confined him on the island of Prote, and ordered him to be shorn; and he himself, along with his brother-in-law and his brother, performed the imperial duties. But it was not possible for the three to be of one mind. For they disagreed in certain matters and spoke against one another; and from this they grew suspicious of one another, and Stephen made it his concern to pull down his brother-in-law Constantine from the empire, and Constantine likewise [sought to do the same to] Stephen. But Constantine was much quicker than he in this work, and his wife Helena was urging him on. Having therefore taken some of the military commanders, he seized both brothers who were dining with him, and immediately sent one away to the island [called] Panormus, or also Antigonia, and the other to [the place] called Terebinthus, and ordered both to be shorn as clerics. And then, when they were about to be transferred, they asked to come into the sight of their father, whom when he saw, he said: "I begat sons and raised them up, but they themselves have set me at naught." From there, therefore, Stephen was confined in Proconnesus, and from there he was transferred to Rhodes, and from that place to Mitylene, while to Constantine Samothrace was assigned as a bounded limit from which it was not lawful to depart. Having attempted to flee many times, and for this reason having removed the chief of his guards by poison, he was put to death by the others. But Stephen died in Lesbos, having spent nineteen years there. Their father passed his life on the island of Prote. Thus, each of them paid the penalty for their crimes.
Various readings and notes
(37) How Romanus usurped the empire which in no way belonged to him, and how he deemed his own children worthy of imperial honor and title, and how he deemed both himself and his children worthy of the highest offices, while he relegated the true-born successor to the empire, Constantine, to the very last of all, has already been narrated by me.
(38) By others he stirs up the sons against the father. But Liutprandus, in book v, cap. 9, writes that the son's conspiracy against the father was done without the knowledge of Constantine. "For with these four reigning, Stephen and Constantine the brothers, without the knowledge of Constantine the son of Leo the emperor, were plotting certain guileful things against their father Romanus. For it irked them that, with their father’s severity in the way, they were not free to do whatever they wished." Thus they complained of their father's severity, which soon he repeats again: "Not bearing their father’s just severity."
(39) And his wife. But especially of a certain Diabolini, by whom he was warned of the conspiracy instigated against him.
(40) And some of the military commanders. Liutprandus, in lib. v, cap. 10, writes that these were Macedonians.
(41) They asked to come into the sight of their father. How they were received by their father with very witty language, Liutprandus describes beautifully, cap. 11.
(42) That his body was brought into the city, and buried in the monastery of Myrelaion built by him himself, others write, as we have noted in *Constantinopolis Christiana*, where we showed from Codinus that two monasteries of the same name existed in the city, of which the other
